<meta>The text on this page is a legal notice or "cautio" (warning) regarding printing privileges. This was a standard legal protection in the 17th century, similar to a modern copyright notice, ensuring that only the authorized printer could sell the book within the King's territories.</meta>

# WARNING

## GIVEN TO THE PRINTER.

I am exercising the Privilege which has been granted to me by the **CATHOLIC** King <note>Referring to the King of Spain, who at this time ruled the Southern Netherlands where the author lived</note>, and by the Special Permission of the Privy Council <note>original: *Consilii Sanctioris*, the high council of the Habsburg government in Brussels</note> which has been added; and to you, **JEAN MOMMAERT** <note>original: *Ioannes Mommarti*; Mommaert was a prominent printer and bookseller in Brussels</note>, I grant in good faith and with affection the power of printing and publishing with your types this my **DISCOURSE ON ANAGRAMS**. <note>original: *De Anagrammatismo Diatribam*. In this context, "diatribe" refers to a scholarly discourse or exercise rather than a verbal attack</note> Should anyone cause you trouble in any way regarding this Edition, he shall sin against me, against you, and against the King himself; he shall be fined moreover, whether he attempts a new edition or hinders yours by importing copies from elsewhere.

->**ERICIUS PUTEANUS**,<-
<note>The Latinized name of Henri van de Putte (1574–1646), a celebrated humanist scholar and the successor to Justus Lipsius at the University of Louvain</note>

<summary>This page contains a formal legal warning (cautio) protecting the copyright of the book, granting the printer Jean Mommaert exclusive rights to publish Puteanus's treatise on anagrams under royal authority.</summary>

# APHORISMS,

->O R  A X I O M S<-

## OF HIPPOCRATES,

->*contained in seven books,*<-
<note>Hippocrates of Kos was an ancient Greek physician often called the "Father of Medicine." His *Aphorisms* are a collection of brief medical principles and observations that remained central to medical education for over two thousand years.</note>

<meta>This page serves as the formal preface or "To the Reader" (Lectori Salutem) following the title page. The signature mark A 2 indicates this is the second leaf of the first gathering of the book.</meta>

# JEAN BAPTISTE DE CONDE
## Sends Greetings to the Reader <note>The Latin "S. D." stands for *Salutem Dicit*, a standard formal greeting meaning "sends health" or "sends greetings."</note>

Behold, kind Reader, those golden Aphorisms <note>The "Aphorisms" of Hippocrates are a collection of several hundred short medical principles and observations.</note> of the divine Old Man Hippocrates <note>original: "divini Senis Hippocratis"</note>, illustrated in heroic verse <term>Heroic verse: The dactylic hexameter meter used in ancient epic poetry like the Iliad; the author uses it here as a mnemonic device to help medical students memorize the text.</term>: a work truly to be uniquely commended for the sake of memory (for what Physician would dare to be ignorant of them without being marked for stupidity?); inasmuch as I have endeavored to describe them in verse not only shorter and more succinct, but also more lucid. For there were some among them quite difficult to understand, which I have restored to a clearer meaning, partly from the commentaries of Galen <note>Galen of Pergamon was an influential 2nd-century Greek physician whose interpretations of Hippocrates shaped Western medicine for 1,500 years.</note> and others, and partly through my own diligence, as much as was in my power. Judge for yourself what I have achieved; perhaps your favor will, in time, call forth more, and more serious, works from me. Farewell.

Given at Brussels. The Kalends of July <note>July 1st</note>, 1647.

<summary>In this preface, Jean Baptiste de Conde introduces his poetic adaptation of Hippocrates' Aphorisms, explaining that he has translated them into verse to make them easier for medical students to memorize and understand.</summary>
<keywords>Jean Baptiste de Conde, Hippocrates, Aphorisms, Galen, medical education, heroic verse, mnemonic, Brussels, 17th-century medicine</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues the prefatory material. Following the prose address to the reader on the previous page, the author now offers a short poem to the reader, followed by a celebratory poem likely written by the printer or a colleague praising De Conde's work.</meta>

## THE SAME TO THE SAME.

> While my mind was clouded by various cares,
>   And there was no love but for passing the time,
> I often used to refresh myself in the gardens cultivated by poets,
>   And on the joyful peaks of Parnaussus <term>Parnassus: A mountain in Greece sacred to the Muses and Apollo, the god of poetry and medicine; it represents the heights of poetic inspiration.</term>;
> I often overcame the tedium of an unpleasant day
>   By arranging verses, for the work was pleasing:
> But lest I should perhaps seem to you to have been idle then,
>   Or to have studied only for my own good,
> I have written poems that can provide benefit to Physicians,
>   And to be read by others with some profit.
> Favor these, studious Reader; this is the only reward,
>   And the only thanks to be given for my studies.

---

### TO THE MOST EXPERT MASTER
## JEAN BAPTISTE DE CONDE,
### *extracting light from the darkness of the Aphorisms.*

> Hippocrates is astonished, and the dispute is still before Apollo <note>Apollo was the patron god of both medicine and poetry; the poet suggests even the god of healing is confused by the high mortality rate.</note>,
>   *Why such a great crowd falls by untimely death.*
> The abyss of Phlegethon <term>Phlegethon: A river of fire in the underworld; here used to represent the depths of the land of the dead.</term> can scarcely hold the bloodless souls,
>   The boat of Charon <note>Charon: The mythological ferryman who carried the souls of the newly deceased across the river Styx.</note> scarcely has room for the wretched.
> Medicines are of no use, herbs lie dormant without strength,
>   And the health hoped for the sick does not result from them.
> Cease to wonder: for if the Doctor knows not the foundations of healing,
>   What help could he possibly give?
> Hippocrates published the **APHORISMS**, but the beginner,
>   Because of the long sequence, is unable to learn them.
> Come now! Put away fear; **DE CONDE** will open the thresholds,
>   By which you may hold them in your mind more easily.
> If an overly long abundance of Prose wearies you,
>   Behold, the Muse will sing these in succinct Verse.
> Thus Apollo owes double Praise to you, **DE CONDE**,
>   Who performs the duty of both Doctor and Poet so well.

->Jean Mommart, Printer<-
->After darkness, I hope for light <note>original: "Post tenebras spero lucem." This was a common Latin motto during the Renaissance and Reformation, often used by printers.</note><-

<meta>This page continues the sequence of commendatory poems praising Jean-Baptiste de Conde. # TO JEAN-BAPTISTE DE CONDE, Physician, and likewise most learned Poet
### *By A. K., Doctor of Medicine*

> Phoebus <term>Phoebus: Another name for Apollo, the Greco-Roman god who presided over both medicine and poetry.</term> is the founder of both the poetic and the healing arts,
> And both Poets and Physicians claim him as their patron.
> Therefore, you are rightly called a dual student of Phoebus,
> And a double laurel <term>laurels: Wreaths of laurel leaves were awarded in antiquity to both great poets and victors to symbolize excellence.</term> must be granted to your head.
> Anyone who is well-versed in either kind of art
> Is rare, but you are far rarer: you are wise in both.
> If you were as double in body as you are in talent,
> And could present yourself in many places at once,
> You should be given the first seat on both peaks of Parnassus <note>Parnassus was a mountain sacred to the Muses; its two peaks were often said to represent different branches of the arts or different sources of inspiration.</note>,
> For you have brought forth a double honor.

---

# TO JEAN-BAPTISTE DE CONDE,
## *Illustrating the Aphorisms of Hippocrates in verse.*

> Whatever was obscure that you touch with eloquent song,
> You bless with light, as your genius makes it clear:
> That the Medical Art—otherwise so complex—becomes easy and pleasant,
> It owes all of that to your verses.

->*From brother to brother, J. de Conde, Licentiate of Both Laws <note>The original abbreviation "I. V. L." stands for Juris Utriusque Licentiatus, a person licensed in both Civil and Church (Canon) law.</note>.*<-

# THE APHORISMS
## OF HIPPOCRATES,
### From the Interpretation of ANUTIUS FOESIUS and GULIELMUS PLANTIUS,
## BOOK ONE.

->I.<-

<image-desc>An ornamental drop cap 'V' featuring a stylized floral design within a square frame.</image-desc> *Life is short, art is long, opportunity is fleeting, experience is deceptive, judgment is difficult.* <note>original: "Vita brevis, ars longa, occasio praeceps, experientia fallax, judicium difficile." This is perhaps the most famous medical maxim in history, emphasizing that a single lifetime is hardly enough to master the complexities of medicine.</note> *Furthermore, it is not enough for the Physician to have done his own duty, unless the patient, the attendants, and the external circumstances are also properly arranged.*

->II.<-

*In disturbances of the bowels and vomitings that occur spontaneously, if the substances purged are such as they ought to be, it is beneficial and easily tolerated; but if not, the opposite occurs. Likewise, if the evacuation of the vessels <term>evacuation of the vessels: Referring to bloodletting or the natural release of bodily fluids.</term> is done as it should be, it is beneficial and easily tolerated; if not, the opposite happens. Therefore, consideration must be given to the place, the season of the year, the age, and the diseases in which these things ought to occur, or not.*

->III.<-

*The well-maintained health of athletes, when it has progressed to the highest point and reached the extreme limit of fullness, is precarious <note>The Latin 'lubrica' (slippery/precarious) implies that extreme physical fitness is a state of unstable equilibrium.</note>, since it cannot remain in the same state nor stay still. Since it does not stay still and can no longer progress toward something better, the only thing remaining is for it to slide into something worse. For these reasons, it is advantageous to dissolve that over-full habit of the body without delay, so that the body may take up the beginning of a new nourishment. However, one must not go so far that the vessels are extremely depleted (for that is dangerous), but rather as...* <note>The text here discusses the theory of 'plethora,' where an overabundance of blood or 'humors' in a peak-condition athlete was thought to lead to sudden illness if not moderated.</note>

<vocab>Hippocrates, Aphorisms, Anutius Foesius <note>A renowned 16th-century French physician and translator of Hippocrates.</note>, Gulielmus Plantius <note>Also known as Guillaume Plantin, a scholar and commentator on Greek medical texts.</note>, Life is short, art is long, health, of athletes</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the first book of Hippocratic Aphorisms, opening with the iconic statement on the difficulty of the medical art and the instability of extreme physical health. It establishes the clinical necessity of observing natural bodily evacuations and environmental factors in treatment.</summary>
<keywords>Hippocrates, medical ethics, Aphorisms, athletic health, plethora, purging, medical philosophy, Anutius Foesius, Gulielmus Plantius</keywords>

# THE APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES,

## From the Library of JEAN-BAPTISTE DE CONDE, Physician of Brussels,

### BOOK ONE.

-> I. <-

<image-desc>Decorative woodcut drop cap 'A' with intricate floral and scrollwork designs.</image-desc>

Art is long, and life is short, opportunity is fleeting, judgment is also difficult, and experience is deceptive. It is not enough for the Physician to perform his duty well, unless the patient also performs what is commanded in like manner, as do the attendants, and unless external factors are properly prepared. <note>original: "Ars longa est, & vita brevis..." This famous opening emphasizes that the mastery of medicine exceeds a single human lifespan and requires the coordination of everyone involved in the healing process.</note>

-> I I. <-

In flows of the belly <term>flows of the belly: A reference to diarrhea or gastric disturbances.</term> and vomitings that arise of their own accord, if the matter purged is of the kind that should be expelled, it is beneficial and the sick bear it without difficulty; if not, the opposite happens to them. Likewise, the depletion of blood from the veins <term>depletion of blood: Specifically referring to bloodletting (phlebotomy), the practice of withdrawing blood to cure illness.</term>, if it is appropriate for the disease, is beneficial and the patients do not tolerate it poorly; if not, the opposite occurs to them. One must therefore look to the diseases, the region, the season, and the age of the patient to determine whether these evacuations ought to happen or not.

-> I I I. <-

When the physical condition of athletes has reached the highest peak of fullness <term>fullness: Known as "plethora," this refers to an overabundance of blood or vital humors within the body.</term>, it is usually precarious. <note>original: "lubricus", meaning slippery or unstable. Hippocrates suggests that extreme physical fitness is a state of unstable equilibrium.</note> For since it can by no means remain in that one state for long, nor can it improve further, it must necessarily decline for the worse. Therefore, this overly-full condition must be depleted without delay, so that the body may begin to take in new nourishment. However, not to the point of extreme...

<summary>This page introduces the first three Aphorisms of Hippocrates, establishing the difficulty of the medical art, the importance of proper bodily evacuations, and the dangers of extreme physical states in athletes.</summary>
<keywords>Hippocrates, medical ethics, bloodletting, plethora, athlete's health, diagnostic judgment, Brussels physicians</keywords>

--- Page 9 ---
<meta>This page continues the first book of the Aphorisms, specifically completing Aphorism III regarding the dangers of extreme physical states and moving into the management of a patient's diet—referred to as "regimen"—during both acute and chronic illnesses. It focuses on the delicate balance between the patient's strength and the severity of the disease.</meta>

# THE APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES,

[...one must proceed] as far as the nature of the one who must endure it allows. For the same reason, evacuations <term>evacuations: The removal of bodily fluids, such as through purging, vomiting, or bloodletting.</term> carried to an extreme are dangerous; and conversely, restorative feedings <term>restorative feedings: The practice of giving a patient concentrated nutrition to recover strength.</term> carried to the highest peak are also dangerous.

### IV.

A slender and precise diet is always dangerous in long-lasting diseases, and in acute diseases, where it is not appropriate, it is not very safe. Again, a diet that has reached extreme slenderness is troublesome; for indeed, those repletions <term>repletions: A state of being "full" or having an excess of humors/nutrients.</term> which have been led to the highest peak of fullness are also troublesome. <note>Hippocrates argues that both extreme hunger (fasting) and extreme fullness (overeating) put the body under dangerous stress, especially during illness.</note>

### V.

In a slender diet, sick people may fail, and because of this, they are more severely harmed. For whatever error is committed, it is usually more serious in a slender diet than in a slightly fuller one. For this reason, even for those in health, a slender, strictly prescribed, and precise diet is not very safe, because they feel the effects of errors more severely. For that cause, a slender and precise diet is for the most part more dangerous than one that is slightly fuller.

### VI.

For extreme diseases, extreme remedies, exquisitely prepared, are the best. <note>original: "Ad extremos morbos, extrema exquisitè comparata remedia, optima." This is the origin of the common proverb "desperate times call for desperate measures."</note>

### VII.

Therefore, when a disease is very acute, it immediately brings extreme hardships, and an extremely slender diet must necessarily be used. But when it is not as severe, and it is permitted to provide a fuller diet, one should withdraw from the slender diet as much as the disease has receded from its extreme peak.

### VIII.

When the force of the disease is at its greatest, then it is necessary to use the thinnest diet possible.

### IX.

But a judgment must also be made regarding the sick person: whether they have enough strength to last with that diet until the height of the disease, or whether the patient might fail first and not be able to endure such a diet, or whether the disease might fail and be blunted first. <note>The physician must predict whether the patient's internal energy will outlast the illness or if the restrictive diet will starve the patient before the disease breaks.</note>

### X.

Therefore, for those in whom the height of the disease is present immediately, a slender diet must be provided right away. But for those in whom the height will occur later...

<meta>catchword: Sub</meta>

<summary>This page outlines the Hippocratic principles of medical dieting (regimen), warning that extreme fasting is often more dangerous than moderate eating and establishing the rule that the most severe diseases require the most "extreme" treatments.</summary>
<keywords>regimen, slender diet, acute disease, chronic illness, medical crisis, extreme remedies, patient strength, Hippocratic medicine</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the commentary on the Aphorisms of Hippocrates, specifically concluding the third aphorism regarding the risks of extreme physical states. It then transitions into a series of guidelines (Aphorisms IV through X) regarding "regimen"—the management of a patient's diet and strength during the different stages of acute and chronic illnesses. It emphasizes that extreme restriction of food can be just as dangerous as overfeeding.</meta>

# BOOK I.
<sig>A 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: Then</meta>

While the veins collapse, it will be permitted to empty them; but only so much as the nature of the athlete <term>athlete: Here referring to the patient's physical constitution and their ability to endure treatment.</term> is capable of bearing. In the same way that a dreaded plethora <term>plethora: An overabundance of bodily fluids, particularly blood, which was believed to cause disease.</term> is feared if it reaches the highest level, an extreme evacuation <term>vacuatio: The medical removal of fluids, usually through bloodletting, purging, or vomiting.</term> is also to be feared.

->IV.<-

Since a slender diet <term>slender diet: A highly restricted or "thin" intake of food, often involving only liquids or very light broths.</term> is never without danger in chronic <term>chronic: Long-lasting or slow-progressing diseases.</term> diseases, it is also not safe in acute <term>acute: Diseases that come on suddenly and reach a crisis point quickly.</term> diseases where it is not required. Again, a diet that is too slender is troublesome for the sick man, just as a repletion <term>repletion: The state of being overfilled with food or humors.</term> carried to the highest peak is troublesome.

->V.<-

In a slender diet, sick people fail more often, and by this they harm themselves more; for any error that is committed is usually more damaging in a slender diet than in a full one. For this reason, a slender diet and a strictly prescribed table often tend to harm even those in health, because they feel any errors more severely; and therefore, a diet that is too slender brings more danger than one that is a little fuller.

->VI.<-

For extreme diseases, extreme remedies are best. <note>original: "Extremis extrema juvant medicamina morbis." This is one of the most famous medical maxims, suggesting that desperate times require desperate measures.</note>

->VII.<-

Immediately, if the disease is very acute, the extremity is felt wherever there is pain; therefore, the slenderest diet will be beneficial to these patients. But if the disease is not very acute, it will be lawful to withdraw from such a slender diet in proportion to how far the disease is judged to be from its extreme.

->VIII.<-

When the disease is at its vigor <term>vigor: The "acme" or peak intensity of a disease's progress.</term>, let the diet be at its slenderest.

->IX.<-

But whether a patient can sufficiently maintain their strength with such a diet or not must be judged from the condition of the weak person.

->X.<-

Therefore, for those in whom the vigor will soon be reached, let a slender diet be maintained immediately; but if it should arrive later,

<vocab>athlete (constitution), plethora (excess), evacuation (emptying), chronic (long-term), diet (nourishment), remedies (medicines), very acute (severe), vigor (crisis point)</vocab>

<summary>This page outlines the Hippocratic principles of dieting during illness, warning that extreme fasting is often more dangerous than slight overfeeding. It introduces the famous rule that extreme diseases require extreme treatments and explains how to time a patient's nutrition based on the "vigor" or peak of the illness.</summary>
<keywords>regimen, plethora, bloodletting, acute disease, chronic disease, fasting, medical crisis, Hippocratic aphorisms</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific dietary instructions for the sick, transitioning from general warnings about restricted diets to the timing of nutrition relative to the disease's peak. It establishes the foundational Hippocratic theories of "innate heat" and how age and season influence a person's ability to process food and endure illness.</meta>

### THE APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES
<meta>catchword: children</meta>

*Just at the time of the peak <note>original: "vigor," referring to the "acme" or most intense point of a disease's progression.</note> of the illness, and a little before it, food should be withdrawn. But before that time, a more abundant diet should be provided, so that the patient may have enough strength.*

->XI.<-

*In exacerbations <term>exacerbation: A sudden increase in the severity of a disease or its symptoms.</term> one must avoid giving food, for to provide it then is harmful. And in whatever diseases worsen in cycles, one must be wary of providing nourishment during the paroxysms <term>paroxysm: A periodic attack or the fit-like return of a disease's symptoms.</term> themselves.*

->XII.<-

*The diseases themselves, the seasons of the year, and the observation of the proportion of the periods—whether they occur daily, on alternate days, or at longer intervals—will indicate the coming paroxysms and the state of the illnesses. Furthermore, signs are taken from those things that appear later; for example, in pleurisy <term>pleurisy: A painful inflammation of the linings around the lungs.</term>, if sputum appears right at the beginning, it announces that the disease will be short; but if it is seen later, it will be long. Moreover, the urine, the feces of the bowels, and sweats, when they appear, demonstrate whether the diseases will be easy or difficult to judge <term>judicatu: Referring to the "crisis" or the point where the physician can determine if the patient will live or die.</term>, and whether they will be short or long.*

->XIII.<-

*Old men endure fasting most easily; next, those who are in the prime of life; adolescents least of all; and especially children, and among those, especially the ones endowed with a sharper and more lively spirit.*

->XIV.<-

*Those who are growing possess the most innate heat <term>innate heat: In ancient medicine, this was the vital warmth or "internal fire" believed to be the source of life and digestion.</term>; therefore they require the most nourishment, otherwise the body is consumed. In old men, however, since there is little heat within, they certainly require little fuel, for they are extinguished by too much; for this same reason, acute fevers do not happen to the elderly in the same way, because their bodies are cold.*

->XV.<-

*During winter and spring, the bellies are naturally warmest and sleep is longest. Therefore, during those times, more abundant food should be provided, since the native heat is at its peak, and thus they require more nourishment. This is demonstrated by the different stages of life and the condition of athletes.*

->XVI.<-

*A liquid diet <note>original: "victus humidus," literally a moist or wet diet, such as broths or ptisans.</note> is beneficial for all those with fevers, especially...*

<summary>This page outlines the critical timing of nutrition during illness, advising fasting during a disease's peak and explaining how age and season affect metabolic "innate heat." It also notes how diagnostic signs like sputum and urine help predict the duration and severity of a condition.</summary>
<keywords>dietary management, paroxysm, innate heat, diagnostics, pleurisy, fasting, aging, seasons, metabolism</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the specific dietary instructions for the sick, transitioning from general warnings about restricted diets to the timing of nutrition relative to the disease's peak. It establishes the foundational Hippocratic theories of "innate heat" and how age and season influence a person's ability to process food and endure illness.</meta>

# BOOK I.
A 6
<meta>catchword: Then</meta>

Then at last, when the peak <note>original: "vigor," referring to the "acme" or most intense point of a disease's progression.</note> is imminent, withdraw food; but before that time, nourish him fully, so that the patient may have sufficient strength.

### X I.

It is necessary to withdraw from the table when the disease is sharpened <term>exacerbation: a sudden increase in the severity of symptoms</term>; for to provide nourishment then is harmful. In whatever diseases worsen through cycles, the patient must always be wary of food during the onset <term>paroxysm: the periodic return or intensification of a disease</term>.

### X I I.

The diseases and their cycles, combined with the seasons of the year—whether these occur daily or on other days—will teach us the nature of the illness and its onsets. No less of a sign is taken from those things that reveal themselves afterward: thus, the sputum that appears in pleurisy <term>pleuritis: a painful inflammation of the linings around the lungs</term> declares that a short illness is coming if it proceeds quickly, but a slow recovery if it is slow. Likewise, the urine, the sweats, and the bowel movements reveal whether the judgment <term>crisis: the turning point of a disease where the patient either recovers or declines</term> will be easy or difficult, and whether the disease will be short or long.

### X I I I.

Above all others, the elderly <note>original: "aetas senilis"</note> can endure fasting without hardship. Next is the stable middle age; after this follows youth. Of all these, children can least endure long fasting, especially those who possess a more active or spirited disposition.

### X I V.

He who is growing has more heat <term>innate heat: the vital energy believed in ancient medicine to drive digestion and life</term>; therefore, he requires more nourishment, otherwise his body is consumed. But since in old men there is only a small amount of heat, it is enough to nourish them moderately, for abundance harms them. Because their bodies languish from cold, acute fevers rarely arise in the elderly.

### X V.

In winter and spring, the belly <note>original: "venter," referring to the stomach and digestive capacity</note> tends to have more heat, and sleep is less brief; therefore, nature demands more food then, since the internal heat boils abundantly. Both the different ages of man and the example of athletes testify to this fact.

### X I V.

When the diet for all those with fever is liquid <note>original: "humida," referring to a diet of broths or thin gruels</note>...

<summary>This page outlines the Hippocratic principles of timing nutrition according to a disease's peak and a patient's age, introducing the concept of "innate heat" as a driver of metabolism. It notes that the elderly and those in winter have less internal heat and thus different dietary requirements than children or those in spring.</summary>

<meta>This page concludes the first book of the Aphorisms. It follows a discussion on the endurance of fasting across different ages, specifically concluding that while the elderly handle hunger well, children do not. This page moves into the timing of meals, the influence of seasons, and the critical rules for purging and managing the body's "humors" during the turning points of an illness.</meta>

12 APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES
<meta>catchword: BOOK</meta>

...especially for children, and others who are accustomed to such a diet, it is beneficial.

->X V I I.<-

We must also observe who should be fed once or twice a day, and for whom a larger, smaller, or partial serving of food is appropriate. Something must also be conceded to the season, the region, the age of the patient, and their personal habits.

->X V I I I.<-

During summer and autumn, patients bear food with the greatest difficulty; during winter, they bear it most easily. Spring, following winter, holds the second place in ease of digestion.

->X I X.<-

For those who suffer periodic attacks <term>accessions: the periodic return or intensification of symptoms, such as a fever spike</term> that occur in fixed cycles, you must give nothing and force nothing, but rather withhold food before the crisis <note>original: "judicationes," the "judgment" or turning point of the disease.</note>.

->X X.<-

When the humors are undergoing a crisis, or have already reached a complete turning point, do not disturb the body or introduce changes through medications or other irritants; instead, let it be.

->X X I.<-

Those things which need to be drawn out <term>evacuation: the removal of waste or harmful humors from the body</term> should be guided in the direction they naturally tend to flow, using the pathways and locations that are beneficial for their passage.

->X I I.<-

One should treat and move those things that are mature and "cooked" <term>concoction: the process where the body's internal heat "ripens" or prepares harmful fluids for expulsion</term>, but not those that are raw, nor at the very beginning of the illness, unless they are moving by their own internal force—which rarely happens.

->X X I I I.<-

The materials expelled from the body are not to be judged merely by their volume; rather, one should look for whether they are of the expected quality and if the patient can endure the loss. Even when it is necessary to purge until the patient faints <note>original: "animi defectionem," referring to syncope or loss of consciousness; a common practice in ancient medicine to reset the body's balance.</note>, it should only be done if the patient's strength is sufficient.

->X X I V.<-

In acute diseases, a purging medicine should be used rarely and only at the very beginning. Even then, it must not be done without great care.

<meta>This page concludes Book I of the Aphorisms. It transitions from discussing the nutritional needs of children and the elderly to the broader environmental and physiological factors governing treatment, such as the seasons and the state of the "humors."</meta>

### BOOK I.

Then it is usually very useful for those who are accustomed to it and for children. <note>This line concludes the thought from the previous page regarding the frequency of meals.</note>

->X V I I.<-

One must also observe those whom it is appropriate to feed twice or once, in parts or all at once; and for whom it will be beneficial to allow a large meal, or rather to prescribe a meager table. Consideration must also be given to their past habits of life, the season, their age, and the region in which they live.

->X V I I I.<-

In the autumn and also in the summer, an abundance of food is digested with great difficulty; digestion is better in the winter; and the springtime holds the second place after the winter season.

->X I I X.<- <note>original: "X I X."</note>

For those whose bouts of illness <term>accessions: the periodic return or intensification of symptoms, such as a fever spike</term> usually come and go in certain cycles, it is forbidden to give anything or to force anything upon them; rather, subtract from their accustomed diet before the crisis <note>original: "crisim," the turning point or "judgment" of a disease when the patient either begins to recover or declines.</note>.

->X X.<-

If matters are undergoing a crisis, or have already undergone one, take care not to disturb or move them with medications or other irritants.

->X X I.<-

Those harmful things that are tending in a certain direction—provided it is a suitable path—you should purge in that direction.

->X X I I.<-

Medicate that which is "cooked," not that which is "raw" <term>concoction: the process in ancient medicine where the body’s internal heat "ripens" or prepares harmful fluids for expulsion</term>, unless the matter is swelling or in a state of agitation.

->X X I I I.<-

Though an ample quantity of matter may proceed from the body, it is of little concern; instead, one must ensure that things are expelled as they ought to be, and that the patient bears it readily. And when it is necessary to empty the body even to the point of fainting, that too must be performed, provided the strength of the patient is sufficient.

->X X I V.<- <note>original: "X V I V." - a likely printer's error for 24.</note>

You shall more rarely provide a purgative in acute diseases, and only in the beginning; do not be rash in this matter.

THE APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES,

# BOOK TWO.

### I.

In whatever disease sleep causes distress, it is a deadly sign; but if sleep brings relief, it is not deadly.

### I I.

When sleep calms a delirium <note>Delirium in ancient medicine often referred to the mental agitation or "wandering" of the mind caused by high fevers.</note>, it is a good sign.

### I I I.

Sleep and wakefulness, if either exceeds the proper limit, are a bad sign.

### I V.

Neither satiety, nor hunger, nor anything else which exceeds the limits of nature, is good.

### V.

Weariness that arises spontaneously <note>original: "sponte obortæ," meaning fatigue that appears without a clear external cause like heavy labor.</note> foretells coming disease.

### V I.

Those who feel pain in some part of the body, but are almost unaware of the pain, are suffering from a sickness of the mind <note>This implies that a loss of physical sensation or an indifference to pain suggests the patient's consciousness or "reason" is impaired.</note>.

### V I I.

Bodies that have been wasted away over a long period of time should be restored gradually; however, those wasted in a short time should be restored quickly.

### V I I I.

If someone recovering from a disease takes food but does not gain strength, it indicates that the body is being burdened by too much nourishment; but if this happens to one eating only moderately, it should be understood that the body requires purging <term>vacuatione: the medical process of emptying or clearing the body of harmful fluids or waste, often through laxatives or bloodletting</term>.

### I X.

When one wishes to purge the body, one must first make it "fluid" <note>The physician would attempt to "thin" the thick, stagnant fluids (humors) of the body through diet or steam to make them easier to expel.</note>.

### X.

The more you nourish impure bodies, the more you harm them <note>Hippocratic theory held that feeding a body full of "bad humors" only provided more fuel for the disease.</note>.

### X I.

It is easier to be restored by drink than by food.

### X I I.

Whatever is left behind inside the body after the crisis <term>judicationem: also known as a "crisis," the moment when a disease reaches its peak and the body either overcomes the illness or succumbs to it</term> is likely to cause a relapse of the disease.

<meta>catchword: XIII. Those who-</meta>

It highlights the Hippocratic focus on moderation and the necessity of "cleansing" the body's internal environment to prevent a relapse.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the transition into Book II of the Aphorisms. While Book I focused largely on general regimens and the influence of seasons, Book II introduces specific diagnostic signs (prognostics) regarding sleep, appetite, and the "crisis" or turning point of an illness.</meta>

BOOK II.
<meta>catchword: XIII, Night</meta>

# BOOK THE SECOND.

### I.
In whatever disease sleep causes a deadly struggle, it is a fatal sign; but if sleep brings benefit, it is by no means to be considered deadly.

### I I.
Let it be a good sign when delirium <note>original: "deliria," referring to the mental wandering or agitation often associated with high fevers.</note> is removed by sleep.

### I I I.
Both to sleep too much and to stay awake too much are harmful.

### I V.
Neither satiety nor hunger is to be praised, nor anything else which has transgressed the accustomed limits of nature.

### V.
Weariness that arises of its own accord <note>original: "sponte fatigari," meaning fatigue occurring without the exertion of labor or exercise.</note> shall be a sign of disease.

### V I.
One who has pain in a specific part of the body, yet feels almost no pain there, is suffering from an unhealthy mind <note>The ancient physician viewed a lack of physical sensation during obvious injury as a sign that the patient's internal "reason" or consciousness was impaired.</note>.

### V I I.
Bodies that have wasted away gradually should be restored slowly; those thinned out in a short time should be restored quickly.

### V I I I.
If someone eating after a disease regains no strength, it indicates that too much food is being given to them; but if they eat only sparingly and still do not recover, it is necessary to empty <term>vacuare: the medical practice of purging or clearing the body of excess humors or waste</term>.

### I X.
In order to purge the body, you must first make it "flowing" <note>original: "fluxile." Before administering a purge, doctors believed the body's humors needed to be softened or thinned so they could be easily expelled.</note>.

### X.
The more you nourish an impure body, the more you harm it.

### X I.
Liquid food and drink provide strength more readily than solid food.

### X I I.
Those things which remain in the body after the "judgment" <term>judicium: the medical "crisis," a critical turning point where the body either overcomes the disease or succumbs to it</term> has occurred are accustomed to produce relapses of the disease.

->APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES,<-

### X I I I.

*For those who experience a crisis <note>original: "crisis," the turning point or decisive stage of a disease.</note>, the night preceding the attack is usually distressing; however, the night that follows is generally easier.*

### X I V.

*In cases of bowel flux <note>original: "alvi fluxionibus," referring to diarrhea or dysentery.</note>, changes in the nature of the discharge are beneficial, provided they do not change for the worse.*

### X V.

*When the throat is diseased or swellings <note>original: "tubercula," which could mean pustules, tumors, or internal lumps.</note> appear on the body, the excretions must be carefully examined. For if they are bilious <term>bilious: relating to an excess of yellow bile, one of the four humors believed to cause heat and irritation in the body</term>, the whole body is sick. But if they appear like the excretions of healthy people, you may safely nourish the body.*

### X V I.

*When hunger prevails, one must abstain from all exertion and labor.*

### X I I I.

*When food is taken in quantities greater than is natural, it creates disease.*

### X V I I I.

*Those things which nourish the body abundantly and quickly are also usually excreted quickly.*

### X I X.

*In acute diseases, predictions of death or recovery are not entirely certain.*

### X X.

*Those who have loose bowels in their youth will find them dry in their old age; conversely, those whose bowels were dry and constricted in youth will find them loose and relaxed in old age.*

### X X I.

*Wine dissolves hunger.*

### X X I I.

*Just as evacuation cures diseases arising from fullness, so fullness cures those arising from evacuation; in all other cases, the remedy is found in opposites <note>original: "contrarietas," referencing the ancient medical principle that a condition is cured by its opposite, such as treating a "cold" illness with "heat."</note>.*

### X I I I.

*Acute diseases are brought to their judgment <note>original: "judicatione," the moment when the disease is decided for better or worse.</note> within fourteen days.*

### X X I V.

*The fourth day is the indicator for the seventh; the eighth day is the indicator for the following week.*

<meta>This page continues the medical aphorisms of Book II, focusing on the timing of disease crises, the relationship between diet and health, and the observation of bodily cycles. It follows the previous discussion on sleep and the recovery of strength.</meta>

<header>BOOK II.</header>

### X I I I.

The night before a crisis <note>original: "crisim"; the turning point or decisive stage of a disease</note> is usually more troublesome than the night after it.

### X I V.

In bowel fluxes <note>original: "fluxibus alvi"; referring to diarrhea or dysentery</note>, a variation in the appearance of the discharge is beneficial to the patient, unless the change is for the worse.

### X V.

When the throat is painful or swellings <note>original: "tubercula"; internal or external lumps or pustules</note> appear, you must inspect the excretions. For if they are full of bile <term>bile: one of the four "humors" or bodily fluids; an excess of yellow bile was thought to cause heat and irritability</term>, the whole body is sick; but if they appear healthy, one may safely nourish the body without fear.

### X V I.

It is right to avoid all labor when hunger prevails.

### X V I I.

Too great a quantity of food produces disease.

### X V I I I.

Whatever nourishes the body quickly is also usually expelled from the body quickly.

### X I X.

In acute diseases, the prognosis <note>original: "prognosis"; the physician's prediction of the course of a disease</note> often fails, whether it predicts death or recovery.

### X X.

Those who have loose and easy bowels in their youth usually find them more constricted in old age; conversely, those whose bowels were dry in youth usually find them loose in old age.

### X I I I.

A drink of generous wine <note>original: "vini generosi"; high-quality or potent wine</note> relieves hunger.

### X X I I.

Just as replenishment cures diseases caused by depletion, so evacuation heals diseases caused by fullness; indeed, in all other cases, contraries are beneficial to all. <note>This reflects the ancient medical principle that "opposites are cured by opposites."</note>

### X X I I I.

Acute diseases usually reach their end through a judgment <note>original: "judicio"; the medical "crisis" or decisive resolution of the illness</note> within the space of fourteen days.

### X X I V.

The fourth day is the indicator of the seventh day; the eighth day is the beginning of the following week <term>hebdomadæ: a seven-day cycle</term>, and it should be observed in the same manner.

It emphasizes the importance of monitoring excretions and timing the progression of acute illnesses by seven-day cycles.</summary>

*...the beginning: The eleventh day must also be watched, since it is the fourth day of the second week. Again, the seventeenth must be watched, for it is the fourth day from the fourteenth, and the seventh from the eleventh.* <note>original: "undecimus... septimanæ quartus"; Hippocratic medicine relied heavily on "critical days" based on Pythagorean number theory to predict when a disease would change or resolve.</note>

->XXV.<-

*Summer quartan fevers <term>quartan fever: a type of malaria where the fever recurs every fourth day, counting the day of the fever itself (every 72 hours)</term> are mostly short; autumn ones are long, especially those that persist into the winter.*

->XXVI.<-

*It is better for a fever to follow a convulsion than for a convulsion to follow a fever.* <note>Hippocrates suggests that a fever can sometimes "cook" or resolve the causes of a convulsion, whereas a convulsion caused by a fever indicates a dangerous escalation.</note>

->XXVII.<-

*If there is relief from symptoms without a rational cause, you should not trust it; nor should you greatly fear bad symptoms that occur without reason. For many such changes are uncertain and are not accustomed to last or persist for long.*

->XXVIII.<-

*In those suffering from a significant fever, it is a bad sign for the body to remain in the same state and not lose weight at all, or to waste away even more than the condition would suggest; for the former signifies a long-lasting disease, while the latter signifies great weakness.*

->XXIX.<-

*If any movement <note>original: "movendum"; likely referring to medical evacuation, such as purging or bloodletting</note> seems necessary at the beginning of a disease, do it. However, when the disease is at its peak <note>original: "vigentibus"; the most intense stage of the illness</note>, it is much better to let the body rest.*

->XXX.<-

*Near the beginnings and the ends of an illness, all symptoms are at their lightest; but around the peak <note>original: "vigores"</note>, they are most violent.*

->XXXI.<-

*If a body gains no strength in someone who is eating well during a disease, it is a bad sign.*

->XXXII.<-

*As a general rule, those who are ill and eat well at the beginning but show no improvement will eventually lose their appetite. On the other hand, those who strongly dislike food at the beginning but later regain a healthy appetite fare much better.*

->XXXIII.<-

*In any disease, it is a good sign for the mind <note>original: "rationem"; referring to the patient's mental clarity or consciousness</note> to remain strong, and for the patient to respond well to those things...*

<meta>This page continues the poetic Latin rendition of Hippocrates' Aphorisms, specifically Book II. It follows the discussion of "critical days"—specific days in the progression of a disease when a change or resolution is expected—and moves into clinical observations on fever duration, patient weight, and appetite as prognostic signs.</meta>

# B O O K  I I.

The eleventh day is the fourth day of the second week; the seventeenth day must also be kept in mind, for it is noted as the fourth day after the fourteenth, and exactly the seventh after the eleventh. <note>original: "undecima... septima lucem"; Hippocratic medicine taught that diseases followed numerical cycles. Physicians tracked these "critical days" to predict when a patient might recover or worsen.</note>

### X X V.

A summer quartan fever <term>quartan fever: a type of malaria characterized by a paroxysm that occurs every fourth day</term> is short, but one beginning in autumn lasts longer, especially if winter overtakes it.

### X X V I.

A fever following a convulsion <term>spasmus: a convulsion or involuntary muscle contraction</term> is better than a convulsion following a fever. <note>Hippocrates observed that a fever might "cook" or resolve the underlying cause of a convulsion, whereas a convulsion caused by high fever suggests a dangerous progression of the disease.</note>

### X X V I I.

If symptoms provide some relief, but not according to a rational cause, trust them little; nor should you dread bad symptoms too greatly if they do not seem to happen for a legitimate reason. For such changes are usually uncertain and tend to vanish quickly.

### X X V I I I.

When the body of one with a high fever remains constantly in the same state, losing almost nothing, or conversely wastes away more than reason would suggest, both are bad signs. The former predicts a long-lasting illness; the latter usually testifies to immoderate weakness.

### X X I X.

At the onset of a disease, move whatever needs moving <note>original: "movenda"; this refers to medical evacuations, such as using purgatives to clear humors, or bloodletting, which were best done early.</note>; however, while the illness is at its peak vigor, it is better to remain at rest.

### X X X.

Everything is mildest around the beginning and the end; all things are to be feared more when the illness is at its peak vigor.

### X X X I.

It is a bad sign for a patient who eats properly to remain thin and wasted.

### X X X I I.

Those who are unwell and eat much at the beginning, yet gain nothing from it, eventually succumb to nausea. But those who initially have a strong aversion to food and later regain their hunger recover more successfully.

### X X X I I I.

When the mind remains steady through illnesses, and to all things properly <note>original: "ritè"; the text cuts off here, continuing the thought that a sound mind is a positive sign for recovery.</note>

<meta>This page continues the Latin transcription of Hippocrates' Aphorisms, Book II. The previous page ended with a discussion on the onset of diseases and the importance of appetite and nourishment. This page picks up mid-sentence at the end of Aphorism 33, discussing the body's reaction to intake.</meta>

->APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES.<-

*...to be in possession of those things which are taken in is good; conversely, to be in such a state [otherwise] is bad.* <note>original: "quæ sumūtur habere, bonū..." This is the conclusion of Aphorism 33, referring to the body's ability to retain and utilize nourishment.</note>

### X X X I V.

*During illnesses, there is less danger for those whose disease is more closely related to their nature, age, constitution, and the current season of the year, than for those in whom the disease is not at all related to any of these factors.* <note>Hippocratic medicine emphasized "constitutional" health—the idea that a winter disease is less dangerous in winter than in summer, or that a disease typical of childhood is less dangerous for a child.</note>

### X X X V.

*In any disease, it is better for the parts around the navel and the lower belly to have some thickness; however, for them to be extremely thin and wasted away is a bad sign. This state is also quite unsafe for administering lower purgations.* <term>purgationes: the use of laxatives or other treatments to "cleanse" the bowels of "corrupt humors."</term>

### X X X I V I.

*Those who are endowed with healthy bodies are immediately weakened by drinking a purgative medicine, as are those who consume poor-quality food.*

### X X X V I I.

*Those who are in perfect bodily health tolerate medications with difficulty and distress.* <note>The author suggests that medical intervention is a violent disruption to a balanced system; a healthy body resists the "poison" of the medicine more visibly than a body already accustomed to illness.</note>

### X X X V I I I.

*Food and drink that are slightly lower in quality, yet more pleasant to the taste, are to be preferred over those that are higher in quality but less agreeable.* <note>This reflects the Hippocratic belief that the patient's spirit and appetite are vital to recovery; food that the patient enjoys is better digested.</note>

### X X X I X.

*Generally speaking, old men fall ill less often than young men. But those chronic diseases that do happen to take hold of them usually accompany them until death.*

### X L.

*Hoarseness and heavy colds in those who are very old are not "cooked."* <term>coquantur: a technical term in humoral theory meaning "concocted" or "ripened." Physicians believed a disease had to "cook" (reach maturity) before the body could expel the harmful fluids.</term>

### X L I.

*Those who frequently and violently faint without any obvious cause are liable to die suddenly.* <note>original: "animo linquuntur" (to be left by the spirit/mind). This likely refers to cardiac issues or severe syncope.</note>

### X L I I.

*A severe apoplexy <term>apoplexia: a sudden loss of consciousness or paralysis, usually what we now call a stroke</term> is in no way cured, and even a mild one is not easily healed.*

### X L I I I.

*Of those who have been strangled or suffocated but are not yet dead, they will not return to life if foam has gathered around the mouth.*

<meta>This page continues the Latin translation of Hippocrates' Aphorisms, Book II. The previous page ended with the beginning of Aphorism 38. # BOOK II.

->APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES.<-

If the sick person retains what is given to him, it is a sign of recovery; however, if his body rejects it, it bodes ill for him. <note>original: "Sefe æger quæ dantur habet..." This concludes the thought from the previous page regarding the body's ability to hold and process nourishment.</note>

### XXXIV.

In diseases, there is less danger when the illness corresponds to the patient's nature, age, and constitution, as well as to the season of the year and the patient's manner of living, than when the disease has no affinity with any of these factors. <note>Hippocratic medicine taught that a "natural" illness—such as a cough in winter—was easier for the body to process than an "unnatural" one, like a winter fever in the height of summer.</note>

### XXXV.

In any disease, it is beneficial for the parts of the lower belly to maintain some degree of thickness; for if they are thin and wasted, it is a dangerous sign. Such a wasted state also makes the administration of lower purgatives unsafe. <term>purgamina: medicines or treatments used to "purge" or cleanse the bowels of harmful humors.</term>

### XXXVI.

Those who have healthy bodies, as well as those who nourish their bodies with poor-quality food, quickly lose their strength after taking a purgative medicine.

### XXXVII.

Those who are in good health tolerate medication with great difficulty. <note>The idea here is that a balanced, healthy system reacts more violently to the disruptive "poison" of medicine than a body already accustomed to the imbalance of disease.</note>

### XXXVIII.

A diet that is slightly inferior, but more pleasing to the patient, is to be preferred over a diet that is nutritionally better but less pleasant. <note>Hippocrates often emphasized the importance of the patient's appetite, believing that the body digests food better when the patient actually enjoys it.</note>

### XXXIX.

Young people are more frequently subject to illness than the elderly; however, when chronic diseases begin to afflict the old, they generally do not leave them until death.

### XL.

In the very old, hoarseness and catarrh cannot be "cooked." <term>coqui: in Hippocratic theory, this refers to 'pepsis'—the process where the body's internal heat "ripens" or "cooks" diseased fluids so they can be expelled.</term>

### XLI.

Those who frequently and severely faint without any manifest or obvious cause are prone to die suddenly.

### XLII.

Severe apoplexy cannot be cured by any means, and even a mild case is cured only with great difficulty. <term>apoplexia: a sudden loss of consciousness or paralysis, historically associated with what we now call a stroke.</term>

### XLIII.

Of those patients who have been seized by a shortness of breath but have not yet perished, there is no hope of recovery if foam collects around the mouth. <note>The "foam" likely refers to the pulmonary edema or severe respiratory distress seen in the final stages of heart or lung failure.</note>

<meta>catchword: XLIV. Na-</meta>

# BOOK II.

->APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES.<-

### XLIV.

Those who are naturally very stout are liable to shorter lives than those who are slender. <note>original: "Qui naturâ sunt valde crassi..." This observation reflects the Hippocratic view that extreme body types were less balanced and therefore more prone to sudden death.</note>

### XLV.

Young people suffering from epilepsy are freed from the disease by a change of location, a change in diet, and especially by the changes that come with age. <note>original: "Epilepsiâ laborantes juvenes..." In ancient medicine, epilepsy was often called the "Sacred Disease." Hippocrates believed that as a child grew and their "humors" shifted, the disease might naturally resolve.</note>

### XLVI.

When two pains occur at the same time but in different parts of the body, the more violent one dulls the sensation of the other.

### XLVII.

Pains and fevers occur more while pus is in the process of forming than after it has been fully formed. <note>original: "Dum pus fit..." This refers to the process of suppuration in an abscess or wound; the "ripening" of the infection is considered the most painful stage.</note>

### XLVIII.

In any movement of the body, as soon as one begins to feel the strain of labor, rest is an immediate remedy for the fatigue.

### XLIX.

Those who are accustomed to enduring their usual labors, even if they are weak or old, bear them more easily than those who are unaccustomed to them, even if they are strong and young.

### L.

Things which one has been accustomed to over a long period of time, even if they are of a poorer quality, usually cause less trouble than things to which one is unaccustomed. Therefore, a change to unusual things must also be made. <note>This suggests that the body adapts to its environment and habits, even bad ones, and that any shift—even toward a "better" habit—should be managed carefully.</note>

### LI.

To empty or fill, to heat or cool, or to move the body in any other way suddenly and all at once is dangerous; for everything in excess is an enemy to nature. But that which is done gradually is safe, especially when one passes from one state to another. <note>original: "omne siquidem nimium naturae inimicum." This is the famous medical maxim "Nothing in excess," emphasizing that the body's nature (physis) requires stability and gradual transitions.</note>

### LII.

If a physician is acting according to right reason, but things do not succeed according to that reason, he should not immediately change to another course of treatment, provided that the original plan still seems correct. <note>Hippocrates encourages clinical consistency; if a diagnosis is sound, a doctor should not abandon the treatment just because the patient does not show immediate improvement.</note>

### LIII.

Those who have moist bowels fare better in their youth than those who have dry ones; but in old age they fare worse, for as people age, they generally become more dried out. <term>alvos humidas: literally "moist bellies," referring here to a tendency toward loose stools or a more fluid digestive system, which was thought to be healthier for the young.</term>

<meta>catchword: LIV. Le</meta>

<summary>This page contains Aphorisms 44 through 53 of Book II, focusing on how age, habit, and gradual change affect health. It highlights the core Hippocratic principle that sudden extremes are harmful to the body's natural balance.</summary>

# BOOK II.

### X L I V.

Those who are naturally and excessively stout are generally liable to shorter lives than those who are naturally slender. <note>original: "Naturâ immodicè crassi." The Hippocratic tradition often warned that extreme obesity was a state of unstable health, as the body had reached the limit of its "fullness."</note>

### X L V.

Young people are cured of the falling sickness <note>The "falling sickness" is an archaic term for epilepsy (Latin: "morbo caduco").</note> by changes in diet, location, and lifestyle, but especially by the natural transition of age into adulthood.

### X L I V I.

When two pains occur at the same time but in different parts of the body, the one that is more intense usually obscures the lesser one.

### X L V I I.

Both pain and fever lessen once the pus has been fully formed. <note>original: "confecto pure." This refers to the maturation of an abscess; the most intense pain occurs while the "humors" are transitioning into pus, and relief follows once the process is complete.</note>

### X L V I I I.

In any movement of the body, as soon as one begins to feel the strain of labor, rest provides immediate relief.

### X L I X.

Those who are accustomed to a task, even if they are weak or elderly, can endure the labor more readily than those who are unaccustomed to it, even if the latter are strong and in the flower of their youth.

### L.

Things which one has been accustomed to over a long period of time, even if they are of a poorer quality, usually cause less trouble than things to which one is unaccustomed; therefore, a change to an unfamiliar routine ought to be made gradually. <note>Hippocratic medicine placed immense value on "regimen"—the daily habits of a patient. Sudden changes to diet or exercise were seen as a shock to the system.</note>

### L I.

To fill or empty the body all at once and suddenly, or to suddenly produce cold or heat, or to disturb the body in any other way is harmful; for every excess is contrary to nature. However, that which proceeds gradually is safer, especially when one passes from one state to another little by little.

### L I I.

If one is managing a case according to correct principles and yet things do not succeed, one should not immediately switch to new treatments for a lingering disease, provided that the symptoms that were observed at the beginning remain the same. <note>This is a warning against "physician's panic"—urging the doctor to trust their original diagnosis if the underlying conditions haven't changed.</note>

### L I I I.

Those who had loose bowels in their youth generally fare well at first; but in their old age they fare worse, for the bowels then usually become drier.

APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES

### L I V.

A tall physical stature in youth is indeed noble and not unseemly; but in old age it is impractical, and worse than a short stature. <note>original: "Longa corporis statura." In the Hippocratic view, a tall frame was associated with vitality and "liberalis" (noble/gentlemanly) beauty in youth, but was believed to become a physical burden as the body's strength or "innate heat" declined with age.</note>

->END OF THE SECOND BOOK.<-

---

# BOOK THREE.

### I.

Changes of the seasons are especially likely to produce diseases, as are great fluctuations of cold or heat within the seasons themselves, and other factors corresponding to these in proportion. <note>Hippocrates is often credited with the first systematic study of how environment and weather (the "constitutions" of the air) affect human health.</note>

### I I.

Some constitutions <note>The term "Naturæ" here refers to an individual's physical temperament or "nature."</note> are naturally well or poorly suited to summer, while others are better or worse in winter.

### I I I.

Different diseases fare well or poorly at different times <note>Meaning different seasons.</note>, as do certain ages in relation to seasons, locations, and different types of diet.

### I V.

In seasons where heat and cold alternate on the same day, autumnal diseases are to be expected. <note>The "autumnal diseases" typically referred to fevers, particularly malarial-type fevers, which the Greeks observed were most common when the weather became unpredictable.</note>

### V.

The South Wind <term>Auster</term> <note>The *Auster* was the hot, humid wind from the south. In humoral medicine, it was thought to "melt" the humors, leading to sluggishness.</note> dulls the hearing, clouds the vision, weighs down the head, and brings a slowness and listlessness to the limbs. Therefore, when this wind prevails, such symptoms occur in those who are weak. On the other hand, the North Wind <term>Aquilo</term> <note>The *Aquilo* was the cold, dry wind from the north, believed to "brace" or "constrict" the body.</note> provokes coughing, irritates the throat, hardens the bowels, suppresses urination, causes shivering, and produces pains in the sides and chest. Consequently, when this wind dominates, one should expect such ailments in the infirm.

### V I.

When summer resembles spring <note>Meaning a summer that is unusually cool and moist rather than hot and dry.</note>, one should expect profuse sweating in cases of fever.

BOOK II.

B

### LIV.

A tall physical stature is indeed handsome in a young person, but it is less suitable for the elderly, and being tall is considered a worse condition for them than being short. <note>original: "Longa statura... pejor brevitate." In the Hippocratic tradition, while a tall frame was admired as "speciosa" (beautiful or noble) in youth, it was believed that as the body's "vital heat" declined with age, a larger frame became a burden that the aging system could no longer properly sustain.</note>

---
->END OF THE SECOND BOOK.<-

# BOOK THREE.

### I.

The change of the seasons is the primary cause of diseases, as are the various shifts between cold and heat that occur within those seasons, along with any other environmental factors that change in similar proportion. <note>Hippocrates was a pioneer in "environmental medicine," arguing that doctors must study the local climate and seasons to predict which diseases will strike a population.</note>

### II.

Some people are naturally healthier in winter, while others find summer to be more favorable to their constitution.

### III.

Different diseases behave better or worse at different times of the year; furthermore, certain stages of life are better or worse suited to specific locations, dietary habits, and seasons.

### IV.

When both heat and cold are felt on the same day, it is right to expect the onset of autumnal diseases. <note>original: "autumnales... morbos." These often referred to intermittent fevers, such as malaria, which the Greeks noticed peaked during the fluctuating weather of late summer and autumn.</note>

### V.

The South Wind <term>Auster</term> <note>original: "Auster." A warm, humid wind from the south believed to "moisten" the brain and body.</note> dulls the hearing and clouds the vision; it makes the limbs feel heavy and fatigues the head with a sense of weight. When this wind blows frequently, those with weaker constitutions suffer these symptoms. But conversely, the North Wind <term>Boreas</term> <note>original: "Boreas." A cold, dry wind from the north.</note> triggers coughs and irritates the throat, suppresses the flow of urine, and causes the bowels to be dry and constricted. It also produces shivering and various pains in the chest. It is necessary for the infirm to expect these conditions when the North Wind prevails.

### VI.

When the summer remains similar in character to the spring, it is common for profuse sweating to break out during fevers.

<summary>This page concludes Book II with a note on physical stature and begins Book III, which explores how seasonal changes and specific winds like the Auster and Boreas affect human health.</summary>
<keywords>Hippocrates, aphorisms, seasons, South Wind, North Wind, environmental health, autumnal diseases, elderly health</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Third Book of Hippocrates' Aphorisms. Following the introductory remarks on the general influence of seasons, this section provides specific clinical predictions based on the shifting patterns of winds and moisture throughout the year. It bridges the previous page’s focus on seasonal changes with detailed observations on how "unseasonable" weather affects different demographics, such as women, children, and the elderly.</meta>

APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES.

->VII.<-

Through unclean conditions, acute fevers are produced; and if the year for the most part remains in such a state as the quality of the season has made it, one should generally expect diseases of a similar nature. <note>original: "Per squalores." This refers to environmental "filth" or "foulness" in the air and water, reflecting the ancient belief that diseases were caused by miasma or corrupted environments.</note>

->VIII.<-

In fixed seasons that maintain their own proper character, the diseases that arise are likewise fixed, seasonal, and easy to judge <note>In this context, "judge" refers to "crisis"—the point at which a doctor can predict if a patient will recover or die.</note>. However, in irregular and unseasonable times, the illnesses also become irregular and difficult to diagnose.

->IX.<-

During autumn, diseases are generally at their most acute and most fatal. Spring, however, is the healthiest time and the least deadly.

->X.<-

Autumn is harmful to those suffering from consumption. <term>Tabes</term> <note>original: "tabidis." Referring to those with "wasting" diseases, most notably tuberculosis, which the Greeks believed worsened as the year turned cold and damp.</note>

->XI.<-

Regarding the seasons of the year: if the winter is drier than is proper and dominated by <term>North Winds</term> <note>original: "aquilonia." The Aquilo was a cold, dry wind from the north-northeast.</note>, but the spring is very rainy and dominated by <term>South Winds</term> <note>original: "austrinum." The Auster was a hot, humid wind from the south.</note>, it is inevitable that acute fevers, eye inflammations, and intestinal difficulties will arise in the summer, especially in women and in men of a naturally "moist" constitution.

->XII.<-

But if the winter is dominated by south winds, very rainy, and mild, while the spring is drier than it should be and dominated by north winds, women whose delivery is due in the spring will miscarry for any reason. Those who do give birth will produce weak and sickly infants, such that they either die immediately or live as thin and frail children. In other people, dry eye inflammations and intestinal troubles arise. In the elderly, however, there occur catarrhs <note>original: "defluxiones." Literally a "flowing down" of humors from the head, which the ancients believed caused sudden death in the old by suffocating the lungs.</note> that kill quickly.

->XIII.<-

If the summer is drier than is proper and dominated by north winds, but the autumn is very rainy and dominated by south winds, headaches occur in the winter, as well as coughs, hoarseness, nasal congestion, and in some people, consumption.

<keywords>Hippocrates, Aphorisms, Seasonal health, North Wind (Aquilo), South Wind (Auster), Consumption (Tabes), Pregnancy, Humoral theory, Environmental medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Third Book of Hippocrates' Aphorisms. Following the clinical predictions based on shifting wind and moisture patterns, this page details how specific seasonal sequences—such as a dry winter followed by a rainy spring—dictate the types of illnesses that will emerge in the summer and autumn. It focuses heavily on the vulnerability of pregnant women, the elderly, and those with "moist" constitutions.</meta>

BOOK III.

### V I I.

In dry periods, acute fevers arise; but if such weather persists, so that the general state of the year remains the same, it is inevitable that diseases of a similar nature will follow. <note>original: "Temporibus siccis febres oriuntur acutæ." The author suggests that consistency in weather, even if harsh, leads to predictable patterns of illness.</note>

### V I I I.

When the seasons maintain their proper character throughout, diseases follow a regular course and are easy to judge <note>In ancient medicine, to "judge" a disease meant to predict its "crisis"—the moment it would either resolve or become fatal.</note>; but when the seasons proceed in an inverse or irregular order, the diseases likewise become unpredictable and difficult to treat.

### I X.

In autumn, diseases arise that are more acute and more lethal; by contrast, the season of spring is the healthiest of all.

### X.

The autumn season is harmful to those suffering from <term>consumption</term>. <note>original: "tabe." Known as *tabes* in Latin or *phthisis* in Greek, this refers to wasting diseases, most commonly tuberculosis.</note>

### X I.

If the winter should be northerly and dry, but the spring is southerly and soaked with rains, then in summer many are vexed by acute fevers, and by <term>ophthalmia</term> <note>Inflammation or infection of the eyes.</note>, and also by the griping of the bowels <note>original: "tormine ventris." This refers to dysentery or severe abdominal cramping.</note>; this affects women especially, and those men with "moister" constitutions. <note>According to the theory of the four humors, some individuals were naturally "wetter" (phlegmatic or sanguine), making them more susceptible to diseases caused by excess moisture.</note>

### X I I.

If, on the contrary, the winter is southerly and rainy, but the spring was northerly, for those women who happen to give birth in the springtime, miscarriage will occur from the slightest cause. Those who do successfully give birth are accustomed to produce sickly offspring, so that they either die immediately or live always weak and unhealthy. Among others, there will occur dry eye inflammations, griping of the bowels, and <term>rheums</term> <note>original: "rheumatáque." A "rheum" or flux was believed to be a discharge of fluid (like mucus) from the head into the lower body; in the elderly, this was often associated with pneumonia or stroke.</note> in the elderly, which soon prove fatal.

### X I I I.

But when the summer is blown through by the North Wind and is drier, but the autumn was southerly and rainy, coughs and headaches occur toward winter, and much hoarseness and <term>stuffiness</term> <note>original: "gravedo." A heavy, dull feeling in the head caused by congestion or catarrh.</note> tend to spread; in some, consumption even begins to arise.

This page concludes the discussion on specific wind patterns and begins mapping the "Ages of Man" and specific diseases to the four seasons.</meta>

->APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES<-

### X I V.
If, however, the north wind prevails and is without rain, it is favorable for those who are of a more <term>moist nature</term> <note>In the humoral theory of medicine, a "moist" constitution was often associated with phlegm or an excess of bodily fluids, common in children and certain temperaments.</note> and for women. For others, however, there will be dry eye inflammations, acute and long-lasting fevers, and some will be afflicted by <term>melancholia</term>. <note>original: "melancholia." In ancient medicine, this was literally "black bile," believed to cause both physical illness and depression or madness.</note>

### X V.
Regarding the general conditions of the year, great droughts are overall healthier and less lethal than periods of constant rain.

### X I V I.
During constant rains, diseases for the most part arise as long fevers, fluxes of the bowels <note>diarrhea or dysentery</note>, putrefactions, <term>epilepsy</term> <note>original: "comitiales." Called the "comitial disease" because meetings of the Roman assembly (*comitia*) were abandoned if someone had a seizure.</note>, apoplexy, and <term>angina</term>. <note>original: "angina." Here, this refers to a severe swelling of the throat or "quinsy" that threatens suffocation, rather than the modern heart condition.</note> During great droughts, however, wasting diseases, eye inflammations, joint pains, difficulty urinating <note>original: "urinæ stillicidia." A slow or painful dribbling of urine.</note>, and disorders of the intestines occur.

### X V I I.
As for daily weather conditions: northerly winds condense the body, making it stronger, more nimble, well-colored, and better at hearing; they dry the bowels but sting the eyes, and if any pain previously existed in the chest, they irritate it further. Southerly winds, however, relax and moisten the body; they bring dullness of hearing, heaviness of the head, and dizziness, and they loosen the bowels.

### X V I I I.
With respect to the seasons: in spring and early summer, children and those closest to them in age fare best and are at their healthiest. In summer and into part of the autumn, the elderly. For the remainder of autumn and in winter, those of middle age.

### X I X.
Any kind of disease can occur in any season. However, some are more likely to occur and are more aggravated during certain specific times.

### X X.
In the spring, there occur: madness, melancholia, epilepsy, flows of blood, throat swellings, head-colds, hoarseness, coughs,

<insert>leprosies</insert> <note>original catchword: "lepra." The text continues on the next page with a list of skin diseases common in spring.</note>
