# BOOK III.

### XIV.
If, however, the autumn is dominated by the North wind and is without rain, it will be beneficial for women and for men of a <term>moister nature</term> <note>In ancient medicine, "moist" temperaments were thought to have an excess of fluids like phlegm; the dry North wind was believed to balance this excess.</note>. For others, however, it is likely that fevers—either acute or long-lasting—will occur, as well as dry eye inflammations, and some will be badly afflicted by <term>melancholy</term> <note>original: "melancholicus morbus." Literally "black bile disease," which practitioners believed caused both physical ailments and despondency.</note>.

### XV.
A drier season is more healthful and less lethal than one of constant rain.

### XVI.
These are the diseases that constant rain produces: <term>apoplexy</term> <note>original: "attonitus morbus," literally the "thunderstruck disease," referring to a sudden loss of sense or motion.</note>, putrefaction, <term>quinsy</term> <note>original: "angina." A severe inflammation of the throat that could lead to suffocation.</note>, <term>epilepsy</term> <note>original: "caducus," the "falling sickness."</note>, long-lasting fevers, and a flux of the bowels. In excessive dry spells, however, there occur <term>dribbling of urine</term> <note>original: "mictûs stillatio."</note>, intestinal cramps, arthritis, <term>consumption</term> <note>original: "tabes." A general term for the wasting away of the body, often associated with tuberculosis.</note>, and likewise eye inflammations.

### XVII.
The North wind strengthens and firms the body; it makes one more nimble in movement, improves the complexion and the hearing, and binds the bowels. However, it may sharpen pains in the chest and "bite" the eyes. By contrast, the South wind <note>original: "Auster."</note> affects the head with heaviness and the vision with dizziness; it moistens the body and relaxes it too much, making the limbs languid and producing a loose bowel.

### XVIII.
In the spring and early summer, children and those close to them in age <note>youths</note> tend to be healthy. In the summer and the first part of autumn, the elderly usually fare better. For the remaining times of the year, those of <term>middle age</term> <note>original: "constans ætas," the "consistent" or "steady age."</note> remain the most stable.

### XIX.
While any kind of disease can arise at any time of year, some diseases seem to be more provoked and occur more frequently in certain seasons.

### XX.
These diseases are accustomed to arise in the springtime: leprosy, outbreaks of melancholy, hoarseness, <term>head colds</term> <note>original: "gravedo." A heavy, congested feeling in the head.</note>, madness, hemorrhages, coughs, quinsy, and epilepsy.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of diseases associated with seasons and ages of life from Book III of the Aphorisms of Hippocrates. # BOOK III.

...leprosy, <term>scaly eruptions</term> <note>original: "impetigines." These were generic terms for various skin diseases characterized by rough or itchy patches.</note>, skin discolorations, many ulcerous pustules, swellings, and joint pains.

->XX I.<-

In summer, some of these same conditions persist, along with continuous fevers, <term>burning fevers</term> <note>original: "ardentes." Known as 'causus' in Greek medicine, these were high, dehydrating fevers.</note>, many <term>tertian</term> and <term>quartan</term> fevers <note>Tertian fevers recur every 48 hours; quartan every 72 hours. These patterns were vital for ancient diagnosis.</note>, vomiting, diarrhea, inflamed eyes, earaches, mouth ulcers, rotting of the genitals, and <term>heat rash</term> <note>original: "sudamina." Literally "sweat-spots," referring to prickly heat or similar skin irritations caused by warmth.</note>.

->XX II.<-

In autumn, many of the summer diseases continue, as well as quartan and irregular fevers, <term>spleen conditions</term> <note>original: "lienes." The ancients believed the spleen was the seat of black bile, which predominated in autumn.</note>, <term>dropsy</term> <note>original: "aqua inter cutem." Literally "water between the skin," referring to generalized swelling or edema.</note>, consumption, <term>dribbling of urine</term> <note>original: "urinæ stillicidia." Also known as strangury.</note>, <term>lientery</term> <note>original: "intestinorum lævitates." A type of diarrhea where food passes through the body undigested.</note>, dysentery, hip pains, <term>sore throats</term> <note>original: "anginæ." Specifically inflammatory conditions like quinsy that obstruct breathing.</note>, shortness of breath, <term>bowel obstructions</term> <note>original: "volvuli." A painful twisting of the intestines.</note>, <term>epilepsy</term> <note>original: "comitiales." Called the "assembly disease" because a seizure was seen as a bad omen, causing the Roman 'comitia' (assembly) to be dismissed.</note>, madness, and <term>melancholy</term>.

->XX III.<-

In winter, there occur side-pains, lung inflammations, <term>lethargy</term> <note>original: "lethargi." A specific type of fever marked by excessive drowsiness and cognitive dullness.</note>, heavy head colds, hoarseness, coughs, pains in the chest, sides, and lower back, headaches, dizziness, and <term>apoplexy</term> <note>Sudden loss of consciousness or paralysis, similar to a modern stroke.</note>.

->XX IV.<-

Regarding the ages of life, these things happen to infants and the newly born: <term>thrush</term> <note>original: "aphthæ." White ulcers in the mouth common in infants.</note>, vomiting, coughs, sleeplessness, night terrors, inflammation of the navel, and discharge from the ears.

->XX V.<-

For those who have progressed to teething, there are irritating itches of the gums, fevers, convulsions, and diarrhea—especially when they begin to cut their <term>canine teeth</term> <note>original: "caninos dentes." The "eye teeth," traditionally thought to cause more severe symptoms during eruption.</note>, and particularly in those children who are quite heavy and have hard bowels.

->XX VI.<-

For those slightly older, there are inflammations of the tonsils, inward displacement of the vertebra at the back of the head, shortness of breath, stones in the bladder, roundworms, <term>warts</term> <note>original: "acrochordones." Derived from the Greek for "hanging strings," describing skin tags or warts.</note>, <term>satyriasis</term> <note>original: "satyriasmi." In children, this likely refers to genital irritation or inflammatory swellings rather than the adult meaning of hypersexuality.</note>, <term>scrofula</term> <note>original: "strumæ." Swelling of the lymph nodes, often associated with tuberculosis of the neck.</note>, and other swellings, but especially the conditions mentioned before.

# BOOK III.

Various skin textures, <term>skin discolorations</term> <note>original: "vitiligo." A condition where the skin loses its pigment cells.</note>, <term>scaly eruptions</term> <note>original: "lichenes." Refers to various papular or crusting skin diseases.</note>, and finally, pain in the joints and many small swellings.

### X X I.

->In the summer, some of these same conditions occur,<-
along with continuous fever and that which burns more vehemently, fevers which return every third or fourth day <note>Known as tertian and quartan fevers, often associated with malaria in the ancient world.</note>, ulcers in the mouth, <term>inflamed eyes</term> <note>original: "Opthalmia."</note>, ear pain, rotting of the genitals, sweats, vomiting, and <term>diarrhea</term> <note>original: "profluvia ventris."</note>.

### X X I I.

->In autumn many of the summer ailments persist, as well as<-
<term>twisted bowels</term> <note>original: "volvulus." A painful and dangerous obstruction caused by the twisting of the intestines.</note>, <term>dropsy</term> <note>original: "hydrops." Generalized swelling caused by fluid retention.</note>, quartan and irregular fevers, tumors of the spleen, <term>cramping</term> <note>original: "tormina." Specifically sharp, griping pains in the intestines.</note>, difficult urination, <term>loose bowels</term> <note>original: "laevior alvus." Refers to lientery, where food passes through the body undigested.</note>, pain in the hips, <term>sore throat</term> <note>original: "angina."</note>, <term>epilepsy</term> <note>original: "epilepsia."</note>, <term>consumption</term> <note>original: "tabes." A gradual wasting away of the body, often from tuberculosis.</note>, asthma, <term>melancholy</term> <note>original: "melancholiae." Traditionally believed to be caused by an excess of "black bile," prevalent in autumn.</note>, and even madness of the mind.

### X X I I I.

->But in the time of the winter solstice, these diseases occur:<-
Inflammation of the lungs, hoarseness and heaviness of the <term>pleura</term> <note>original: "pleurae." The membrane surrounding the lungs; this refers to the symptoms of pleurisy.</note>, <term>lethargy</term> <note>original: "lethargus." A state of morbid drowsiness or coma rather than simple tiredness.</note>, dizziness of the head, <term>apoplexy</term> <note>original: "apoplexia." A sudden loss of consciousness, similar to a stroke.</note>, coughing, and pains of the chest, the loins, and the brain.

### X X I V.

->Through the various ages, these happen to the small and the newly born:<-
Inflammation of the navel, coughing, creeping ulcers of the mouth, lack of sleep, moisture in the ears, <term>twitches</term> <note>original: "subsultus." Sudden involuntary jerks or starts.</note>, and night terrors.

### X X V.

->But when the tender teeth begin to break through:<-
Itching of the gums, convulsions, fever, and liquid discharges of the bowels are common, especially when the <term>canine teeth</term> <note>original: "dentes canini."</note> begin to emerge, particularly in heavy children and those who are usually constipated.

### X X V I.

->For those who are slightly more advanced in age:<-
Inward dislocation of the <term>neck vertebrae</term> <note>original: "vertebrae occipitis." Specifically the joint at the base of the skull.</note>, worms, hanging warts, <term>swollen tonsils</term> <note>original: "paristmia." Inflammation of the glands in the throat.</note>, <term>bladder stones</term> <note>original: "lythiasis."</note>, asthma, <term>persistent erections</term> <note>original: "priapismi."</note>, <term>scrofula</term> <note>original: "strumae." Swelling of the lymph nodes in the neck.</note>, and many other various conditions, though the aforementioned swellings occur most of all.

# APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES

->XXVII.<-

*Furthermore, for those more advanced in age and those who have progressed to puberty, many of these same conditions occur, as well as more lingering fevers and <term>nosebleeds</term> <note>original: "sanguinis ex naribus profluvia."</note>.*

->XXVIII.<-

*For children, the majority of diseases reach a crisis <note>A "crisis" in ancient medicine was the turning point of a disease where the patient either began to recover or took a turn for the worse.</note>, some within forty days, others within seven months, some within seven years, and others as they approach puberty itself. However, those diseases which persist and are not resolved in boys around the time of puberty, or in girls when menstruation begins, usually become <term>chronic</term> <note>original: "inveterascere." Literally, to grow old or become deep-rooted.</note>.*

->XXIX.<-

*In young people, there occur the coughing up of blood, <term>wasting disease</term> <note>original: "tabes." Often referring to pulmonary tuberculosis or similar progressive physical decline.</note>, acute fevers, <term>epilepsy</term> <note>original: "epilepsia."</note>, and other diseases, but especially those just mentioned.*

->XXX.<-

*For those advanced beyond this age, there occur <term>asthma</term> <note>original: "asthmata."</note>, <term>pleurisy</term> <note>original: "pleuritides." Inflammation of the membranes around the lungs.</note>, <term>pneumonia</term> <note>original: "peripneumoniæ."</note>, <term>lethargy</term> <note>original: "lethargi." A state of pathological sleepiness or coma.</note>, <term>brain fever</term> <note>original: "phrenitides." Delirium associated with fever.</note>, burning fevers, long-lasting diarrhea, <term>cholera</term> <note>original: "choleræ." In this period, usually referring to severe vomiting and diarrhea rather than the specific modern bacterial infection.</note>, <term>dysentery</term> <note>original: "dysenteria."</note>, <term>lientery</term> <note>original: "lienteriæ." A form of diarrhea where food passes through the body undigested.</note>, and <term>hemorrhoids</term> <note>original: "hæmorrhoides."</note>.*

->XXXI.<-

*But in the elderly, there occur difficulty breathing, <term>catarrhs</term> <note>original: "destillationes." Excessive discharge or drainage from the mucous membranes.</note> that cause coughing, <term>painful urination</term> <note>original: "stranguriæ."</note>, difficulty in passing urine, pain in the joints and kidneys, dizziness, <term>apoplexy</term> <note>original: "apoplexiæ." A sudden loss of consciousness or paralysis, often a stroke.</note>, <term>poor bodily condition</term> <note>original: "malus corporis habitus." Known in Greek as cachexia.</note>, itching of the body, sleeplessness, discharges from the bowels, eyes, and ears, dullness of sight, <term>cataracts</term> <note>original: "glaucedines." A clouding of the lens of the eye.</note>, and hardness of hearing.*

->THE END OF THE THIRD BOOK.<-

# BOOK III.

B 5

->XXVII.<-

*But for those now entering puberty, many of the previously mentioned ailments occur, as well as lingering fevers and a flow of blood from the nostrils <note>original: "fluxusque è nare cruoris."</note>.*

->XXVIII.<-

*In children, diseases are resolved—without taking mastery of the body—either within forty days, or within seven months, or within seven years, or near the onset of puberty. However, those diseases that persist and have not ceased in a woman around her first menstruation <note>original: "menstrua prima."</note>, or in a boy by the time he has reached puberty, usually tend to become <term>chronic</term> <note>original: "veterascere." To grow old, become deep-rooted, or settle permanently into the constitution.</note>.*

->XXIX.<-

*But when they have become youths, there occur acute fevers, the spitting of blood <note>original: "sanguineum sputum."</note>, <term>wasting disease</term> <note>original: "tabes." Generally refers to consumption or a progressive physical decay, often associated with pulmonary tuberculosis.</note>, <term>epilepsy</term> <note>original: "epilepsia."</note>, and various other diseases, the greatest part of which have already been mentioned.*

->XXX.<-

*For those advanced beyond this age <note>Referring here to adulthood or the "prime" of life.</note>, there occur <term>asthma</term> <note>original: "asthmata."</note>, inflammations <note>original: "phlegmone."</note> in the <term>pleura</term> <note>The membrane surrounding the lungs.</note>, the lungs, and the brain, as well as <term>lethargy</term> <note>original: "lethargi." A state of pathological sleepiness or coma-like stupor.</note>, long-lasting flows of the bowels, <term>colic</term> <note>original: "tormina." Gripping, twisting pains in the intestines.</note> with <term>cholera</term> <note>original: "cholera." In the Hippocratic sense, this refers to a violent gastrointestinal upset involving the discharge of bile, rather than the modern bacterial infection.</note>, the passing of undigested food <note>original: "crudi dejectio pastûs." Known in medicine as lientery.</note>, burning fevers, and <term>hemorrhoidal</term> discharges.*

->XXXI.<-

*In the elderly, however, there occur <term>vertigo</term> <note>original: "vertigo." Dizziness or the sensation of spinning.</note>, <term>apoplexy</term> <note>original: "apoplexia." A sudden loss of consciousness or paralysis, similar to what we call a stroke.</note>, <term>rheum</term> <note>original: "rheumata." A discharge of watery fluids, typically from the head or lungs.</note> with coughing, pain in the joints, <term>kidney disease</term> <note>original: "nephritis."</note>, asthma, <term>difficulty urinating</term> <note>original: "dysuriae."</note>, a trickling of hot urine, itching <note>original: "pruritus."</note>, a poor state of the body <note>original: "foedíque habitus." Referring to a general physical decline or cachexia.</note>, infrequent sleep, as well as moisture of the eyes, bowels, and ears, dullness of hearing, <term>glaucoma</term> <note>original: "glaucoma."</note>, and a weakening of the sight.*

->THE END OF THE THIRD BOOK.<-

<meta>This page begins Book IV of the Aphorisms. The previous page concluded Book III, which categorized diseases by the stages of human life, from childhood through old age. This new section shifts focus toward the administration of purgatives—medicines used to cleanse the body of harmful fluids—and how their use varies by season, physical constitution, and specific ailments.</meta>

THE APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES,

## BOOK FOUR.

->I.<-

For pregnant women <note>original: "uterum gerentibus," literally "those carrying a womb."</note>, purgative medicines should be administered—if the <term>humor</term> <note>original: "humor." In ancient medicine, the four vital fluids (blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile) whose balance was essential for health.</note> is driven by its own impulse toward excretion—from the fourth month until the seventh, though less so in the latter. In cases where the fetus is quite small or quite large, one must proceed with great caution.

->II.<-

Through the use of purgative medicines, such things should be drawn out of the body as would be beneficial if they were to come out of their own accord. However, those things that cause distress when they exit should be restrained.

->III.<-

If the substances that ought to be purged are indeed purged, it is beneficial and the patient bears it easily; but if the opposite happens, it is borne with great difficulty.

->IV.<-

In the summer, purge the upper bellies <note>Meaning the stomach, usually via induced vomiting.</note> by medicine; in the winter, however, purge the lower bellies <note>Meaning the intestines, via laxatives.</note>.

->V.<-

Near the rising of the <term>Dog Star</term> <note>original: "Canis ortum." Referring to Sirius; the "dog days" of midsummer were considered a dangerous time for strong medical treatments due to the extreme heat.</note>, and just before its rising, purgations by medicine are troublesome.

->VI.<-

Slender people and those who vomit easily should be purged through the upper parts <note>i.e., by emetics.</note>, unless it is winter.

->VII.<-

Those who vomit with difficulty and are moderately fleshy—whom the Greeks call <term>eusarcos</term> <note>original: "eusarcos" (εὔσαρκος). Meaning well-fleshed or having a healthy, robust muscular build.</note>—should be purged from below, while avoiding the summer season.

->VIII.<-

Do not purge those susceptible to <term>phthisis</term> <note>original: "phthisi." A progressive wasting disease, typically what we now know as pulmonary tuberculosis.</note> from above.

->IX.<-

You should purge those of a <term>melancholic</term> <note>original: "melancholicos." Those believed to have an excess of black bile, a condition linked to both physical illness and a despondent temperament.</note> disposition more vigorously from below, following the same principle by choosing the opposite path for evacuation <note>The "opposite path" implies that since their "humors" tend to settle or cause issues internally, they must be drawn out through the bowels rather than the stomach.</note>.

->X.<-

In very acute cases, if the humor is driven toward excretion by its own momentum, a purgative medicine should be used on that same day; for in such cases, delay is harmful.

<meta>catchword: XI. Those who</meta>

<meta>This page continues the transition into Book IV of the Aphorisms. The previous page established that Book III concluded the discussion of diseases linked to the ages of man. This page initiates the fourth book, which focuses on the principles of "evacuation"—the use of purgatives and emetics to cleanse the body of harmful fluids (humors) according to the patient's condition, the season, and the specific disease.</meta>

<header>BOOK IV.</header>

## BOOK FOUR.

### ->I.<-
Administer purgative medicines to pregnant women—if the <term>humors</term> <note>original: "humor." The four vital fluids: blood, phlegm, yellow bile, and black bile.</note> are active and swelling—from the fourth until the seventh month, though less so than for other patients. However, when the infant is quite small or quite large, it is necessary to proceed with great caution.

### ->II.<-
It is appropriate to draw out through a purgative such substances as would be beneficial if they were to exit the body of their own accord; however, you should restrain the excretion of those things that cause distress when they are expelled.

### ->III.<-
If the substances are purged in the proper manner, it is beneficial and the sick bear it without great effort; but if the opposite occurs, the patient suffers.

### ->IV.<-
The lower belly <note>The intestines; referring to the use of laxatives.</note> should be purged during the time of winter; however, it is better to purge the upper belly <note>The stomach; referring to the use of emetics to induce vomiting.</note> during the summer.

### ->V.<-
During the rising of the <term>Dog Star</term> <note>original: "Canem." Sirius, the Dog Star; its rising in mid-July marked the "dog days," the hottest and most dangerous part of the year for strong medical treatments.</note>, and just before its rising, medicinal purging is usually troublesome.

### ->VI.<-
Those who are slender of body and who vomit with very little effort should be purged upwards <note>By vomiting.</note>, unless it is the peak of winter.

### ->VII.<-
Conversely, those who are moderately fleshy and who vomit with difficulty should be emptied downwards <note>By the bowels.</note>, unless it is summer.

### ->VIII.<-
Beware of purging those prone to <term>consumption</term> <note>original: "tabem." A wasting disease, historically associated with tuberculosis.</note> in an upward direction.

### ->IX.<-
One may purge <term>melancholics</term> <note>original: "melancholicos." Those believed to have an excess of black bile, often associated with depression or specific physical ailments.</note> more vigorously in a downward direction, thereby following the opposite path for evacuation <note>The theory here is to draw the humors away from the upper body/head by purging through the bowels.</note>.

### ->X.<-
In acute diseases, if the <term>humor</term> is active and swelling, purge immediately; indeed, to delay brings about complications.

<meta>catchword: XI. In-</meta>

It emphasizes that medical intervention should mimic natural bodily processes and be adjusted based on a patient's physical build and the time of year.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Book IV of the Aphorisms, moving from general advice on the timing of purging to specific diagnostic signs and the use of hellebore. While the previous page ended at Aphorism VI, this page begins with Aphorism XI; the intervening points (VII-X) are omitted in this specific transcription but would have continued the discussion on the physical effects of evacuation.</meta>

<header>APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES</header>

### X I.

*For those whose intestines are twisted, and who feel distress around the navel and pain in the lower back—pains which cannot be relieved by purging medicine or any other means—this condition will settle into a <term>dry dropsy</term> <note>original: "siccum hydropem." A condition where the belly swells with air rather than fluid, similar to what we might call bloating or tympanites today.</note>.*

### X I I.

*Those who are troubled by a <term>slipperiness of the intestines</term> <note>original: "alvi intestinorum lævitate." Referring to lientery, a type of diarrhea where food passes through the body so quickly it remains undigested.</note> should preferably be purged by emetics <note>original: "per superiora," meaning "through the upper parts" or induced vomiting.</note> during the winter season.*

### X I I I.

*For those who are not easily purged through the upper parts after taking <term>hellebore</term> <note>original: "veratro." White hellebore was a potent and dangerous plant used in antiquity to induce vomiting. Because of its toxicity, the physician here recommends preparing the body first.</note>, their bodies should be pre-moistened with more abundant food and rest before the dose is taken.*

### X I V.

*When someone has taken a drink of hellebore, movement of the body is more suitable than sleep and rest. For even the motion of sailing shows that movement disturbs the body <note>Hippocrates is observing that "sea-sickness" (motion) helps trigger the vomiting reflex, which makes the medicine more effective.</note>.*

### X V.

*When you wish the hellebore to act more strongly, move the body; but when you wish to stop its action, encourage sleep and do not move.*

### X V I.

*Hellebore is dangerous for those with a healthy body, for it induces <term>convulsions</term> <note>original: "convulsionem." Spasms or seizures. The ancient view was that if the medicine had no "bad humors" to work on, it would attack the healthy nerves and muscles instead.</note>.*

### X V I I.

*If someone without a fever experiences a loathing for food, a biting pain at the mouth of the stomach, a darkening dizziness <note>original: "tenebrosa vertigo."</note>, and a bitter taste in the mouth, these signs indicate that a purging through the upper parts is necessary.*

### X V I I I.

*Pains located above the <term>diaphragm</term> <note>original: "septum transversum." The muscle separating the chest from the abdomen.</note> indicate that a medicine which purges upwards is needed. However, those pains located below the diaphragm require a medicine that purges downwards.*

### X I X.

*Those who do not become thirsty while they are being purged by a medicinal drink should not stop the purging process until they have become thirsty <note>Thirst was viewed as a sign that the medicine had successfully drawn out the excess fluids or humors from the tissues.</note>.*

### X X.

*If a twisting pain in the intestines, a heaviness of the knees, and pain in the lower back are present without a fever, they signify that there is a need for medicine that purges downwards.*

<meta>This page continues Book IV of the Aphorisms. The previous page discussed the general timing of purging (cleansing the body of humors) and warned against purging during the peak of an illness. This page moves into specific diagnostic signs that indicate whether a patient should be purged from above (vomiting) or below (bowel movement), and focuses heavily on the use of hellebore, a potent medicinal plant.</meta>

<header>B O O K  I V.</header>

## X I.
For those whose intestines are twisted, and who suffer distress around the navel and pains in the lower back—pains which cannot be relieved by purging or any other means—this condition eventually hardens into <term>dry dropsy</term> <note>original: "hydropa morbus." Also known as tympanites; a condition where the abdomen swells with air rather than fluid, making it sound like a drum when tapped.</note>.

## X I I.
Winter prevents those suffering from <term>lientery</term> <note>original: "lientericos." A form of diarrhea where food passes through the body undigested because the "slippery" intestines cannot grip it.</note> from being purged from above <note>original: "purgare superne," referring to induced vomiting.</note>.

## X I I I.
For those who are purged from above by taking <term>hellebore</term> <note>original: "veratro." Specifically white hellebore (*Veratrum album*), a powerful and potentially toxic plant used by ancient physicians to induce violent vomiting to clear "black bile."</note> not without great effort, it is proper to first moisten their bodies with abundant food and plenty of sleep before the dose is given.

## X I V.
Once a dose of hellebore has been swallowed, movement of the body is more beneficial than sleep or rest; indeed, the way a person sailing upon the sea <note>original: "navigat aequor."</note> becomes ill provides proof that the body is disturbed by motion.

## X V.
If you desire to greatly accelerate the power of the hellebore, keep the body active; but if your intent is to stop its effects, induce sleep and take care not to move the body.

## X I I.
In healthy people, hellebore is usually not without grave danger, for it produces <term>convulsions</term> <note>original: "spasmum."</note> in them.

## X V I I.
If anyone should experience pains at the pit of the heart <note>original: "cordis dolores." In ancient medicine, this often referred to the *cardia* or the upper opening of the stomach rather than the heart itself.</note> without a fever, or suffers from nausea, dark vertigo <note>original: "vertigo tenebrosa," likely referring to "blacking out" or dizzy spells accompanied by darkened vision.</note>, and a bitterness in the mouth, these signs indicate that the person needs to be purged from above.

## X V I I I.
If a pain requiring purging is present above the <term>diaphragm</term> <note>original: "diaphragma." The muscular wall separating the chest from the abdomen.</note>, it denotes that the purging should be done from above (vomiting); if, however, the pain is below the diaphragm, it should be done from below (the bowels).

## X I X.
Those who do not feel thirsty while the medicinal drink is purging their bodies will continue to be purged until they finally do feel thirst.

## X X.
If there is a heaviness in the knees and a twisting in the belly, along with pains in the lower back without a fever, these signs teach that it is necessary to use purges that act from below.

<summary>This page outlines diagnostic rules for purging the body, specifying that symptoms above the diaphragm require emetics while those below require laxatives. It also provides detailed instructions on managing the effects of hellebore, a dangerous but common ancient remedy.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Book IV of Hippocrates' Aphorisms. The previous page discussed the preparation for purging treatments, specifically the use of hellebore to induce vomiting, and how motion can assist in the action of the medicine. This page focuses on the diagnostic and prognostic meaning of various bodily discharges, particularly "black bile" and blood, and how the timing of fevers affects the patient's "crisis" or turning point.</meta>

<header>THE APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES</header>

### X X I.

*Black bowel movements, resembling dark blood, occurring spontaneously—whether with a fever or without one—are a very bad sign. The more varied and foul the colors of these discharges are, the worse the condition. However, if such matter is expelled due to a dose of medicine, it is a better sign, especially if the discharge shows many different colors.* <note>original: "Excrementa alvi nigra." The ancient Greeks viewed the spontaneous passage of black, tarry stools (melena) as a sign that the body’s internal systems were failing or that "black bile" had become dangerously concentrated.</note>

### X X I I.

*In the early stages of any disease, if <term>black bile</term> <note>original: "atra bilis." One of the four vital fluids or "humors" in ancient medicine. While yellow bile was associated with heat, black bile was associated with cold and dryness; its presence in waste was often considered a sign of a terminal illness.</note> is expelled from either above <note>via vomiting</note> or below <note>via the bowels</note>, it is a deadly sign.*

### X X I I I.

*For those who have been wasted away by acute or long-lasting diseases, or by wounds, or by any other cause, if they pass black bile or something resembling black blood, they will die on the following day.*

### X X I V.

*<term>Intestinal distress</term> <note>original: "intestinorum difficultas." Historically, this refers to dysentery or severe inflammation of the bowels accompanied by painful straining.</note> is fatal if it has its origin in black bile.*

### X X V.

*Blood expelled from above, of whatever kind it may be, is a bad sign; but blood passed from below is a good sign if it is black.* <note>The ancient physician likely interpreted black blood from the bowels as "cooked" or "processed" waste being successfully purged, whereas blood from the mouth suggested a fresh, active rupture of a vessel.</note>

### X X I V I.

*If someone suffering from intestinal distress passes what looks like small pieces of flesh, it is a fatal sign.* <note>original: "carunculae." These "little bits of flesh" were likely segments of the intestinal lining being shed during severe infection.</note>

### X X V I I.

*Those who experience a great flow of blood from any part of the body during the course of a fever will find that their bowels become loose and moist as they begin to recover.*

### X X V I I I.

*In those who have bilious discharges, the condition ceases if deafness occurs; and for those who are suffering from deafness, the condition is ended when bilious discharges begin.* <note>This reflects the Hippocratic theory of "metastasis," where a disease-causing humor moves from one part of the body (the gut) to another (the head).</note>

### X X I X.

*In cases of fever, if shivers or chills occur on the sixth day, the <term>judgment</term> <note>original: "judicationem." From the Greek "krisis," the critical point in a disease where the patient either moves toward recovery or toward death.</note> of the illness will be difficult.*

### X X X.

*For those who suffer paroxysms <note>Sudden recurrences or intensifications of symptoms.</note>, if the fever returns the next day at the exact same hour it left them the day before, the crisis of the disease is usually difficult to resolve.*

<meta>catchword: XXXI. Qui- (He who...)</meta>

<summary>This page lists several grim prognostic signs related to "black bile" and blood in waste, identifying them as indicators of near-certain death. It also introduces the concept of the "crisis" in fever, where the timing of symptoms determines the difficulty of recovery.</summary>
<keywords>black bile, dysentery, prognosis, fever, crisis, Hippocratic medicine, humors, melena, intestinal distress</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Book of the Aphorisms of Hippocrates. While the previous section focused on the mechanics of purging the body using hellebore and the physical movements required to aid medicine, this section transitions into specific diagnostic observations. It details the prognostic significance of various bodily discharges—specifically black bile, blood, and tissue—and the timing of fevers in relation to a patient's recovery or death.</meta>

<header>BOOK IV.</header>

### X X I.

*Black bowel movements, resembling dark, decaying blood, which occur spontaneously—whether accompanied by a fever or occurring without one—are a very bad sign. These should be considered even worse the more varied and foul the colors of the discharge appear. However, it is better when such matter is cleared from the body by a dose of medicine; in that case, a variety of colors in the discharge is a more favorable sign.* <note>original: "Nigra excreta alvi." In ancient Greek medicine, "black" discharges were often associated with "black bile," a humor thought to cause severe illness if it became corrupted or "burnt."</note>

### X X II.

*If <term>black bile</term> <note>original: "bilis atra." One of the four essential humors; an excess was thought to cause melancholy or deathly illness.</note> is expelled at the very beginning of a disease, whether it comes from above [by vomiting] or from below [through the bowels], it is a deadly sign.*

### X X I I I.

*For those whose bodies have been wasted away by long-lasting or acute diseases, by wounds, or by any other causes, if they then pass black bile or something resembling black blood, they will die on the following day.*

### X X I V.

*<term>Griping pains</term> <note>original: "tormina." This refers to the severe, twisting abdominal cramps associated with dysentery or internal inflammation.</note> that are produced by black bile are fatal.*

### X X V.

*Any kind of blood that is discharged from above [the stomach] is a bad sign; however, blood discharged from below [the bowels] is a good sign, provided it is black.* <note>The Hippocratic school often viewed the passage of dark blood from the bowels as a "crisis" or a successful purging of harmful humors from the body, whereas blood from the upper tract suggested internal rupture.</note>

### X X V I.

*If a patient suffering from <term>dysentery</term> <note>original: "difficilis patitur profluvia ventris," literally a "difficult flow of the belly."</note> passes what looks like small pieces of flesh, it is a deadly sign.*

### X X V I I.

*In cases of fever where there has been a great loss of blood from any part of the body, the bowels will become looser and more moist as the patient begins to recover.*

### X X V I I I.

*The discharge of bilious stools ceases if <term>deafness</term> <note>original: "surditie." The Hippocratic writers often noted strange correlations between the senses and the digestive system; here, the onset of deafness is seen as a "metastasis," where the disease moves from one part of the body to another.</note> occurs; conversely, if the bilious discharge returns, the deafness is relieved.*

### X X I X.

*In cases of fever, if shivering fits occur on the sixth day, the <term>crisis</term> <note>original: "judicium." In medical Latin, this refers to the 'judging' or turning point of a disease, where the patient will either begin to recover or decline toward death.</note> of the illness is usually very difficult and troublesome.*

### X X X.

*For those who suffer from recurring fever fits: if the fever returns at the exact same hour it previously subsided, the crisis of that illness will be difficult.*

<summary>This page lists several grave medical omens, focusing on the presence of black bile and blood in waste as indicators of mortality. It also establishes the "crisis" or turning point of fevers based on the timing of chills and the recurrence of symptoms.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Book of the Aphorisms of Hippocrates. Following the previous page’s warnings about the deadly nature of black bile and internal "griping" pains, the text now shifts toward physical indicators and "crises"—specific turning points in an illness—marked by abscesses, sweats, and changes in body temperature.</meta>

# APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES

->XXXI.<-

*For those who feel a sense of exhaustion during fevers, <term>abscesses</term> <note>original: "abscessus." In ancient medicine, an abscess was seen as the body's attempt to "cook" or "collect" diseased humors into a single spot to expel them.</note> will arise at the joints, and most especially around the jaws.*

->XXXII.<-

*For those recovering from a disease, if a certain part of the body begins to ache, an abscess will form in that location.*

->XXXIII.<-

*But if any part of the body was painful before the illness began, the disease will take up its seat there.*

->XXXIV.<-

*If a person suffering from a fever suddenly experiences <term>strangulation</term> <note>original: "strangulatio." This refers to a sudden sensation of choking or airway obstruction without an obvious physical cause like a tumor.</note>, even though there is no visible swelling in the throat, it is a fatal sign.*

->XXXV.<-

*In one suffering from a fever, if the neck is suddenly twisted and the patient can barely swallow, yet there is no swelling, it is a fatal sign.*

->XXXVI.<-

*Sweats in feverish patients are a good sign if they begin on the third, fifth, seventh, ninth, eleventh, fourteenth, seventeenth, twenty-seventh, thirty-first, or thirty-fourth days; for these sweats <term>bring the disease to a crisis</term> <note>original: "judicant." This refers to the medical theory of "Critical Days," specific intervals when the body was expected to either expel the disease or succumb to it.</note>. Sweats that do not occur on these days indicate hardship, a long duration of the illness, and the likelihood of a relapse.*

->XXXVII.<-

*Cold sweats occurring during an acute fever signify death; however, in a milder fever, they signify that the illness will be prolonged.*

->XXXVIII.<-

*And wherever there is sweat on the body, it announces that the disease is located there.*

->XXXIX.<-

*And in whichever part of the body there is heat or cold, the disease is present there.*

->XL.<-

*And when changes occur throughout the whole body—such as if the body becomes cold and then hot again, or if the skin color changes from one hue to another—it signifies that the illness will be long.*

->XLI.<-

*A heavy sweat occurring during sleep indicates that the body has been supplied with too much food. But if this happens to one who has eaten sparingly, it shows that the body requires <term>evacuation</term> <note>original: "vacuatione indigere." This suggests the body needs to be purged of excess humors through medical intervention, such as bloodletting or a laxative.</note>.*

<meta>catchword: XLII. Su-</meta>

<summary>This page lists diagnostic signs for fevers, focusing on the formation of abscesses at the joints and the significance of "critical days" for sweating. It also details fatal symptoms, such as sudden choking or a twisted neck, and interprets body temperature fluctuations as signs of a long illness.</summary>
<keywords>Hippocrates, Aphorisms, critical days, fever, abscesses, sweating, diagnostic signs, medical prognosis, humoral theory</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Book of the Aphorisms of Hippocrates. Following the previous page’s warnings about the deadly nature of black bile and internal "griping" pains, the text now shifts toward physical indicators and "crises"—specific turning points in an illness—marked by abscesses, sweats, and changes in body temperature.</meta>

# BOOK IV.

### ->XXXI.<-

In those who are exhausted by a fever, <term>abscesses</term> <note>original: "abscessus." In ancient medicine, an abscess or "aposteme" was viewed as a collection of harmful humors that the body was attempting to expel or "cook" at a specific site.</note> occur at the joints, and most especially near the jaw-bones.

### ->XXXII.<-

If a person recovering from a disease suffers pain in any particular part, it is in that very part where the pain is felt that an <term>abscess</term> <note>original: "apostemata." This is a synonym for abscess, indicating a localized swelling or gathering of fluid.</note> will form.

### ->XXXIII.<-

But if a certain part was painful before the illness began, the disease will take up its seat in that same location.

### ->XXXIV.<-

If, during a fever, breathing is suddenly suppressed while no swelling is visible in the throat, it is a <term>fatal sign</term> <note>original: "perniciosum." In a clinical context, this implies the condition is likely to lead to death.</note>.

### ->XXXV.<-

In a feverish patient, if the neck is suddenly twisted and they are barely able to swallow, yet no swelling is present, it is a fatal sign.

### ->XXXVI.<-

Sweats are beneficial for those suffering from fevers if they begin to flow on the third, fifth, seventh, ninth, eleventh, fourteenth, seventeenth, twenty-first <note>original: "quarto à septemdecimo," literally the fourth day from the seventeenth.</note>, twenty-seventh <note>original: "decimóve sequenti," the tenth day following the seventeenth.</note>, thirty-first, or finally the thirty-fourth day. For these sweats are accustomed to <term>resolve the disease</term> <note>original: "solvere morbos." This refers to a "crisis," where the body successfully expels the cause of the illness.</note>; those occurring on other days usually predict great struggle, a relapse, or a long-lasting illness.

### ->XXXVII.<-

A cold sweat occurring in acute fevers signifies death; however, in milder fevers, it predicts a long-lasting disease.

### ->XXXVIII.<-

In whatever part of the body the sweat flows, the disease is located in that same part.

### ->XXXIX.<-

And in whatever part of the body there is unusual heat or cold, the disease is located in that same part.

### ->XL.<-

Furthermore, where changes happen throughout the entire body—as when the body grows cold and then becomes hot again, or the complexion changes—these are the <term>omens</term> <note>original: "præsagia," or signs that allow a physician to predict the future course of the illness.</note> of a long-lasting disease.

### ->XLI.<-

If a great deal of sweat flows from the body during sleep, it signifies that the person is being nourished by too much food; but if this happens while they are eating very little, it signifies that the body has a need to be <term>purged</term> <note>original: "vacuare," likely referring to medical evacuation such as bloodletting or the use of laxatives.</note>.

<meta>catchword: XLII. Im-</meta>

<summary>This page details the prognostic signs of fevers, specifically how the location of pain, the timing of sweats (critical days), and sudden throat symptoms can predict whether a patient will recover or perish.</summary>
<keywords>Hippocratic aphorisms, critical days, abscesses, prognosis, sweat in fever, diagnostic signs, ancient medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Book of the Aphorisms of Hippocrates. Following the previous page’s discussion on the timing and benefits of sweats during a fever, the text now details the specific qualities of those sweats and moves into the grave prognostic signs of "continuous" fevers—those which do not pause or remit—and the physical distortions that signal approaching death.</meta>

# HIPPOCRATIC APHORISMS.

### X L I I.

*Profuse sweat, whether hot or cold, that flows continuously; a cold sweat signifies a more severe disease, while a hot sweat signifies one less severe.*

### X L I I I.

*Fevers that strike every third day <note>This likely refers to "sub-continuous" fevers or a variation of the tertian fever that does not provide the patient with a break.</note> and do not cease are more dangerous. However, if they cease in any manner, it indicates the danger has passed.*

### X L I V.

*In those who suffer from long-lasting fevers, <term>tubercles</term> <note>original: "tubercula." In ancient medicine, this referred to any small, hard swelling or lump on the body, not specifically the modern disease of tuberculosis.</note> or pains in the joints will arise.*

### X L V.

*Those who develop <term>tubercles</term> or joint pains after long-lasting fevers are consuming too much food.*

### X L V I.

*If a shivering fit frequently occurs during a continuous fever when the patient is already weak, it is a <term>fatal sign</term> <note>original: "lethale." Indicating a condition from which the patient is not expected to recover.</note>.*

### X L V I I.

*In continuous fevers, excretions that are leaden-colored, bloody, foul-smelling, or bilious are all bad. However, if they are expelled effectively, they are a good sign, whether through the bowels or the urine. But if something is excreted through these channels that brings no relief, it is a bad sign.*

### X L V I I I.

*In continuous fevers, if the external limbs are cold while the internal parts are burning and tormented by thirst, it is a <term>fatal sign</term>.*

### X L I IX.

*In a continuous fever, if the lip, eyebrow, eye, or nose becomes distorted, or if the patient—with their body already weakened—can no longer see or hear; whatever of these occurs, death is at hand.*

### L.

*When difficulty in breathing and delirium occur during a continuous fever, it is a <term>fatal sign</term>.*

### L I.

*In fevers, <term>abscesses</term> that are not resolved by the first <term>crises</term> <note>original: "judicationibus." This refers to the "days of judgment" or "critical days" in Hippocratic theory, where the body was thought to attempt to expel the disease-causing humors.</note> indicate that the disease will be long-lasting.*

<meta>catchword: LII. Those-</meta>

<summary>This page details clinical observations and prognostic signs for fevers, specifically distinguishing between the severity of hot and cold sweats and identifying the facial distortions and respiratory failures that signal imminent death.</summary>
<keywords>Hippocrates, Aphorisms, fever, prognosis, continuous fever, clinical signs, ancient medicine, critical days</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Book of the Hippocratic Aphorisms. The previous page concluded mid-sentence during a discussion on the dangers of internal heat paired with external coldness (Aphorism XLVIII). This page completes that thought and continues with a sequence of grave prognostic signs—physical indicators of a patient's recovery or decline—specifically focusing on continuous fevers and facial distortions that signal imminent death.</meta>

# BOOK IV.

### XLII.

Excessive sweat that flows constantly from the body indicates a more severe disease if the sweat is cold, but it is usually a sign of a less severe disease if the sweat is hot.

### XLIII.

A tertian fever <note>A fever that occurs every third day (modern "every 48 hours"), typical of certain types of malaria.</note> is not without danger if it continues without interruption; but if it pauses or remits, the danger is considered to have passed.

### XLIV.

If fevers afflict a person for a long time, pains or <term>tubercles</term> <note>original: "tubercula." In ancient medicine, this term referred to any small, hard swelling or lump on the body, not specifically the modern lung disease.</note> will develop around the joints.

### XLV.

When joint pains or swellings arise from a long-lasting fever, you may believe those patients have been too voracious <note>original: "voraces." Suggesting that overeating or poor diet during the illness contributed to the complication.</note> in their eating.

### XLVI.

In a continuous fever, if a shivering fit (rigor) frequently attacks a patient who is already weakened, it must be considered a <term>fatal sign</term> <note>original: "lethale." Indicating a condition from which recovery is unlikely.</note>.

### XLVII.

If at any time during persistent fevers the patient excretes material that is leaden-colored, foul-smelling, or tinged with bile or blood, it is entirely bad. Conversely, it is a good sign if such matter is expelled effectively, whether it happens through the <term>urine</term> <note>original: "lotia."</note> or the <term>bowels</term> <note>original: "alvum."</note>. However, if these excretions occur but do not bring relief to the patient, it is a bad sign.

### XLVIII.

In a continuous fever, if the external limbs are cold while the internal parts are burning and thirsty, it is a pernicious <note>Deadly or highly destructive.</note> condition.

### XLIX.

In a continuous fever, if the eyes, the lip, the eyebrow, or the nose of the patient becomes distorted; or if the patient does not see or hear properly—whatever of these occurs, the patient's strength is now broken and death is imminent. <note>These facial distortions are often referred to as the "Hippocratic Face" (*facies Hippocratica*), a classic medical sign of approaching death.</note>

### L.

In a continuous fever, if the feverish person breathes with difficulty and becomes delirious, it must be held as a fatal sign.

### LI.

<term>Abscesses</term> <note>In this context, a "settling" or collection of humors in a specific part of the body.</note> appearing during a fever, which have not been fully resolved by previous <term>crises</term> <note>original: "crises." The turning point of a disease where the patient either recovers or declines.</note>, are omens of a long-lasting illness.

<summary>This page lists specific physical symptoms—such as cold sweats, joint swellings, and facial distortions—that help a physician predict the severity or fatal outcome of continuous fevers. It emphasizes that excretions only signal recovery if they provide the patient with visible relief.</summary>

<keywords>prognosis, continuous fever, Hippocratic face, tubercles, joint pain, fatal signs, crisis, bodily excretions</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Book of the Hippocratic Aphorisms. The previous page (Aphorism XLVII) discussed the grave signs of leaden-colored or bloody excretions in persistent fevers. This page continues the sequence of prognostic observations, detailing specific physical symptoms—from tearing eyes to the appearance of jaundice—that indicate whether a fever will worsen, resolve, or return.</meta>

# THE APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES

### L I I.

In those suffering from fevers or other diseases, if the eyes shed tears voluntarily <note>original: "voluntariè." Referring to tears shed for emotional reasons or with the patient's awareness.</note>, it is not strange; but if they tear up against the patient's will, it is more <term>ill-omened</term> <note>original: "absurdius." In ancient medicine, symptoms occurring without the patient's control were often seen as signs of a deeper physiological crisis.</note>.

### L I I I.

For those in whom a sticky film grows upon the teeth during fevers, the fevers become more violent.

### L I V.

Those who suffer from long-lasting, dry, and slightly irritating coughs during burning fevers are usually not much distressed by thirst.

### L V.

All fevers resulting from a <term>bubo</term> <note>original: "bubone." A swelling of the lymph nodes, especially in the groin or armpit.</note> are dangerous, except for those that are <term>ephemeral</term> <note>original: "ephemeras." Fevers that last only one day.</note>.

### L V I.

If a patient in a fever begins to sweat while the fever persists, it is a bad sign. It indicates that the disease will be prolonged and signifies an excess of moisture <note>original: "humiditatem." In the theory of the four humors, an excess of fluid was often seen as the cause of lingering illness.</note> in the body.

### L V I I.

If a fever comes upon someone who is already suffering from a convulsion or a <term>tension of the nerves</term> <note>original: "distentione nervorum." This likely refers to tetanus or similar muscular spasms.</note>, it resolves the disease <note>original: "morbum solvit." Hippocrates often noted that the high heat of a fever could sometimes "cook" or neutralize the imbalances causing other symptoms.</note>.

### L V I I I.

If a shivering fit (rigor) occurs in someone suffering from a burning fever, a resolution of the illness follows.

### L I X.

A true <term>tertian fever</term> <note>original: "Tertiana exquisita." A fever where the paroxysms occur every 48 hours.</note> is judged <note>original: "iudicatur." Meaning the outcome or "crisis" is reached.</note> in seven cycles at the very most.

### L X.

In those who have become deaf during a fever, the disease is resolved if blood flows from the nostrils or if the bowels become disturbed.

### L X I.

Unless a fever leaves the patient on an <term>odd-numbered day</term> <note>Hippocratic medicine placed great importance on "critical days" (3, 5, 7, 9, etc.), believing that changes occurring on these days were more likely to lead to a permanent recovery.</note>, it is accustomed to return.

### L X I I.

It is a bad sign for those in a fever if <term>jaundice</term> <note>original: "morbus regius." Literally "the royal disease," so named because it was thought to require the expensive remedies of a king, or perhaps due to the golden color.</note> appears before the seventh day.

### L X I I I.

In those fevers where shivering fits repeat on a specific day, the fevers are resolved on that same day.

<meta>catchword: LXIV. He who-</meta>

<summary>This page lists several prognostic signs for fevers, describing how involuntary tearing, dental film, and early jaundice indicate a worsening condition, while shivering or specific "critical days" can signal recovery.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Book of the Hippocratic Aphorisms. The previous page (Aphorism XLVII) discussed the grave signs of leaden-colored or bloody excretions in persistent fevers. This page continues the sequence of prognostic observations, detailing specific physical symptoms—from tearing eyes to the appearance of jaundice—that indicate whether a fever will worsen, resolve, or return.</meta>

# BOOK IV.

### ->L I I.<-

In a fever, or in other diseases, for the eyes to weep voluntarily <note>original: "volenti." Referring to tears shed with the patient's awareness or for emotional reasons.</note> is not strange; but when the eyes weep against the patient's will, it is seen as more <term>ill-omened</term> <note>original: "absurdum." In ancient medicine, involuntary symptoms were often viewed as a sign that the body's internal systems were losing control.</note>.

### ->L I I I.<-

If, in one laboring under a fever, a sticky film <note>original: "humor viscidus."</note> grows upon the teeth, the fever is becoming more violent.

### ->L I V.<-

In a <term>burning fever</term> <note>original: "causone." A specific type of high, acute fever characterized by intense heat and dryness.</note>, those who suffer for a long time from a dry, slightly irritating cough are not usually afflicted by great thirst.

### ->L V.<-

Fevers arising from a <term>bubo</term> <note>original: "bubone." A painful swelling of the lymph nodes, usually in the groin or armpit.</note> are bad, unless they are <term>ephemeral</term> <note>original: "ephemeræ." Fevers that last only one day.</note>.

### ->L V I.<-

If a sweat breaks out upon a person with a fever while the fever still persists, it is a bad sign; for the disease is then made longer, and it indicates a great excess of moisture <note>original: "humiditatem." In Hippocratic theory, an overabundance of fluids or "humors" was believed to prolong illness.</note>.

### ->L V I I.<-

A fever coming upon a <term>convulsion</term> <note>original: "spasmũ."</note> or <term>tetanus</term> <note>original: "tetanũ." In this era, this referred to general muscular rigidity or stiffening of the body.</note> resolves the ailment.

### ->L V I I I.<-

In a burning fever, the onset of a <term>rigor</term> <note>original: "rigor." A fit of shivering or a sudden chill.</note> brings about a cure for the fever.

### ->L I X.<-

A <term>true tertian fever</term> <note>original: "febris exquisita." A fever that returns every other day (on the 1st, 3rd, 5th days, etc.).</note> reaches its end, at the latest, within seven cycles.

### ->L X.<-

If any deafness occurs during a fever, it is cured by a flow from the bowels or by a <term>hemorrhage</term> <note>original: "hæmorrhagiáve." Specifically, a nosebleed was often seen as a "crisis" that could clear the head of disease.</note>.

### ->L X I.<-

A fever that leaves the patient on an <term>odd day</term> <note>original: "imparibus... diebus." Hippocratic medicine placed great importance on "critical days" (the 3rd, 5th, 7th, etc.); if a fever broke on an even day, it was thought to be a false recovery.</note> is likely to return.

### ->L X I I.<-

If <term>jaundice</term> <note>original: "aurigo." From the Latin "aurum" (gold), referring to the yellowing of the skin.</note> appears during a fever before the seventh day, it is a bad sign.

### ->L X I I I.<-

On whatever days the shivering <note>original: "rigor."</note> usually occurs in a fever, the fever is also resolved at that same time.

<meta>catchword: LXIV. If (original: "Si")</meta>

<summary>This page lists several aphorisms regarding the prognosis of fevers, focusing on the significance of involuntary tears, dental films, and the timing of shivering fits. It highlights the Hippocratic belief that certain symptoms, like deafness or spasms, can be resolved by the onset of a fever or a "crisis" such as a nosebleed.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the Fourth Book of the Hippocratic Aphorisms. <header>APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES</header>

# L X I V.

*In those for whom <term>jaundice</term> <note>original: "morbus regius," literally the 'royal disease.' It was called this either because of the golden color or because it was thought to require the expensive, luxurious treatment of a king.</note> appears during a fever on the seventh, ninth, eleventh, or fourteenth day, it is a good sign, provided that the <term>hypochondrium</term> <note>original: "praecordia." This refers to the area of the upper abdomen, specifically below the ribs and above the navel.</note> on the right side does not remain hard; otherwise, it is not a good sign at all.*

# L X V.

*In fevers, a violent heat around the stomach and a "biting" sensation <note>original: "morsus." A sharp, gnawing pain often associated with high acidity or inflammation.</note> at the mouth of the stomach is a bad sign.*

# L X I V I. <note>The Roman numeral in the original text is L X V I.</note>

*In acute fevers, convulsions and violent pains around the internal organs are a bad sign.*

# L X V I I.

*In fevers, terrors during sleep or convulsions are bad signs.*

# L X V I I I.

*In fevers, a labored or "stumbling" breath <note>original: "spiritus offendens." This refers to an irregular, interrupted, or difficult breathing pattern.</note> is bad, for it signifies a coming convulsion.*

# L X I X.

*In patients whose urine is thick, clotted, and scanty during a fever, it is beneficial if the urine becomes thin and flows copiously. This change occurs most frequently in those whose urine showed a sediment <note>In ancient medicine, sediment in the urine was studied carefully as a sign of "concoction," or the body's success in processing disease.</note> immediately from the beginning of the illness, or shortly thereafter.*

# L X X.

*In those for whom the urine remains turbid during a fever—resembling the urine of beasts of burden—a headache is either currently present or will soon occur.*

# L X X I.

*In those for whom the <term>crisis</term> <note>original: "judicatio." The turning point of a disease when it is decided if the patient will recover or perish.</note> occurs on the seventh day, the urine shows a red "cloud" <note>original: "nubeculam." A suspended formation in the urine used for diagnosis.</note> on the fourth day, and other signs follow in a similar proportion.*

# L X T I I. <note>The Roman numeral in the original text is L X X I I.</note>

*Transparent and white urine is a bad sign, especially if it appears in those suffering from <term>phrenitis</term> <note>original: "phrenitice." An ancient term for an acute fever accompanied by delirium or inflammation of the brain.</note>.*

# L X X I I I.

*In those whose upper abdomen is distended and rumbling, followed by pain in the lower back, the bowels will become loose, unless flatulence [passes] downward*

<header>APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES,</header>

*...should burst, or a great quantity of urine should come forth. And these things happen in fevers.*

->L X X I V.<-

*In those where it is expected that some <term>abscess</term> <note>original: "abscessurum"; in Hippocratic medicine, an "abscess" was not just an infection but a "falling away" or shifting of diseased humors to the extremities.</note> will move toward the joints, a copious, thick, and white urine being passed frees the patient from that outbreak; such as begins to occur in some on the fourth day of a fever accompanied by a sense of weariness. But if blood also flows from the nostrils, then a solution to the illness occurs quite shortly thereafter.*

->L X X V.<-

*If blood or pus is voided with the urine, it signifies an <term>ulceration</term> <note>original: "exulceratio"; a sore or open wound.</note> of the kidneys or of the bladder.*

->L X X V I.<-

*In those who pass small pieces of flesh <note>original: "carunculæ"; literally "little bits of meat."</note> or things like hairs along with thick urine, these substances are being excreted from the kidneys.*

->L X X V I I.<-

*In those who pass certain bran-like scales <note>original: "furfurosa"; resembling bran or dandruff.</note> together with thick urine, the bladder is suffering from <term>scabies</term> <note>original: "scabie"; here meaning an irritation, roughness, or scaly condition of the bladder lining.</note>.*

->L X X V I I I.<-

*Those who unexpectedly pass blood in their urine, for them it signifies that a small vein has ruptured in the kidneys.*

->L X X I V.<-

*In those who have sandy <term>sediments</term> <note>original: "subsidentiæ"; the material that settles at the bottom of the vessel.</note> in their urine, the kidneys or the bladder are suffering from a stone.*

->L X X X.<-

*If someone should pour out blood and clots with their urine, and suffer from <term>strangury</term> <note>original: "stranguriam"; a condition where urine is passed drop by drop with pain and straining.</note>, and a pain falls into the lower belly and the <term>perineum</term> <note>original: "interfæmineum"; the region between the thighs.</note>, those parts belonging to the bladder are in distress.*

->L X X X I.<-

*If someone passes blood, or pus, or small scales in the urine, and a heavy odor is present, it signifies an ulceration of the bladder.*

->L X X X I I.<-

*In those for whom a small tumor or lump grows in the <term>urethra</term> <note>original: "fistulâ"; literally a pipe or tube, referring here to the urinary passage.</note>, when that turns into pus and bursts, recovery occurs.*

->L X X X I I I.<-

*A great quantity of urine passed during the night indicates that there will be little evacuation of the bowels.*

->THE END OF THE FOURTH BOOK.<-

One becomes more moist, unless they urinate much, or gas 
breaks out from below; and these symptoms are present in fevers.

->L X X I V.<-

In those for whom an <term>abscess</term> <note>original: "abscessus"; in ancient medicine, this referred to a "falling away" or shifting of diseased humors from the internal organs to the joints or extremities.</note> is expected to form in the joints, a great abundance of thick, white urine frees them from that threat; this is the kind of urine that begins to appear on the fourth day in some who suffer from fever and exhaustion. But if blood also breaks out from the nostrils of the patient, a recovery from the disease usually follows shortly thereafter.

->L X X V.<-

If ever blood or pus is voided through the urine, it signifies <term>ulcerations</term> <note>original: "ulcera"; open sores or lesions within the internal urinary tract.</note> of the bladder or the kidneys.

->L X X V I.<-

If something like a hair, or a small <term>caruncle</term> <note>original: "caruncula"; a small piece of flesh or meat-like tissue.</note>, floats in thick urine, it has come from the kidneys.

->L X X V I I.<-

If any pass what look like <term>bran-scales</term> <note>original: "furfura"; flaky particles resembling dandruff or the outer husks of grain.</note> with their thick urine, their bladder is suffering from a scaly irritation.

->L X X V I I I.<-

For those in whom blood unexpectedly appears in the urine, it must be understood that a small vein in the kidney has ruptured.

->L X X I X.<-

If sand settles to the bottom of the chamber pot for anyone, their kidneys or bladder are suffering from <term>lithiasis</term> <note>original: "Lithiasi"; the formation of stones or calculi in the urinary system.</note>.

->L X X X.<-

If someone should urinate clots of blood and can only pass their urine drop by drop, while experiencing pain in the <term>perineum</term> <note>original: "interfæmineo"; literally the area "between the thighs."</note> and the lower abdomen, those parts situated near the bladder are diseased.

->L X X X I.<-

If scales, blood, or pus come forth in the urine, and a heavy odor is present, it denotes an ulcer of the bladder.

->L X X X I I.<-

If the urinary passage is closed by a <term>tumor</term> <note>original: "tubere"; a swelling or lump.</note>, a resolution of the problem will occur once the growth has suppurated and ruptured.

->L X X X I I I.<-

A great amount of urination at night reduces the frequency of bowel movements.

<header>APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES</header>

## BOOK FIVE.

### I.
A convulsion caused by <term>hellebore</term> <note>original: "elleboro"; a plant used by ancient physicians as a powerful, and often dangerous, purgative to induce vomiting or bowel movements.</note> is deadly.

### II.
A convulsion resulting from a wound is fatal.

### III.
If a convulsion or hiccups occur after a heavy loss of blood, it is a bad sign.

### IV.
A convulsion or hiccups following excessive purging (which they call <term>hypercatharsis</term> <note>original: "hypercatharsim"; a Greek-derived term for an extreme or dangerous over-cleansing of the bowels.</note>) is a bad omen.

### V.
If a drunk person suddenly loses their voice, they will die in convulsions, unless a fever seizes them, or they regain their voice at the hour when the effects of the wine usually wear off.

### VI.
Those seized by <term>tetanus</term> <note>original: "tetano"; a state of severe, rigid muscle tension. Hippocrates observed this often followed injuries.</note> die within four days; but if they survive these, they recover.

### VII.
Those who suffer from the <term>falling sickness</term> <note>original: "Comitiales"; epilepsy. It was known as the 'comitial disease' because if a person had a seizure during a Roman assembly (comitia), the meeting was immediately cancelled as a bad omen.</note> before the age of puberty may find relief from it. But for those in whom it occurs during their twenty-fifth year, it usually stays with them until death.

### VIII.
For those suffering from <term>pleurisy</term> <note>original: "pleuritide"; an inflammation of the membranes surrounding the lungs, causing sharp chest pain.</note>, unless they are cleared out from above <note>That is, through coughing up phlegm (expectoration).</note> within fourteen days, the disease turns into an <term>empyema</term> <note>original: "empyema"; a condition where pus collects in the space between the lungs and the chest wall.</note>.

### IX.
<term>Consumption</term> <note>original: "Tabes"; a gradual wasting away of the body, typically referring to what we now know as pulmonary tuberculosis.</note> chiefly occurs between the ages of eighteen and thirty-five.

### X.
In those for whom a <term>quinsy</term> <note>original: "anginâ"; here referring to a severe, suffocating inflammation of the throat or tonsils.</note> spreads to the lungs, they die within seven days. But if they escape these days, they develop internal abscesses.

<meta>catchword: XI. In</meta>

<header>BOOK V.</header>

# BOOK FIVE.

->I.<-
A convulsion arising from <term>hellebore</term> <note>original: "elleboro"; a plant used as a violent purgative in antiquity, which could cause fatal dehydration or nervous system failure if dosed incorrectly.</note> is to be considered deadly.

->II.<-
A convulsion that follows a wound is fatal.

->III.<-
Hiccups or a convulsion are a bad sign when they occur after a great loss of blood.

->IV.<-
Hiccups or a convulsion denote a bad outcome for the sick if they arise after an immoderate purging of the bowels.

->V.<-
If anyone suddenly loses their voice after drinking wine, they will die in convulsions, unless a fever seizes them, or they recover their voice at the hour when the effects of the <term>drunkenness</term> <note>original: "crapula"; refers to the distress or intoxication caused by excessive wine.</note> pass away.

->VI.<-
Death usually follows <term>tetanus</term> <note>original: "tetano"; a condition of rigid muscle contraction. Hippocrates noted its extreme lethality within the first few days.</note> within four days; if the patients can survive these, they recover.

->VII.<-
The <term>Herculean disease</term> <note>original: "Herculeus morbus"; a name for epilepsy, so-called because of its powerful and "heroic" hold over the patient.</note> is cured in those who have not yet reached puberty; but if it arises after the age of twenty-five, it does not leave the body until the final day of life.

->VIII.<-
In those whose <term>pleura</term> <note>original: "pleura"; the membrane surrounding the lungs.</note> is inflamed, unless the chest is cleared by an upward evacuation <note>meaning the coughing up of phlegm</note> within fourteen days, the <term>inflammation</term> <note>original: "phlegmone"; a localized heat and swelling.</note> usually turns into an abscess.

->IX.<-
<term>Consumption</term> <note>original: "tabes"; a general term for wasting diseases, most often referring to pulmonary tuberculosis.</note> usually occurs for the most part between the eighteenth and the thirty-fifth year of age.

->X.<-
In those for whom the fluid from <term>quinsy</term> <note>original: "angina"; a severe, suffocating throat infection.</note> turns toward the lungs, they depart from the body within seven days; if they survive these, an abscess will form.

<header>THE APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES</header>

->X I.<-

*In those suffering from <term>consumption</term> <note>original: "tabe"; usually referring to a wasting disease like pulmonary tuberculosis.</note>, if the <term>sputum</term> <note>phlegm or mucus from the lungs</note> which they cast up by coughing emits a foul odor when poured onto hot coals, and if the hair falls from the head, it is a fatal sign.*

->X I I.<-

*In those with consumption, if the hair falls from the head, they will die if <term>diarrhea</term> <note>original: "alvi profluvio"; a flowing of the bowels, which in a weakened state was often the final stage of the illness.</note> subsequently sets in.*

->X I I I.<-

*Those who cough up frothy blood are bringing it up from the lungs.*

->X I V.<-

*If diarrhea comes upon someone held by consumption, it is a deadly sign.*

->X V.<-

*Those who develop an <term>empyema</term> <note>original: "empyi"; a collection of pus in the space between the lung and the chest wall.</note> following <term>pleurisy</term> <note>inflammation of the lung membranes</note> are cured if they are cleared by coughing within forty days of the abscess breaking. Otherwise, the condition turns into consumption.*

->X V I.<-

Heat, when used too frequently, brings these harms: softening of the flesh, weakness of the nerves, dullness of the mind, hemorrhages, and fainting, which is followed by death.

->X V I I.<-

Cold, however, brings on convulsions, <term>tetanus</term> <note>rigid muscle spasms</note>, blackening <note>original: "nigrores"; likely referring to gangrene or the darkening of skin from lack of circulation</note>, and feverish shivering.

->X V I I I.<-

Cold is an enemy to the bones, the teeth, the nerves, the brain, and the spinal cord; heat, however, is a friend to them.

->X I X.<-

Parts that have been chilled ought to be warmed, except for those parts which are bleeding or are about to bleed.

->X X.<-

Cold stings ulcers, hardens the skin, causes pain that prevents the formation of pus, and brings on blackening, feverish shivering, convulsions, and tension of the nerves.

->X X I.<-

Sometimes, however, in a case of tension of the nerves without an ulcer,

<meta>This page continues the fifth book of the Aphorisms, moving from respiratory failure and the symptoms of consumption (tuberculosis) to the physiological effects of temperature—specifically how extreme heat and cold affect the human body and various tissues.</meta>

<header>BOOK V.</header>

## XI.
If a person with **consumption** <note>original: *pthyſicus*; a term used historically for those suffering from a wasting disease of the lungs, typically tuberculosis.</note> coughs up **sputum** <note>mucus or phlegm from the lungs</note> that gives off a foul odor when thrown onto hot coals, and the hair of the head falls out, it is a fatal sign.

## XII.
In those with consumption, if the hair falls from the crown of the head, they usually die if a **flowing of the bowels** <note>diarrhea</note> follows.

## XIII.
Those who cough up frothy blood are bringing it up from the lungs.

## XIV.
It is a deadly sign for a person with consumption to be seized by a flowing of the bowels.

## XV.
If those who develop an **empyema** <note>original: *empyi*; a collection of pus in the cavity between the lung and the chest wall</note> after **pleurisy** <note>inflammation of the membranes surrounding the lungs</note> do not clear their lungs within forty days of the abscess breaking, the condition usually turns into a wasting of the body.

## XVI.
Frequent use of heat produces these evils: softening of the flesh, weakness of the nerves, dullness of the mind, heavy hemorrhages, and fainting, after which the final companion is death.
->is.<-

## XVII.
That which is cold produces **tetanus** <note>severe muscle rigidity</note>, spasms, blackening of the skin, and, with frequent use, feverish shivering.

## XVIII.
Cold is harmful to the teeth, the bones, the nerves, the brain, and the spinal marrow; heat, on the contrary, is a friend to them.

## XIX.
Restore heat to those parts that have become cold, unless there is a flow of blood, or one is about to occur.

## XX.
That which is cold induces spasms, tetanus, and feverish shivering; it also hardens the skin, turns it black, and irritates ulcers. It likewise tends to cause pain that does not result in the formation of pus.

## XXI.
In cases of tetanus, however, for those who have no ulcers,
