<meta>This page continues the transition from the end of Aphorism XXI, which discusses the use of cold water to stimulate a heat response in healthy young men during summer, into a detailed comparison of thermal treatments for various ailments.</meta>

<header>APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES</header>

...to help, in a young man of a square-built <note>original: *quadrati corporis*; refers to a sturdy, well-proportioned, or muscular physique</note> body, in the middle of summer, a copious pouring of cold water brings about a return of heat; yet heat is what heals these conditions.

### XXII.

*Heat, when it promotes **suppuration** <note>original: *suppurationem*; the formation or discharge of pus, which ancient doctors often saw as a sign of a wound "ripening" toward healing</note> (though not in every kind of ulcer), provides the greatest sign of safety. It softens and thins the skin, removes pain, and soothes shivering, convulsions, and the tension of the nerves. It resolves heaviness of the head and is particularly beneficial for bone fractures—especially those where the bone is exposed, and most of all for wounds to the head. For parts of the body dying from cold or suffering from ulcers, as well as for spreading **herpes** <note>original: *herpetibus exedentibus*; literally "eating herpes," likely referring to shingles, lupus, or other spreading skin eruptions</note>, and for the anus, the private parts, the womb, and the bladder: in all these cases, heat is welcome and brings about a recovery, while cold is an enemy and is destructive.*

### XXIII.

*Cold should be used in those cases where blood is flowing, or is soon to flow—not applied directly to the site of the wound, but to the areas from which the blood originates. It should also be applied to inflammations or burnings that tend toward a bright red or blood-stained color, where the blood is fresh and active. For if the blood is old, cold will turn it black. Cold also helps **erysipelas** <note>original: *erysipelas*; a bacterial skin infection characterized by a bright red rash, historically called "St. Anthony's Fire"</note> when it is not yet ulcerated, but it is harmful if the skin has already broken.*

### XXIV.

*Cold things, such as snow and ice, are enemies to the chest; they provoke coughing, cause the rupture of veins, and produce **catarrhs** <note>original: *destillationes*; the downward flow of mucus from the head to the respiratory tract</note>.*

### XXV.

*Swelling and pain in the joints without an ulcer, as well as gouty afflictions and sprains—large amounts of cold water poured over these will for the most part relieve and reduce them, and dissolve the pain. For a moderate **numbness** <note>original: *torpor*</note> has the power to take away pain.*

### XXVI.

*Water that heats up quickly and cools down quickly is the lightest <note>Ancient medicine categorized water by "weight," where "light" water was considered more easily digestible and healthier</note>.*

### XXVII.

*For those who have a craving to drink during the night: if they are very thirsty but manage to fall asleep, it is a good sign.*

<summary>This page details the specific medical applications of heat and cold, recommending heat for bone fractures and internal organs, while suggesting cold for stopping hemorrhages and numbing joint pain.</summary>
<keywords>hydrotherapy, suppuration, hemorrhage, erysipelas, gout, respiratory health, water quality, thermal medicine</keywords>

--- Page 56 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of thermal treatments (heat and cold) from Book V of the Aphorisms. The text begins mid-sentence, concluding the thought from the previous page regarding the use of cold water to stimulate a internal heat response in healthy young men during the summer.</meta>

<page-num>35</page-num>
<header>BOOK V.</header>

Moreover, in a well-fleshed <note>original: *eusarco*; from the Greek, referring to a sturdy, muscular, or well-proportioned physique</note> young man, in the vigor of summer, a large amount of cold water poured on him calls back the innate heat from the core; and it is heat that heals such things.

### X X I I.

Heat is safe when it brings a wound to **suppuration** <note>original: *pus*; ancient doctors often viewed the formation of pus as a sign that the body was successfully "cooking" or resolving an infection</note>. It provides a sign of safety, relieves spasms, tetanus, and stiffness, and thins and softens the skin while taking away pain. It lightens the brain and is fit for broken bones—especially those stripped of flesh, and even more so for those in the head. It also helps those whose parts have been killed by cold or are prone to ulcers, as well as for spreading sores <note>original: *herpetibus*; "creeping" skin conditions like shingles or lupus</note>, the bladder, the womb, the genitals, and the anus. For all these, heat is beneficial and considered a turning point toward health; whereas, on the contrary, cold is deadly and harmful.

### X X I I I.

However, it is right to use cold in those cases where there is a flow of blood, or soon will be; it should not be applied to the site of the wound itself, but above it, from where the blood flows toward that spot. And if there is a burning in any part, such as a new inflammation <note>original: *phlegmone*; a hot, red swelling caused by "fresh blood"</note> that glows reddish, cold is helpful there. Indeed, it turns old inflammations black. It heals **erysipelas** <note>original: *erysipelas*; a bacterial skin infection</note> when there is no open sore, but it makes **sacred fire** <note>original: *sacrum ignem*; a term for severe, spreading skin inflammations, often applied to the same condition when it becomes more dangerous</note> worse.

### X X I V.

Cold things, such as ice or snow, harm the chest; they burst veins and cause coughs and catarrhs <note>original: *catharros*; excessive drainage or mucus from the nose or throat</note>.

### X X V.

Cold water applied abundantly relieves swellings in the joints, spasms, and **gout** <note>original: *podagras*; a form of inflammatory arthritis, usually in the feet</note>, as well as most pains where no ulcer is present, for it lessens and dissolves the pain; indeed, even a slight numbness is able to resolve such things.

### X X I V I.

The water <note>original: *lympha*; literally "water" or "clear liquid"</note> that cools down and heats up most quickly is the lightest.

### X X V I I.

It is healthy to fall asleep at night even when suffering from a great thirst.

<meta>catchword: XXVIII. Fœ-</meta>

<meta>This page continues the fifth book of Hippocratic Aphorisms. While the previous page discussed the topical use of heat and cold for wounds and inflammation, this page shifts focus to women's health (gynecology) and pregnancy, specifically addressing treatments, signs of miscarriage, and menstrual health.</meta>

# THE APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES

->XXVIII.<-

*Fumigation* <note>original: *suffitus*; the practice of exposing the body, especially the genitals, to medicinal smoke or vapors</note> *with scented substances draws out the womanly discharges. It would be useful for many other things, if it did not cause a heaviness in the head.*

->XXIX.<-

*In pregnant women, purging medicines should be administered if the humors are rushing toward excretion—specifically from the fourth to the seventh month, though less so in the latter. However, in the earlier and later stages of pregnancy, one must proceed with great caution.* <note>Ancient physicians believed that during the middle of pregnancy, the fetus was "stronger" and less likely to be dislodged by the violent effects of laxatives or emetics.</note>

->XXX.<-

*Opening a vein* <note>original: *vena secta*; bloodletting or phlebotomy</note> *in a pregnant woman causes a miscarriage, especially if the fetus is quite large.*

->XXXI.<-

*It is fatal for a pregnant woman to be seized by any acute disease.*

->XXXII.<-

*For a woman who is vomiting blood, a resolution of the illness occurs when her menstrual flow breaks out.*

->XXXIII.<-

*If a woman's menstrual periods are suppressed, a nosebleed is a good sign.* <note>Ancient medicine often viewed different bodily fluids as interchangeable; a nosebleed was seen as a "vicarous" menstruation that relieved internal pressure.</note>

->XXXIV.<-

*If a pregnant woman suffers from severe diarrhea, there is a danger that she may miscarry.*

->XXXV.<-

*For a woman suffering from the "strangulation of the womb"* <note>original: *uteri strangulatu*; a condition also known as hysteria, where the womb was thought to move upward and press against other organs</note> *or laboring with a difficult birth, a sneeze following afterward is a good sign.*

->XXXVI.<-

*When a woman’s menstrual blood is off-colored and does not always arrive at the same regular interval, it indicates that a purging of the body is necessary.*

->XXXVII.<-

*If the breasts of a pregnant woman suddenly become thin and shrunken, a miscarriage usually follows.*

->XXXVIII.<-

*In a woman carrying twins, if one breast becomes shrunken, she will miscarry one of the children. If the right breast shrinks, she loses the male child; if the left breast shrinks, she loses the female.* <note>This reflects the ancient theory that the right side of the body was "warmer" and associated with males, while the left was "colder" and associated with females.</note>

<meta>catchword: XXXIX. If</meta>

<summary>This page contains medical aphorisms concerning women's reproductive health, focusing on the risks of bloodletting and acute illness during pregnancy, and identifying physical signs (such as breast changes) that predict miscarriage.</summary>
<keywords>gynecology, pregnancy, miscarriage, menstruation, bloodletting, fumigation, Hippocrates, ancient medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the fifth book of the Hippocratic Aphorisms. Following the discussion on the therapeutic uses of heat and cold, this section focuses on gynecological health, pregnancy risks, and the diagnostic significance of menstruation and bodily changes in women.</meta>

# BOOK V.

### XXVIII.

*Fumigation* <note>original: *suffitus*; the practice of exposing the body to medicinal vapors or smoke</note> *with pleasant-smelling substances draws out the womanly discharges; and it would be useful for many more conditions if it did not cause a heaviness in the head.*

### XXIX.

*Give purging medicines* <note>original: *pharmaca purgantia*; substances used to induce vomiting or bowel movements to clear "corrupt humors"</note> *to pregnant women if the humors are turgid* <note>meaning swollen or excessively full</note> *from the fourth until the seventh month, but less so than for others. However, when the infant is either younger or older, one must proceed with great caution.*

### XXX.

*Bloodletting* <note>original: *phlebotomia*; the surgical opening of a vein</note> *causes a miscarriage in a pregnant woman, and this is especially likely if the fetus is quite large.*

### XXXI.

*It is fatal if an acute disease afflicts a pregnant woman.*

### XXXII.

*The vomiting of blood is stopped by the onset of the menstrual flow.*

### XXXIII.

*For those whose menses are absent, a hemorrhage* <note>usually understood here as a nosebleed</note> *is a healthy sign.* <note>Ancient medicine viewed the body as a system of fluids; if one "gate" was closed, nature would find another exit to prevent toxic buildup.</note>

### XXXIV.

*In pregnant women, a flux of the belly* <note>original: *fluxus ventris*; diarrhea</note> *threatens a miscarriage.*

### XXXV.

*Sneezing is a good sign for a woman suffering from hysteria* <note>original: *hystericæ*; literally "suffering in the womb," a condition ancients believed was caused by the uterus moving out of place</note> *and for one who is having a difficult labor.*

### XXXVI.

*When the menses vary in their timing and appear with a bad color, they indicate that it is right to use purging treatments.*

### XXXVII.

*If the breasts of a pregnant woman should suddenly become thin* <note>original: *gracilescere*; to grow slender or wither</note>, *a miscarriage of the offspring usually follows.*

### XXXVIII.

*If one breast withers in a woman carrying twins, it signifies the miscarriage of one of the children; if the left breast withers, the female child is lost; if the right, the male.* <note>This reflects the ancient Greek belief that males developed on the right side of the womb and females on the left.</note>

-><term>Phlebotomia</term>: bloodletting; <term>abortus</term>: miscarriage; <term>gravidis</term>: pregnant women; <term>menstrua</term>: monthly periods; <term>hystericæ</term>: relating to the womb; <term>Pharmaca</term>: medicines or drugs; <term>suffitus</term>: fumigation; <term>geminos</term>: twins.<-

<meta>This page continues the fifth book of the Hippocratic Aphorisms. The previous page discussed the impact of sneezing, menstruation, and purging on the health of pregnant women. This page continues the gynecological and obstetric observations, focusing on diagnostic signs of pregnancy, the health of the mother, and the causes of miscarriage.</meta>

<header>APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES,</header>

### X X X I X.

*If a woman who is neither pregnant nor has recently given birth has milk, her menstruation has ceased.* <note>Ancient physicians believed that bodily fluids like blood and milk were interchangeable; if the monthly "cleansing" of menstruation stopped, the excess fluid might be transformed into milk.</note>

### X L.

*In women for whom blood collects in a swelling of the breasts, it is a sign of impending madness.* <note>original: *furor*; likely referring to a state of delirium or mania believed to be caused by "corrupt" blood migrating from the reproductive system to the upper body.</note>

### X L I.

*If you wish to know whether a woman has conceived, give her honey-water* <note>original: *aquam mulsam*; a mixture of honey and water</note> *to drink when she is about to sleep; if she is then seized by cramps in the belly, she has conceived; but if she is not seized, she has not.*

### X L I I.

*A pregnant woman has a better complexion if she carries a male; if a female, her color is poorer.* <note>The Greeks believed male fetuses were "warmer" and "drier" than females, which they thought imparted a healthier, more vibrant glow to the mother.</note>

### X L I I I.

*If erysipelas* <note>original: *erysipelas*; a severe bacterial skin infection, though here likely meaning an internal inflammation or "fire"</note> *should arise in the womb of a pregnant woman, it is fatal.*

### X L I V.

*Women who are unnaturally thin while pregnant will suffer a miscarriage until they become more well-fleshed.*

### X L V.

*When women of an average build miscarry in the second or third month without any obvious cause, the cavities of the womb (which the Greeks call "cotyledons")* <note>original: *cotyledonas*; from the Greek for "cups," referring to the vascular connections between the mother and the placenta</note> *are full of mucus; because of this, they cannot support the weight of the fetus and are torn away.*

### X L I V I.

*Those who are unnaturally fat do not conceive; in their case, the caul* <note>original: *omentum*; the fatty tissue lining the abdomen</note> *compresses the opening of the womb, and they do not become pregnant until they have lost weight.*

### X L V I I.

*If that part of the womb which rests against the hip should develop an abscess,* <note>original: *suppuratus*; indicating the formation of pus or a localized infection</note> *it must be treated by applying suitable medicinal liniments.*

### X L V I I I.

*Male fetuses are usually carried in the right side of the womb, and females in the left side.* <note>This reflects the ancient belief that the right side of the body was hotter and more "noble," thus attracting the male seed.</note>

<header>BOOK V.</header>

### X X X I X.

If a woman who has neither given birth nor is currently pregnant has milk in her
->breasts,<-
it is a sign that her menstruation has failed. <note>Ancient physicians believed that bodily fluids were interconnected; if the monthly "cleansing" of blood stopped without pregnancy, they thought the excess fluid was diverted and transformed into milk.</note>

### X L.

If blood should collect in a swelling of the breasts, it is a sign of madness. <note>original: *furoris*; this refers to a state of delirium or acute mental distress, believed to be caused by "corrupt" blood migrating from the reproductive organs to the upper body.</note>

### X L I.

If you wish to determine whether a woman has conceived,
provide her with honey-water to drink at the time of sleep;
for if she has conceived, she will suffer from griping pains in the belly,
but if she has not, she will remain free from such pains. <note>original: *mulsam aquam*; a mixture of honey and water. This was an ancient diagnostic test based on the belief that a closed, "occupied" womb would react to the honey's properties.</note>

### X L I I.

In pregnant women, if the infant is male,
the complexion is better; but if it is female, the color is worse. <note>The Greeks associated the male sex with "heat" and "dryness," which they believed produced a healthy, vibrant glow in the mother, whereas females were "colder" and "moister."</note>

### X L I I I.

If ever an inflammation of the womb <note>original: *erysipelas uteri*; while erysipelas is now known as a specific bacterial skin infection, here it refers to a severe, spreading "fire" or inflammation within the uterus.</note>
arises in a woman carrying a fetus, it must be considered fatal.

### X L I V.

If a woman is unnaturally thin while she is pregnant,
she will suffer a miscarriage until she becomes more well-fleshed.

### X L V.

If, however, women of a moderate build miscarry in the second
or third month without an obvious cause,
the membranes of their womb are filled with mucus,
and they break, because they cannot bear the weight of the
->fetus.<-

### X L V I.

If those who are excessively fat do not conceive,
it is because the mouth of the womb is narrowed by the fatty membrane, <term>omentum: a fold of fatty tissue in the abdomen</term>
and they do not become pregnant unless they grow thin.

### X L V I I.

If the womb should suppurate in the part near the
hip, you shall treat it with a suitable ointment.

### X L V I I I.

A male fetus lies more on the right side, and a female on the left. <note>The right side of the body was traditionally associated with heat and strength (masculinity), while the left was associated with cold and weakness (femininity).</note>

<header>HIPPOCRATES' APHORISMS</header>

<meta>catchword: fumigate</meta>

->XLIX.<-

*To ensure the afterbirth is expelled, apply a sneezing agent to the nostrils; then, block both the nostrils and the mouth.* <note>original: *secunda*; referring to the placenta or "second birth." Ancient physicians believed that the forceful pressure of a suppressed sneeze could help physically dislodge the placenta after the child was born.</note>

->L.<-

*If you wish to stop a woman’s menstruation, apply the largest possible cupping glass to the breasts.* <note>The Hippocratic school believed in "revulsion"—drawing a fluid from one part of the body to another. By applying suction to the breasts, they believed they could pull the blood flow upward and away from the womb.</note>

->LI.<-

*In those who are pregnant, the mouth of the womb is tightly closed.* <note>original: *connivet*; this is a correct anatomical observation regarding the cervix during pregnancy.</note>

->LII.<-

*In a pregnant woman, if milk flows abundantly from the breasts, it indicates that the fetus is weak. But if the breasts remain firm, it signifies a stronger fetus.* <note>Ancient theory held that the fetus was nourished by the same blood that would later become milk; therefore, if milk was leaking out, the "nourishment" was being diverted away from the baby.</note>

->LIII.<-

*In those about to miscarry, the breasts become thin and wasted. If, on the contrary, they become hard, there will be pain in the breasts, or in the hips, or in the eyes, or in the knees, but they will not miscarry.*

->LIV.<-

*In those for whom the mouth of the womb is hard, it is necessary that the mouth of the womb also be closed.*

->LV.<-

*Pregnant women who are seized by fevers and waste away significantly without an obvious cause will experience a difficult and dangerous birth, or will be at risk of miscarriage.*

->LVI.<-

*If a convulsion or fainting occurs during the menstrual flow, it is a bad sign.*

->LVII.<-

*Diseases occur when the menses flow too abundantly, and diseases of the womb also arise when the flow does not occur at all.*

->LVIII.<-

*Painful or obstructed urination follows inflammation of the rectum or the womb, or when the kidneys are full of pus. However, when the liver suffers from inflammation, hiccups follow.* <note>original: *stranguria* and *singultus*. This aphorism connects specific secondary symptoms to internal organ inflammation.</note>

->LIX.<-

*If a woman does not conceive, and she wishes to know whether she is capable of conceiving, wrap her in clothes on all sides and fumigate her from below* <note>original: *suffi*; the text breaks off here. This refers to a common Hippocratic test where incense was burned under a woman; if the scent reached her mouth or nose, it was believed her internal "tubes" were open and she was fertile.</note>

# LIBER V.

### X L I X.

*If you desire to quickly expel the lingering afterbirth, provide a sneezing agent, then close the nostrils and the mouth.* <note>original: *secundas*; referring to the placenta or "second birth." Ancient physicians believed that the forceful pressure of a suppressed sneeze could help physically dislodge the placenta after the child was born.</note>

### L.

*If you wish to halt excessive menstruation in a woman, apply a large cupping glass to her breasts.* <note>The Hippocratic school believed in "revulsion"—drawing a fluid from one part of the body to another. By applying suction to the breasts, they believed they could pull the blood flow upward and away from the womb.</note>

### L I.

*In a woman carrying a fetus, the mouth of the womb is tightly closed.* <note>original: *os uteri*; a correct anatomical observation regarding the cervix during pregnancy.</note>

### L I I.

*If much milk flows from the breasts in pregnant women, it indicates a weak fetus; if the breasts remain firm, it indicates a strong one.* <note>Ancient theory held that the fetus was nourished by the same blood that would later become milk; therefore, if milk was leaking out, the "nourishment" was being diverted away from the baby.</note>

### L I I I.

*In those women who are about to lose their fetus, the breasts become small and wasted. If, on the contrary, the breasts become hard, pains will seize them in the hips, the breasts, the eyes, or the knees, but they will not suffer a miscarriage.*

### L I V.

*In those for whom the mouth of the womb is hard, it is also tightly closed.*

### L V.

*If a woman, while carrying a fetus, suffers from a fever and becomes greatly wasted without an obvious cause, she will give birth with difficulty and not without great danger, or it is to be feared that a miscarriage may occur.*

### L V I.

*A spasm occurring at the onset of the monthly period, accompanied by fainting, is a bad sign.* <note>original: *syncope*; a sudden loss of consciousness or strength.</note>

### L V I I.

*Both excessive and suppressed menstruation cause their own specific diseases.*

### L V I I I.

*If an inflammation occurs in the rectum or the womb, or if the kidneys should suppurate, difficulty in urinating follows; however, inflammation of the liver produces hiccups.* <note>original: *phlegmone* (a hot swelling or inflammation); *stranguria* (the painful, drop-by-drop discharge of urine).</note>

### L I X.

*If you wish to know whether a woman who has not conceived will do so in the future, wrap her tightly in a garment on all sides...*

# APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES

...then applied, and if the odor seems to pass through the body to your nostrils and mouth, know that she is not sterile by her own nature. <note>original: *non ex sese infœcundam*; this concludes a diagnostic test from the previous page where a scent—likely incense or garlic—was introduced vaginally to check if the internal passages were clear. If the smell reached the mouth, the woman was considered physically capable of conception.</note>

### L X.

*If a pregnant woman's monthly purgings <note>original: *purgationes menstruæ*; menstruation.</note> continue their course, it is impossible for the fetus to be healthy.*

### L X I.

*If a woman’s periods do not appear, and neither shivering nor fever follows, but she experiences a loathing for food, consider her to be pregnant.*

### L X I I.

*Those who have cold and dense wombs do not conceive. And those who have overly moist ones cannot become pregnant, for the seed <note>original: *genitura*; the reproductive essence or "seed."</note> is extinguished within them. And those whose wombs are too dry and parched [fail], for the seed is ruined by a lack of nourishment. But those who have obtained a moderate balance of these parts, avoiding either extreme, are robust in fertility.*

### L X I I I.

*In the male sex, the reasoning is similar: either because of great porosity of the body the vital breath <note>original: *spiritus*; the "pneuma" or driving force that ancient doctors believed moved fluids through the body.</note> is scattered outward so that the seed cannot be expelled; or because of density the fluid does not leap forth; or because of coldness it is not "fired" enough to gather in that place; or because of heat this same result occurs.*

### L X I V.

*Giving milk to those with headaches is harmful. It is also harmful for those with fevers, those whose upper abdomen <note>original: *præcordia*; the region of the diaphragm and stomach.</note> is swollen and rumbling, and those who are thirsty. It is also harmful for those with bilious stools, those suffering from acute fever, and those who have had a great loss of blood. However, it is suitable to give milk to those with wasting diseases <note>original: *tabidis*; those suffering from consumption or chronic physical decline.</note>, provided they do not have a very high fever, and in long, lingering fevers—provided none of the conditions mentioned above are present—and to those who are excessively thin.*

### L X V.

*Those in whom swellings appear alongside ulcers are generally neither seized by convulsions nor fall into a frenzy. But if these swellings suddenly vanish—at least those on the back—*

# BOOK V.

*Perform the fumigation, and if the scent pervades the body until it reaches the mouth and nostrils, remember that the woman is not sterile by her own nature.* <note>original: *Suffito... de se sterilem non esse memento*; this concludes the "garlic test" or incense test used to determine if the reproductive tracts were clear.</note>

### LX.

*A fetus cannot be properly healthy if the monthly purgings <note>original: *menstrua*; menstruation.</note> continue to flow during pregnancy.*

### LXI.

*If a woman's periods cease, and neither fever nor shivering follows, but she feels a loathing for food, you may believe her to be pregnant.*

### LXII.

*Women do not conceive if their womb <term>matrix (womb)</term> is dense and cold, or if it is overly moist, for in such cases the seed is extinguished. Likewise, those whose wombs are too dry and parched fail to conceive, for the seed is destroyed by a lack of nourishment. However, those whose wombs maintain a moderate balance of these qualities are blessed with offspring.*

### LXIII.

*A similar reasoning usually applies to the male sex: if the vital spirit <note>original: *spiritus*; the "pneuma" or life-force believed to move fluids.</note> escapes immoderately because of a very porous skin, they are unable to discharge the seed; or the fluid does not leap forth because of the body’s density; or it is not ignited due to cold, preventing it from gathering; or these failures occur because of excessive heat, which dissolves the seed.*

### LXIV.

*Milk is harmful to those whose midriff <term>præcordia (the area of the diaphragm and upper abdomen)</term> is raised and produces rumbling sounds, as well as those who are thirsty, or who suffer in the brain or with fever. It is also harmful to those troubled by acute fever, those with excess bile, or those who have suffered a great discharge of blood. Conversely, milk is beneficial for those suffering from wasting <note>original: *tabe*; consumption or a gradual withering of the body.</note> who do not have much fever, as well as those held by a mild and very long-lasting fever—provided none of the previously mentioned symptoms are present—and those who are greatly emaciated without a known cause.*

### LXV.

*Those in whom ulcerous swellings <note>original: *ulcerei tumores*; likely referring to abscesses or inflamed growths.</note> appear do not suffer much from convulsions <note>original: *spasmo*; involuntary muscle contractions.</note> or madness of the mind. However, if red swellings on the back suddenly*

<header>APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES</header>

...falls into the [vitals], convulsions and tensions of the nerves <note>original: *nervorum distentiones*; likely referring to spasms or tetanus</note> occur. But for those in whom it occurs toward the forehead, there come fits of madness, or acute pains in the side, or an accumulation of pus, or bowel distress, if red swellings should appear.

->L X V I.<-

*If no swelling appears in cases of large and dangerous wounds, it portends a great evil for the sick person.* <note>In ancient medicine, a lack of swelling in a severe wound was often feared as a sign that the body was too weak to mount a defense or that the "bad humors" were retreating into the internal organs.</note>

->L X V I I.<-

*Soft swellings are favorable; but raw <note>original: *crudi*; meaning hard, unripened, or not yet coming to a head</note> swellings are dangerous.*

->L X V I I I.<-

*For one who suffers from pain in the back of the head, opening the straight vein in the forehead brings relief.* <note>This refers to the practice of phlebotomy or bloodletting, based on the theory that relieving pressure in one area could draw "excess" blood away from another.</note>

->L X I X.<-

*Shivering fits <term>rigores (chills or tremors)</term> in women usually begin from the loins and travel through the back up to the head. In men, they also tend to arise in the back of the body rather than the front, such as from the elbows and thighs. This is indicated by the thinness of the skin, which is also revealed by the growth of hair.*

->L X X.<-

*Those seized by quartan fever <note>A fever that spikes every fourth day (by inclusive counting), typically associated with malaria.</note> are not much troubled by convulsions; and if they were troubled by them before, they are freed of them when the quartan fever arrives.*

->L X X I.<-

*Those whose skin is drawn tight, dry, and hard die without sweating; but those whose skin is loose and porous pass away with a sweat.*

->L X X I I.<-

*Those who suffer from the royal disease <term>morbus regius (jaundice)</term> are not very flatulent.*

# BOOK V.

They vanish, but tetany <note>original: *terani*; likely a transcription error for *tetani*, referring to the rigid muscle spasms of tetanus</note> and spasms seize them; for those whom there is madness of the mind, an accumulation of pus, griping pains in the bowels, and great pains in the side.

### L X V I.

*If no swellings appear in cases of large and dangerous wounds, it portends a great evil for the sick person.* <note>In ancient medicine, a lack of swelling in a severe wound was often feared as a sign that the body was too weak to mount a defense or that the "bad humors" were retreating into the internal organs.</note>

### L X V I I.

*Hard lumps <note>original: *tubera cruda*; literally "raw" lumps that have not yet "ripened" or softened</note> are bad; but on the contrary, soft ones are good.*

### L X V I I I.

*For those whose brain suffers in the back part of the head, opening the straight vein in the forehead brings relief.* <note>The practice of bloodletting often involved lancing a vein far from the site of pain to "draw" the offending blood or humor away from the affected area.</note>

### L X I X.

*In a woman, shivering fits <term>rigor (chills accompanied by tremors)</term> generally begin in the loins and travel through the back up to the brain. In the male sex, they also tend to arise from the back parts of the body, namely from the thighs and elbows. A sign of this is thin skin, which the growth of hair itself reveals.*

### L X X.

*Those suffering from quartan fever <note>A fever that recurs every third day, or every 72 hours, usually associated with malaria in antiquity.</note> are rarely seized by convulsions; but if they are convulsed, the fever liberates them from the condition.* <note>Hippocrates often observed that one disease could sometimes "cure" another; here, the intense heat of the fever was thought to resolve the spasms.</note>

### L X X I.

*Those who have dry and hard skin do not sweat at the time of death; conversely, those who have dry but thin skin do sweat.*

### L X X I I.

*Those who are overcome by bile <note>original: *suffusi felle*; likely referring to jaundice, where the skin is "stained" by bile</note> do not suffer much from gas.*

->THE END OF THE FIFTH BOOK.<-

<meta>catchword: BOOK</meta>

<summary>This page concludes Book V of the Aphorisms, providing clinical observations on the prognostic value of swellings in wounds and the specific physiological paths that chills take in men versus women. It also notes the unusual relationship where a quartan fever can actually end a period of convulsions.</summary>

<keywords>tetanus, spasms, wound swelling, phlebotomy, shivering, quartan fever, jaundice, bile, Hippocratic medicine</keywords>

<meta>This page marks the beginning of Book VI of the Hippocratic Aphorisms. It follows the conclusion of Book V, which ended with observations on fevers, convulsions, and the physical signs of illness in different sexes and ages.</meta>

<header>APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES</header>

# BOOK SIX

## I.

*In long-lasting smoothness of the intestines <note>original: *intestinorum levitatibus*; likely referring to lientery, a form of diarrhea where food passes through the body undigested</note>, if sour belching occurs which was not there before, it is a good sign.*

## II.

*Those whose nostrils are naturally moister, and whose seed <note>original: *genitura*; referring to reproductive fluids or "constitution"</note> is moister, enjoy less prosperous health; but those for whom the opposite is true live more healthily.*

## III.

*In long-lasting intestinal troubles, a loathing of food is a bad sign, and if accompanied by fever, it is even worse.*

## IV.

*Ulcers that are smooth all around are malignant.* <note>In ancient medicine, a "smooth" or glassy-looking wound was often a sign of poor healing, lack of "good" inflammation, or cancerous growth.</note>

## V.

*One must learn thoroughly whether pains in the sides, in the chest, and in other parts differ much from one another.*

## VI.

*If any defects appear in the kidneys or the bladder during old age, they are difficult to cure.*

## VII.

*Pains that occur in the belly, if they are high up <note>original: *sublimes*; referring to the upper abdomen or superficial pains</note>, are lighter; but those that are not high up <note>referring to deep-seated or lower abdominal pains</note> are more severe.*

## VIII.

*In those suffering from water under the skin <term>dropsy (a condition now known as edema, where fluid accumulates in the body's tissues)</term>, ulcers that break out on the body are not easily healed.*

## IX.

*Broad pustules are not very itchy.*

## X.

*When the head is aching and suffering intensely, pus, water, or blood flowing out through the nostrils, the mouth, or the ears removes the disease.* <note>Ancient Greek medicine believed the body could "purge" itself of harmful humors or excess fluids through these passages to find relief.</note>

## XI.

*The occurrence of hemorrhoids (that is, a flow of blood through the mouths of the veins accustomed to shedding blood in the anus) following melancholic conditions <note>referring to illnesses caused by an excess of "black bile," often including depression or digestive issues</note> or kidney defects is a good thing.*

<meta>catchword: XII. For a long time</meta>

<header>BOOK VI.</header>

# BOOK SIX.

->I.<-

A sour belching that follows, which was not there before, announces favorable omens in long-lasting cases of <term>lientery (a form of diarrhea where food passes through the digestive tract undigested)</term>. <note>Hippocrates suggests that the appearance of stomach acid indicates that the digestive process is restarting after a period of failure.</note>

->II.<-

Those whose nostrils and <term>seed (referring to reproductive fluids or general constitution)</term> are naturally dry live more healthily than those who are, by contrast, naturally moist.

->III.<-

In long-lasting cases of <term>griping pains (intestinal colic)</term>, nausea brings bad news; it is even worse if a fever simultaneously grips the patient.

->IV.<-

Ulcers that are smooth <note>original: *glabra*; literally hairless, but in a clinical sense, it refers to a glassy, non-healing appearance</note> all around the edges are malignant.

->V.<-

Learn to distinguish whether pains in the chest, the side, or any other part hold many differences among themselves.

->VI.<-

If any ailment should take hold of the bladder or the kidneys in old age, it is usually difficult to cure.

->VII.<-

Pains deep in the belly <note>original: *humiles*; literally "low," referring here to deep-seated or lower abdominal pain</note> cause more torment than those that are high up <note>superficial or in the upper abdomen</note>.

->VIII.<-

In those suffering from <term>dropsy (edema, or fluid retention)</term>, any ulcers that break out are a serious matter for treatment.

->IX.<-

A broad pustule does not suffer from much itching <note>original: *purigine*; a variant of *prurigine*, meaning an itch</note>.

->X.<-

If ever pus, water, or blood should exit through the nostrils, mouth, or ears, an acute <term>headache (cephalgia)</term> will cease.

->XI.<-

In ailments of the kidneys and afflictions of <term>black bile (one of the four humors, associated here with melancholy or systemic illness)</term>, a flow of blood from the veins of the anus <note>referring to hemorrhoidal bleeding</note> is beneficial.

<meta>catchword: XII. From</meta>

<summary>This page lists medical aphorisms regarding the prognosis of chronic illnesses, noting that certain bodily discharges or changes in symptoms (like sour belching or hemorrhoidal bleeding) can indicate the resolution of deeper ailments.</summary>
<keywords>lientery, digestion, colic, ulcers, kidney disease, dropsy, pustules, headache, black bile, hemorrhoids</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the sixth book of the Aphorisms of Hippocrates. The previous page discussed the prognosis of chronic conditions involving the kidneys, bladder, and internal ulcers. This page moves into specific signs of recovery (crises) and the dangers of certain internal injuries or the sudden cessation of chronic discharges.</meta>

<header>APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES.</header>

## X I I.

*If you are treating a patient for a long-lasting flow of blood from the veins in the anus (which they call <term>hemorrhoids</term>), it is dangerous to cure all of them unless one is preserved; otherwise, there is a risk that <term>dropsy (fluid retention under the skin)</term> or <term>consumption (a wasting disease)</term> may follow.* <note>Ancient physicians believed that chronic bleeding like hemorrhoids acted as a "safety valve" for the body's humors. If stopped too abruptly, they feared the excess fluid would settle in the tissues or lungs.</note>

## X I I I.

*When a person is seized by hiccups, if sneezing occurs, it will stop the hiccups.*

## X I V.

*In a person suffering from <term>dropsy</term>, the disease is resolved when the fluid flows from the veins into the belly.* <note>This likely refers to the fluid being purged through the bowels.</note>

## X V.

*In one suffering from long-lasting diarrhea, the onset of spontaneous vomiting resolves the illness.*

## X I V.

*If diarrhea occurs in someone struggling with <term>pleurisy (inflammation of the side)</term> or inflammation of the lungs, it is a bad sign.*

## X V I I.

*It is a good sign for someone suffering from <term>ophthalmia (eye inflammation)</term> to be seized by diarrhea.* <note>original: "lippitudo"; a general term for inflamed, runny, or bleary eyes. The theory was that the "humors" causing the eye trouble were being redirected and expelled through the gut.</note>

## X V I I I.

*It is fatal if the bladder is cut through, or the brain, or the heart, or the <term>diaphragm (the transverse septum)</term>, or any part of the small intestine, or the stomach, or the liver.*

## X I X.

*If a bone, a cartilage, a nerve, a thin part of the jaw, or the foreskin is cut through, it neither grows back nor unites.* <note>In the context of the time, "nerve" (*nervus*) often referred to tendons or ligaments as well as actual nerves.</note>

## X X.

*If blood is spilled into the abdominal cavity in an unnatural way, it must necessarily turn into pus.* <note>This observes the process of internal bleeding leading to infection or abscess.</note>

## X X I.

*In those suffering from madness, if varicose veins or a flow of blood from the veins in the anus (called hemorrhoids) should appear, it marks the resolution of the insanity.*

## X X I I.

*Pains that descend from the back to the elbow joints are resolved by <term>bloodletting (the opening of a vein)</term>.*

<summary>This page lists several clinical observations regarding the "crisis" or turning point of diseases, specifically how one bodily discharge (like diarrhea or sneezing) can cure another ailment (like eye inflammation or hiccups). It also provides a grim list of internal organs where wounds are considered inevitably fatal.</summary>
<keywords>hemorrhoids, dropsy, hiccups, diarrhea, pleurisy, ophthalmia, fatal wounds, internal bleeding, insanity, bloodletting</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Book VI of the Aphorisms of Hippocrates. The previous page ended mid-sentence while listing vital organs that, if pierced, result in a fatal prognosis. This page completes that list and continues with observations on how certain conditions or bodily discharges can either signal danger or provide a cure for other ailments.</meta>

<header>BOOK VI.</header>

### X I I.

If you are treating a patient for a long-standing flow of blood from the veins of the anus (hemorrhoids), unless one of these veins is always kept open, there is a great danger that <term>dropsy</term> <note>original: "hydropis"; an old term for swelling caused by excess fluid in the body</note> or <term>consumption</term> <note>original: "tabis"; a wasting away of the body, often associated with tuberculosis</note> may develop.

### X I I I.

When a person is suffering from hiccups, the onset of sneezing will soon bring relief.

### X I V.

If blood should flow from the veins into the belly of a person suffering from <term>dropsy</term>, it frees the patient from the disease. <note>The "belly" here likely refers to the digestive tract, suggesting that an internal discharge of blood might trigger a purgative effect that relieves the fluid buildup.</note>

### X V.

Spontaneous vomiting brings an end to a long-lasting bout of diarrhea.

### X I V.

If diarrhea occurs in those who have developed <term>phlegmon</term> <note>original: "phlegmone"; a localized inflammation of soft tissue</note> of the <term>pleura</term> <note>the membrane surrounding the lungs</note> or of the lungs themselves, it is a deadly sign.

### X V I I.

It is a good sign for those suffering from <term>ophthalmia</term> <note>original: "lippis"; referring to bleary or inflamed eyes</note> if they are seized by a flow from the bowels.

### X V I I I.

If anyone has their stomach, heart, bladder, brain, liver, <term>diaphragm</term> <note>continuing from the previous page: the transverse muscle separating the chest from the abdomen</note>, or small intestine pierced through, it must be considered a fatal injury.

### X I X.

When a bone, the <term>foreskin</term> <note>original: "præputia"</note>, a nerve, cartilage, or a small portion of the cheek is completely severed, the wound usually will not grow back together or heal.

### X X.

If blood breaks out into the cavity of the belly, it must necessarily turn into pus. <note>Ancient medicine taught that stagnant blood inside the body would inevitably "ripen" or decay into purulent matter.</note>

### X X I.

If varicose veins or hemorrhoids appear in a person suffering from madness, these conditions usually restore the patient to a healthy mind. <note>This reflects the "humoral" theory, where mental illness was thought to be caused by excess "black bile" or heat, which could be drained away by physical bleeding elsewhere in the body.</note>

### X X I I.

Any pain or rupture that descends from the back to the elbow and limbs is cured by <term>phlebotomy</term> <note>original: "phlebotomia"; the surgical opening of a vein to withdraw blood</note>.

<meta>catchword: XXIII. And</meta>

<summary>This page lists several aphorisms regarding the "crisis" or turning points of diseases, focusing on how bodily discharges like sneezing, vomiting, or hemorrhoids can unexpectedly cure or complicate existing conditions.</summary>
<keywords>Hippocrates, hemorrhoids, dropsy, hiccups, diarrhea, pleurisy, fatal wounds, madness, bloodletting, phlebotomy, anatomy</keywords>

<header>APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES,</header>

->X X I I I.<-

If fear and sadness persist for a long time without a manifest cause, it is an indication of <term>melancholia</term>. <note>original: "melancholiæ"; in ancient medicine, this was attributed to an excess of "black bile" and encompasses what we now call chronic depression or anxiety.</note>

->X X I V.<-

If any part of the small intestines is cut through, it does not knit back together. <note>original: "minimè coalescit"; the "thin" or small intestines were considered too delicate to heal from major trauma in antiquity.</note>

->X X V.<-

*If <term>erysipelas</term> <note>a skin infection characterized by a bright red, spreading rash</note> spreads from the outside to the inside, it is not at all good; but if it moves from the internal parts to the outside, it is a good sign.*

->X X V I.<-

*In those burning fevers where tremors occur, the <term>delirium</term> is resolved.*

->X X V I I I.<- <note>This is likely a printer's error in the original text, as it follows number XXVI and should be XXVII.</note>

*Those who collect pus in the chest, or suffer from <term>dropsy</term> <note>original: "aquâ inter cutem"; literally "water between the skin," referring to generalized swelling or edema</note>, if they are cauterized or cut, and the pus or water flows out all at once, they will surely perish.*

->X X V I I I.<-

*Eunuchs neither suffer from <term>gout</term> <note>original: "podagrâ"; specifically gout of the foot</note> nor do they go bald.*

->X X I V.<-

*A woman does not suffer from gout unless her menstruation has ceased.*

->X X X.<-

*A boy does not suffer from gout before the use of sexual pleasures.* <note>original: "venereorum usum"; referring to the onset of puberty.</note>

->X X X I.<-

*Pains of the eyes are relieved by drinking pure wine, or a bath, or a warm compress, or bloodletting, or the administration of a purgative medicine.*

->X X X I I.<-

*Stutterers are especially prone to long-lasting <term>diarrhea</term> <note>original: "alvi profluvio"; literally a "flow of the bowels"</note>.*

->X X X I I I.<-

*Those who belch acid are not very prone to <term>pleurisy</term> <note>original: "morbo laterali"; a painful inflammation of the chest lining</note>.*

->X X X I V.<-

*Those who are bald do not develop large <term>varicose veins</term> <note>original: "varices"</note>; but if bald men do develop varicose veins, their hair grows back.* <note>Hippocratic medicine often associated hair growth and vein conditions with the movement of internal humors or "vapors."</note>

->X X X V.<-

*In those suffering from dropsy, a cough following the condition is a bad sign.*

# BOOK VI.

### X X I I I.

If fear and sorrow persist for a long time without cause, they are the messengers of <term>black bile</term>. <note>original: "atræ nuntia bilis"; in the theory of the four humors, an excess of black bile was thought to cause "melancholy," which encompasses modern concepts of chronic depression and anxiety.</note>

### X X I V.

A completely severed small intestine does not knit back together. <note>The "thin" or small intestines were considered too delicate for the surgical techniques of the time to repair successfully.</note>

### X X V.

It is a good sign whenever <term>erysipelas</term> <note>a bacterial skin infection characterized by a bright red, spreading rash</note> migrates from the deeper parts of the body to the surface; it is the opposite, however, if it turns from the outside inward.

### X X V I.

In a burning fever, the onset of tremors resolves <term>delirium</term>.

### X X V I I.

Those suffering from <term>dropsy</term> <note>original: "hydrope"; generalized swelling or edema</note>, or those gathering pus in the chest, will surely perish if all the water or discharge is drained away at once by burning or cutting. <note>Ancient medicine favored slow drainage; a sudden loss of fluid was often observed to cause fatal shock in weakened patients.</note>

### X X V I I I.

A eunuch never suffers from baldness or <term>gout</term>. <note>original: "podagrâ"; specifically gout affecting the feet.</note>

### X X I X.

A woman does not suffer from gout unless her menstruation has ceased.

### X X X.

A boy does not suffer from gout until after he has experienced the acts of <term>Venus</term>. <note>original: "veneris"; referring to the onset of sexual activity or puberty.</note>

### X X X I.

Baths, the medicinal drinking of wine, <note>original: "thorexis"; a Greek term for drinking wine until the body is "warmed through" or "saturated."</note> purging, bloodletting, or warm compresses usually heal pains of the eyes.

### X X X I I.

A long-lasting flux of the belly <note>chronic diarrhea</note> more often plagues those who stutter. <note>Ancient physicians often linked speech impediments to an "excess of moisture" in the brain or tongue; a flux was seen as the body's attempt to redirect that moisture.</note>

### X X X I I I.

<term>Pleurisy</term> <note>inflammation of the lining of the lungs</note> rarely seizes those who experience acid belching.

### X X X I V.

Large varicose veins do not arise in bald men; but if they should arise, the hair grows back as it was before. <note>This observation relates to the ancient belief that both hair growth and varicose veins were governed by the movement of specific humors or "vapors" through the body.</note>

### X X X V.

In a person with dropsy, a cough following the condition is fatal.

<meta>catchword: XXXVI. Stran- (likely "Stranguria" or "Strangury")</meta>

<summary>This page lists several of the most famous Hippocratic aphorisms regarding the demographic distribution of gout and the relationship between physical traits and internal humors. It provides prognostic signs for serious conditions like dropsy and internal infections.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the sixth book of Hippocrates' Aphorisms, moving from general observations on constitutional conditions like gout to specific clinical prognoses regarding cancer, internal inflammation, and chronic respiratory illnesses.</meta>

HEADER: APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES

### X X X V I.

*Bloodletting resolves difficulty in urinating; however, the internal veins should be opened.* <note>original: "Urinæ difficultatem venæ sectio solvit: secandæ autem sunt interiores." Hippocratic medicine often used venesection (bloodletting) to relieve pressure or "divert" harmful humors from an affected organ.</note>

### X X X V I I.

*In a person suffering from <term>angina</term> <note>original: "anginâ"; in this context, it refers to a severe throat infection or swelling (like quinsy), rather than the modern heart condition</note>, if a swelling arises in the neck, it is a good sign: for the disease is being turned outward.*

### X X X V I I I.

*For those who have hidden cancers, it is better not to provide treatment. For those who are treated perish quickly, but those who are not treated draw out their lives for a longer time.* <note>original: "Quibus occulti cancri adsunt, non curare meliùs." This famous aphorism reflects the ancient observation that invasive surgery or cautery for deep-seated tumors often hastened death through infection or shock.</note>

### X X X I X.

*Hiccups usually arise from the same causes as convulsions: namely, from either excessive fullness or excessive emptying.* <note>Ancient physiology believed that "spasms" of the body were caused by the humors being either too abundant (repletion) or depleted (evacuation).</note>

### X L.

*For those who experience pains in the <term>precordia</term> <note>the region of the midriff or the area just below the ribs</note> without inflammation, a subsequent fever resolves the pain.*

### X L I.

*When an accumulation of pus exists in the body but does not reveal itself, it fails to show a sign either because of the thickness of the pus or because of the density of the location.*

### X L II.

*For those suffering from the <term>Royal Disease</term> <note>original: "Morbo regio"; an ancient name for jaundice, so-called perhaps because it was thought to require expensive, "regal" comforts to cure</note>, it is a bad sign if the liver becomes hard.*

### X L I I I.

*If those with an enlarged spleen are seized by a "difficulty of the intestines" <note>dysentery or chronic diarrhea</note>, and this intestinal trouble persists for a long time, it leads to either <term>dropsy</term> <note>original: "aqua inter cutem"; fluid under the skin</note> or <term>lientery</term> <note>original: "lævitas intestinorum"; a condition where food passes through the bowels undigested</note>, and they die.*

### X L I V.

*Those in whom a <term>volvulus</term> <note>a twisted or obstructed bowel</note> follows a difficulty in urinating will die within seven days, unless a fever occurs and a large amount of urine flows out.*

### X L V.

*In the case of year-long ulcers, or those of even longer duration, the underlying bone must necessarily flake away, and the resulting scars will be hollow.* <note>This describes chronic infection reaching the bone (osteomyelitis), causing fragments of dead bone to shed.</note>

### X L V I.

*Whoever becomes hunchbacked due to a cough or difficult breathing before the age of puberty will die.* <note>original: "Quicumque ex tussi... gibbosi fiunt." This is likely an early clinical description of Pott’s Disease (tuberculosis of the spine), which causes spinal deformity and was often fatal in children.</note>

<meta>catchword: XLVII. He who-</meta>

<summary>This page contains Aphorisms 36 through 46 of Book VI, offering clinical observations on various conditions including cancer, jaundice, and spinal deformities. It emphasizes the "outward" movement of disease as a positive sign and cautions against the treatment of deep-seated cancers.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Book VI of the Hippocratic Aphorisms. The previous page concluded with the beginning of Aphorism XLI regarding the detection of hidden pus. This page continues with clinical observations on internal organ failure, the prognosis of chronic ulcers, and childhood respiratory conditions.</meta>

<page-num>73</page-num>
<header>BOOK VI. 73</header>

### XXXVI.

Bloodletting from the internal vein cures <term>dysuria</term> <note>difficulty or pain during urination</note>.

### XXXVII.

If a swelling appears in the neck of those seized by <term>angina</term> <note>original: "anginâ"; here referring to severe throat inflammation or quinsy</note>, it is a good sign: for the disease is being redirected outward.

### XXXVIII.

Do not attempt to treat hidden cancers: for if left alone, the patients live for a longer time than those who undergo treatment. <note>In the ancient world, invasive surgery or cauterization for deep-seated tumors often led to fatal infections or hemorrhage, leading to this conservative medical advice.</note>

### XXXIX.

Hiccups arise from the same causes as convulsions: specifically, from either excessive fullness or excessive emptying. <note>Ancient medicine taught that many ailments were caused by an imbalance of the humors, resulting in either a "repletion" (over-fullness) or "evacuation" (depletion) of bodily fluids.</note>

### XL.

If pain should afflict the <term>precordia</term> <note>the region of the diaphragm and upper abdomen</note> without any accompanying inflammation, this pain is usually driven away by the arrival of a fever.

### XLI.

If a hidden collection of <term>sanies</term> <note>a thin, foul-smelling discharge or watery pus</note> reveals no signs of its presence, this is due either to the density of the body part or the thickness of the discharge itself.

### XLII.

If the liver becomes hard in those who are suffused with bile <note>referring to jaundice, where the skin and eyes turn yellow</note>, it is a cause for great fear.

### XLIII.

Those suffering from diseases of the spleen who also experience <term>tormina</term> <note>severe, twisting abdominal griping or cramping</note> of the belly will face a poor outcome: after long-lasting diarrhea, they will develop either a "smooth bowel" <note>lientery, where food passes through undigested</note> or <term>hydrops</term> <note>dropsy or edema, the accumulation of fluid in the tissues</note>, and finally they depart from this life.

### XLIV.

Those in whom a <term>volvulus</term> <note>a painful twisting of the intestines</note> arises from <term>strangury</term> <note>a condition where urine is passed drop by drop with pain</note> will die within seven days, unless a fever occurs and they are able to pass a great deal of urine.

### XLV.

If ulcers should persist for a year or even longer, it is inevitable that the underlying bone will come away <note>sequestration or necrosis of the bone</note> and the resulting scar will be hollowed out.

### XLVI.

Whosoever becomes hunchbacked from a cough or from asthma before the age of puberty, death will overtake them before they reach those years. <note>This likely describes the effects of spinal tuberculosis, also known as Pott’s disease, where respiratory symptoms often precede the collapse of the vertebrae.</note>

<meta>catchword: XLVII. If</meta>

<summary>This page lists several grave medical prognoses, famously advising against the treatment of "hidden" cancers and describing the lethal progression of liver, spleen, and intestinal disorders. It also notes the high mortality rate of children who develop spinal deformities following chronic respiratory illness.</summary>

<page-num>74</page-num>
<header>APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES</header>

## XLVII.

For those whom bloodletting or a purging medicine benefits, it is fitting to bleed the vein in the **spring**, or to administer the purging medicine then. <note>Ancient medicine placed great importance on the seasons; spring was considered the ideal time for "cleaning" the body's humors before the heat of summer.</note>

## XLVIII.

In those with diseased spleens <note>original: "lienosis"; referring to those suffering from splenetic disorders, often associated with malaria or melancholy in the ancient world</note>, the onset of a flux of the intestines is a good sign.

## XLIX.

Those diseases of <term>gout</term> <note>original: "podagrici morbi"</note> that arise usually cease within forty days once the inflammation has subsided.

## L.

In those whose brains are deeply affected, it is necessary for a fever and bilious vomiting to follow.

## LI.

In those who are otherwise healthy, if sudden headaches occur and they immediately lose their voice and begin to snore, they perish within seven days, unless a fever seizes them. <note>This describes a stroke or apoplexy; the "snoring" refers to the heavy, labored breathing of a comatose patient. It was believed a fever might "cook" and disperse the blockage causing the fit.</note>

## LII.

But one must also observe, even during sleep, whether anything of the eyes appears from beneath the lids. For if the eyelids do not close perfectly and some of the white appears—provided it does not occur because of a purging of the bowels or from a medicinal drink—it is a bad sign and highly dangerous.

## LIII.

Those delusions that are accompanied by laughter are safer; however, those that occur with serious intent are more dangerous.

## LIV.

If, during acute diseases accompanied by fever, moaning breaths occur, they signal a bad outcome.

## LV.

Gouty pains are generally stirred up in the **spring** and the **autumn**.

## LVI.

In melancholic diseases, suspicious shifts of the humors are dangerous, for they announce either a paralysis of the body, a convulsion, mania, or blindness.

## LVII.

But <term>strokes</term> <note>original: "syderationes"; literally "blasts from the stars," referring to sudden paralysis or apoplexy thought to be caused by celestial influence</note> of the body usually happen most frequently at that age which is from forty to sixty years.

This page (75) continues the numbered aphorisms, focusing on the timing of treatments, specific symptoms of the brain and eyes, and the onset of chronic conditions like gout and apoplexy.</meta>

<page-num>75</page-num>
<header>BOOK VI.</header>

### XLVII.

*If purging or bloodletting benefits anyone,*
*It is helpful to purge them, or to open a vein, in the **spring**.* <note>Hippocratic medicine emphasized that treatments should align with the seasons; spring was the preferred time to balance the humors before the heat of summer.</note>

### XLVIII.

*Griping pains of the bowels occurring in those with diseased spleens are beneficial.* <note>original: "Tormina spleneticis"; it was believed that a flux or diarrhea helped "drain" the excess black bile associated with the spleen.</note>

### XLIX.

*Whoever is vexed by <term>gout</term> <note>original: "podagra"; specifically gout of the foot.</note> is freed from the symptoms within forty days, provided the inflammation is healed.*

### L.

*In those whose brains have been violently affected, it is necessary for a fever and vomiting of yellow bile to follow.*

### LI.

*If a sudden headache occurs in those who are otherwise healthy, and they immediately become mute and begin to snore, they pass from this body within seven days, unless a fever arises.* <note>original: "stertāntque" (snoring/stertorous breathing). This describes a massive stroke or apoplexy. Ancient doctors believed a fever might "cook" or dissolve the blockage causing the paralysis.</note>

### LII.

*One should observe whether anything of the eyes appears during the time of sleep; for if any of the white protrudes while the eyelids are not properly closed—provided it has not been caused by a purging drink or a flux of the bowels—it is a bad sign and much to be feared.* <note>This clinical observation of the eyes remaining partially open during sleep (lagophthalmos) is still recognized today as a sign of serious neurological or physical exhaustion.</note>

### LIII.

*Deliriums that occur with laughter are safer; however, those that happen with serious intent are worse.* <note>The "serious" delirium (original: "studio") refers to a focused, angry, or agitated state, which was seen as a sign of deeper brain inflammation.</note>

### LIV.

*If ever in an acute disease joined with fever, a moaning respiration occurs, it is a bad sign.*

### LV.

*Gout occurs mostly during the seasons of spring and autumn.*

### LVI.

*Let the shifting of humors be suspected in melancholic diseases; for by this, spasms, loss of sight, apoplexy, or mania are signified.* <note>The "shifting" refers to the movement of "black bile" to different parts of the body, which was thought to trigger these various neurological conditions.</note>

### LVII.

*The "thunderstruck" disease usually occurs between the ages of forty and sixty.* <note>original: "Attonitus morbus"; a historical term for apoplexy or a sudden stroke, where the patient appears as if struck by lightning.</note>

<header>APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES</header>
<sig>C 2</sig>

### L V I I I.

*When the <term>omentum</term> <note>original: "omentum"; the fatty membrane that covers the intestines. If it protruded through a wound, ancient doctors believed it could not be reinserted because it would decay.</note> has protruded, it must necessarily rot.*

### L I X.

*In those who are afflicted with long-standing pain of the hip-joints, where the top of the thigh bone falls out of the socket and then slips back in again, <term>mucous pituita</term> <note>original: "mucosa pituita"; a reference to the accumulation of phlegm or synovial fluid, which the Greeks believed caused the joint to become loose and slippery.</note> is collected there.*

### L X.

*In those who are troubled by long-lasting <term>ischiadic</term> <note>original: "ischiadico"; refers to sciatica or chronic hip pain.</note> pain, if the head of the thigh bone falls out of its socket, the thigh wastes away and they become lame, unless they are <term>burned</term>.* <note>original: "urantur"; Hippocratic medicine frequently used cauterization—applying heat or a hot iron—to treat deep-seated inflammation and "drain" the harmful humors causing the joint to slip.</note>

->THE END OF THE SIXTH BOOK.<-

***

# SEVENTH BOOK.

### I.

*In acute diseases, coldness of the extremities is a bad sign.*

### I I.

*When the flesh near a diseased bone becomes livid, it is a bad sign.*

### I I I.

*Hiccups and red eyes following bouts of vomiting are a bad sign.*

### I V.

*Shivering following a sweat is not good.*

### V.

*In cases of insanity—which the Greeks call <term>mania</term>—the onset of bowel trouble, <term>dropsy</term> <note>original: "aqua inter cutem" (water under the skin); an accumulation of fluid.</note>, or a powerful movement of the mind called <term>ecstasy</term> <note>original: "extasis"; here used in the medical sense of a sudden shift in the mental state that breaks the "mania."</note> is a favorable sign.*

### V I.

*In a long-lasting disease, an aversion to food and the passing of pure, unmixed bowel movements are bad signs.*

### V I I.

*Shivering and <term>delirium</term> following excessive drinking are bad signs.*

### V I I I.

*When an internal abscess ruptures, vomiting and fainting will occur.*

<page-num>77</page-num>
<header>BOOK VI.</header>

### L V I I I.

If at any time it should fall out, it is inevitable that the <term>omentum</term> <note>original: "omentum"; the fatty membrane covering the bowels. Hippocratic doctors believed that if this tissue protruded from a wound and was exposed to air, it would necessarily mortify and could not be saved.</note> must rot.

### L I X.

If, after there has been long-standing pain in the hip-joint, the hip falls out of the highest part of the thigh and then slips back in again, <term>mucus</term> <note>original: "mucus"; here referring to pituita or synovial fluid. The accumulation of this "cold" humor was thought to lubricate the socket too much, causing the joint to become unstable.</note> is generated within.

### L X.

In those whose <term>ischia</term> <note>original: "ischia"; the hip joints or the seat of sciatic pain.</note> have been affected by long-standing pain, if the head of the thigh bone falls out of its socket, <term>cauterization</term> <note>original: "ustio"; the application of a hot iron or caustic substance. This was a common treatment to "dry out" the excess humors causing the dislocation.</note> should be performed; otherwise, the leg will waste away and they will remain lame.

->THE END OF THE SIXTH BOOK.<-

---

# BOOK SEVENTH.

### I.

In acute diseases, he says that external coldness <note>Referring to the extremities (hands and feet) becoming cold while the patient suffers from an internal fever.</note> is a bad sign.

### I I.

Livid flesh near a diseased bone is a dangerous sign.

### I I I.

Hiccups and red eyes following vomiting are bad signs.

### I V.

A shivering fit that takes hold after a sweat is not at all good.

### V.

If madness, or <term>dropsy</term> <note>original: "hydrops"; an accumulation of fluid in the body tissues or cavities.</note>, or <term>griping of the bowels</term> <note>original: "tormina ventris"; often associated with dysentery or severe abdominal cramping.</note> should follow after <term>mania</term> <note>original: "maniae"; a state of frenzied mental derangement.</note>, they are considered good signs.

### V I.

If nausea and <term>undigested</term> <note>original: "sincera"; literally "pure," but in this medical context referring to stools that are unmixed with the proper "concocted" humors, indicating the body is not processing the illness.</note> discharges from the bowels occur during a long illness, it is a dangerous sign.

### V I I.

Shivering and delirium after much drinking are bad signs.

### V I I I.

<term>Syncope</term> <note>original: "syncope"; a sudden loss of consciousness or fainting.</note> occurring with vomiting is caused by the rupture of an internal <term>abscess</term>. <note>original: "tubere"; a swelling, tumor, or localized collection of pus within the body.</note>

<meta>This page continues the seventh book of the Aphorisms, following the introductory observations on acute diseases and external symptoms. It lists a series of "bad signs"—prognostic warnings where one symptom or condition leads to a more dangerous complication, reflecting the Hippocratic focus on predicting the course of an illness.</meta>

<page-num>78</page-num>
<header>APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES</header>

### IX.

From a great loss of blood, delirium or even a convul-<margin>sion</margin> is a bad sign. <note>original: "profluvio"; a heavy flow or hemorrhage. In the Hippocratic view, losing too much blood "cooled" the body too quickly or deprived the brain of vital spirits, leading to mental wandering or physical spasms.</note>

### X.

From a disease of the small intestine (which they call the ileum), vomiting <margin>or hiccups, or a convulsion, or delirium, is a bad sign.</margin> <note>The "ileum" here refers to what we might call a bowel obstruction or "iliac passion," which ancient doctors feared due to the high risk of gangrene and painful death.</note>

### XI.

From a disease of the s<gloss>i</gloss>de, <note>original: "morbo laterali"; likely referring to pleurisy or pain in the rib area.</note> inflammation of the lungs is a bad sign.

### XII.

From inflammation of the lungs, <term>phrenitis</term> <note>original: "phrenitis"; an acute inflammation of the mind or "brain fever," characterized by delirium and fever. Hippocrates observed that respiratory infections often coincided with mental disturbances in terminal stages.</note> foretells a bad outcome.

### XIII.

From violent burning fevers, a convulsion or a tension of the nerve<gloss>s</gloss> is a bad sign.

### XIV.

Because of a blow received to the head, stupor or <term>loss of reason</term> <note>original: "desipientia"; a state of being "out of one's senses" or foolishness caused by trauma.</note> is a bad sign.

### XV.

From the spitting of blood, the spitting of pus is a bad sign. <note>This suggests the progression from a simple hemorrhage or injury to an internal infection or abscess.</note>

### XVI.

From the spitting of pus, <term>consumption</term> <note>original: "phtisis"; a wasting disease of the lungs, typically tuberculosis.</note> and a wasting discharge follow; but when the sputum is held back, the patients die.

### XVII.

From inflammation of the liver, hiccups are a bad sign.

### XVIII.

Because of prolonged wakefulness, a convulsion or a loss of reason is a bad sign.

### XIX.

From the baring of a bone, <term>erysipelas</term> <note>original: "erysipelas"; a bacterial skin infection. In antiquity, it referred to any spreading, angry red inflammation. If it occurred over an exposed bone, it suggested the infection was reaching the deep tissues or marrow.</note> is a bad sign.

### XX.

From erysipelas, if rot or <term>suppuration</term> <note>original: "suppuratio"; the formation of pus.</note> arises beneath it, it foretells a bad outcome.

### XXI.

From a violent and visible pulsing of the arteries in ulcers, an eruption of blood is likely to occur.

### XXII.

From long-lasting pain in the parts belonging to the belly, <term>suppuration</term> <note>An internal abscess forming in the abdominal cavity.</note> occurs.

### XXIII.

From an unmixed discharge of the bowels, <term>dysentery</term> <note>original: "intestinorum difficultas"; literally "difficulty of the intestines," usually referring to the painful passing of blood and mucus.</note> follows.

<div align="right">XXIV.</div>

<summary>This page lists several medical "prognostics," where Hippocrates identifies specific secondary symptoms—such as hiccups, convulsions, or delirium—that signal a worsening condition or impending death across various illnesses like lung inflammation and liver disease.</summary>

<page-num>79</page-num>
<header>BOOK VII.</header>

### IX.
From a great loss of blood, delirium or a convulsion is a bad sign. <note>original: "A fuso... cruore"; literally "from poured-out blood." As noted previously, the loss of "vital heat" through bleeding was thought to trigger these neurological crises.</note>

### X.
In a disease of the small intestine, <note>original: "ilei morbo"; referring to *ileus*, a painful and often fatal intestinal obstruction.</note> vomiting, delirium, a convulsion, or hiccups denote a bad omen for the patient.

### XI.
Inflammation of the lungs arising from pleurisy is a bad sign. <note>original: "pleuritide"; an inflammation of the membranes surrounding the lungs. Hippocrates warns that when this spreads into the lung tissue itself—"phlegmone"—it is increasingly dangerous.</note>

### XII.
<term>Phrenitis</term> arising from inflammation of the lungs is a bad sign. <note>original: "phrenitis"; a state of acute delirium or "brain fever." Ancient doctors believed that the heat from the lungs could travel to the brain, causing mental collapse.</note>

### XIII.
From intense heat, a convulsion or <term>tetanus</term> is a bad sign. <note>original: "tetanus"; here used to describe a general rigid spanning or tension of the muscles and nerves caused by high fever.</note>

### XIV.
Because of a wound to the head, stupor or insanity is a bad sign.

### XV.
Let the coughing up of pus after the coughing up of blood be considered a bad sign. <note>This progression suggested that a physical injury or rupture in the lungs had become infected, leading to an abscess.</note>

### XVI.
From the coughing up of pus, consumption and a "flux" occur; <note>original: "tabes, & fluxio"; *tabes* refers to a wasting away of the body, often associated with tuberculosis.</note> but when the coughing stops, they pass away from the body. <note>This implies that if the discharge ceases, the patient either recovers or, more likely in this context, the disease has finished its course as the patient expires.</note>

### XVII.
Hiccups arising from inflammation of the liver is a bad sign.

### XVIII.
Delirium or a convulsion resulting from sleepless nights is a bad sign. <note>original: "vigiles... noctes"; the Latin implies a state of "watchful" or forced insomnia caused by the disease.</note>

### XIX.
<term>Saint Anthony’s Fire</term> is bad when it occurs where a bone is exposed. <note>original: "sacer ignis"; literally "holy fire," usually identifying *erysipelas*, a severe bacterial skin infection. If it reached the bone, it often meant gangrene or fatal sepsis.</note>

### XX.
If an accumulation of pus or rotting occurs from <term>Saint Anthony's Fire</term>, it signifies a bad outcome for the patient.

### XXI.
Whenever a strong throbbing is observed in an ulcer, a flow of blood usually breaks out from it.

### XXII.
<term>Sanies</term> collects after a long-lasting pain in the abdomen. <note>original: "sanies"; a thin, greenish or bloody discharge from a wound or internal infection, signaling that the body's humors have "corrupted."</note>

### XXIII.
From a discharge of pure bile in a bowel movement, <term>tormina</term> of the belly follows. <note>original: "tormina"; acute, twisting, or griping pains in the intestines, often associated with dysentery.</note>

It highlights the Hippocratic focus on clinical observation to predict a patient's decline.</summary>
