<header>APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES</header>

->X X I V.<-

*In the case of a wound to the bone, delirium follows if the wound has penetrated all the way into the cavity.* <note>original: "Ab ossis vulnere, desipientia..."; this refers to a compound fracture or a head wound where the marrow or the interior of the skull is exposed, leading to infection or brain trauma.</note>

->X X V.<-

*A convulsion resulting from the drinking of a purgative medicine is fatal.* <note>original: "medicamenti purgantis"; ancient purgatives were often harsh chemicals or herbs that could cause severe dehydration and electrolyte imbalances, leading to "convulsions" or seizures.</note>

->X X V I.<-

*Due to intense pain in the parts relating to the belly, a chilling of the extremities is a dangerous sign.*

->X X V I I.<-

*In a pregnant woman, if a frequent and empty urge to evacuate the bowels (which they call <term>tenesmus</term><note>A distressing sensation of needing to pass stool even when the bowels are empty.</note>) occurs, it causes a miscarriage.*

->X X V I I I.<-

*Whether a bone, a cartilage, or a nerve has been cut in the body, it neither grows back nor knits together.* <note>In ancient medicine, it was believed that these "bloodless" tissues lacked the "vital heat" necessary to regenerate once severed, unlike skin or muscle.</note>

->X X I X.<-

*If a person suffering from "white phlegm" (which they call <term>leucophlegmatia</term><note>A condition characterized by pale swelling or edema, often associated with a sluggish metabolism or dropsy.</note>) experiences a strong flow of the bowels, it resolves the disease.*

->X X X.<-

*In those whose bowel movements are frothy during diarrhea, the discharge comes from the head.* <note>original: "à capite fit defluxio"; this reflects the ancient "catarrh" theory, where excess humors were thought to accumulate in the brain and then "drip" down into other organs, causing various illnesses.</note>

->X X X I.<-

*In those whose urine during fevers contains sediment resembling coarse meal, it signifies that a long illness will follow.*

->X X X I I.<-

*In those whose urine shows bilious sediment, but is thin on the surface, it signifies an acute disease.*

->X X X I I I.<-

*In those whose urine is inconsistent, there is a violent disturbance in the body.*

->X X X I V.<-

*In those where bubbles persist on the surface of the urine, it signifies a disease of the kidneys, and that it will be long-lasting.*

->X X X V.<-

*But in those where the surface of the urine is oily and thick, it signifies a kidney disease, and an acute one.*

->X X X V I.<-

*In those indeed affected by a defect of the kidneys, if the aforementioned signs occur, and pains arise around the muscles of the spine, if indeed—* <note>The text breaks off mid-sentence; the catchword "dem" indicates the next page begins with "quidem."</note>

<page-num>81</page-num>
<header>BOOK VII.</header>

### XXIV.
*From a wound to the bone, if it penetrates into the cavity, frenzies follow.* <note>original: "furores"; this refers to delirium or mental agitation resulting from severe trauma or infection reaching the marrow or the interior of the skull.</note>

### XXV.
*A convulsion resulting from a drunk purgative is fatal.* <note>Ancient purgatives were often violent in their effects; a "spasm" or seizure following their use indicated terminal dehydration or nervous system failure.</note>

### XXVI.
*Whenever, due to intense pain in the parts within the belly, a coldness occurs in the extremities, it is a deadly sign.*

### XXVII.
*<term>Tenesmus</term><note>A distressing and constant urge to evacuate the bowels, often with little result.</note> occurring in a pregnant woman causes a miscarriage.*

### XXVIII.
*Whether a bone, a cartilage, or a nerve is cut in the body, it is neither accustomed to grow back nor ever to knit together.* <note>original: "coalescere"; the ancients believed these "bloodless" tissues lacked the regenerative power of skin or muscle.</note>

### XXIX.
*If a powerful flow of the belly seizes one troubled by "white phlegm," it frees him from the disease.* <note>original: "albo phlegmate"; likely referring to <term>leucophlegmatia</term>, a form of dropsy or pale swelling. A strong bowel movement was seen as the body purging the excess cold humor.</note>

### XXX.
*In those whose bowel movements appear frothy, the humor is sliding down from the brain.* <note>This reflects the Hippocratic theory of "catarrh," where excess fluids (humors) were thought to flow from the head down into the lower organs, causing various ailments.</note>

### XXXI.
*In fevers, those whose urine sediment resembles coarse flour, these are prognostic signs of a long-lasting illness.*

### XXXII.
*In those whose urine yields a sediment tinged with bile, while the top part is thin, these testify to an acute disease.*

### XXXIII.
*In those whose urine is varied and inconsistent, a great disturbance of the body occurs.*

### XXXIV.
*In those whose urine produces bubbles on the surface, a long-lasting disease of the kidneys is indicated.* <note>Modern medicine recognizes that "bubbles" or foam in the urine often indicates protein loss, a hallmark of chronic kidney disease.</note>

### XXXV.
*When fatty and dense substances appear on the surface of the urine, they testify to an acute disease of the kidneys.*

### XXXVI.
*If indeed the aforesaid signs come upon those who labor in the kidney, and they are afflicted with pain—*

# APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES

<page-num>82</page-num>

...are carried to the exterior parts, expect an <term>abscess</term><note>original: "abscessum"; the OCR is damaged here, but the context refers to a collection of pus moving toward the skin's surface.</note> to appear on the outside. But if the pains tend more toward the internal parts, one must expect that the abscess will also be internal.

->XXXVII.<-

For those who pour back blood by vomiting, if it happens without a fever, it is a sign of recovery; but if it occurs with bile, it is a bad sign. It must be treated with those things that have the power to cool and to bind. <note>original: "refrigerandi, & adstringendi"; astringent remedies were used to constrict blood vessels and stop bleeding.</note>

->XXXVIII.<-

<term>Catarrhs</term><note>original: "Destillationes"; a "dripping" of excess fluid or humors from the head into the body.</note> that flow into the upper belly reach a state of <term>suppuration</term><note>The formation or discharge of pus.</note> within twenty days.

->XXXIX.<-

If someone passes blood and clots with their urine, and suffers from <term>stranguria</term><note>A condition characterized by painful, frequent, and drop-by-drop urination.</note>, and if pain falls upon the lower belly and the <term>perineum</term><note>original: "inter foemineum"; the region between the thighs/genitals and the anus.</note>, it is the parts pertaining to the bladder that are suffering.

->XL.<-

If the tongue suddenly becomes uncontrollable, or if any part of the body becomes <term>siderated</term><note>original: "syderata"; literally "star-struck," used here to mean suddenly paralyzed or struck down, as in a stroke.</note>, it indicates an excess of black bile. <note>In the Hippocratic system, an excess of "black bile" was associated with serious nervous system collapses and melancholy.</note>

->XLI.<-

If hiccups happen to the elderly following an excessive dose of a purgative, it is not a good sign.

->XLII.<-

If a fever takes hold that did not arise from bile, the fever is resolved by pouring a copious amount of warm water over the head.

->XLIII.<-

A woman does not become ambidextrous. <note>This is a famous, though scientifically incorrect, Hippocratic observation suggesting a fundamental biological difference in how the sexes utilize their limbs.</note>

->XLIV.<-

When those suffering from internal pus are treated with cautery or the knife, if the pus that flows out is pure and white, they recover; but if it is bloody, muddy, and foul-smelling, they perish.

->XLV.<-

Those who are cauterized for a purulent liver: if the pus that flows out is pure and white, they survive, because the pus was enclosed within its own membrane. But if it flows out like <term>amurca</term><note>The dark, watery, bitter dregs of pressed olive oil. In a medical context, it describes a foul, dark, watery discharge.</note>, they perish.

->XLVI.<-

Treat pains of the eyes with a draft of pure wine, a copious warm water bath, and bloodletting.

# BOOK VII.

<page-num>83</page-num>

Where the muscles of the spine are located, if the swelling tends outward,
Believe that an <term>abscess</term><note>A collection of pus resulting from infection or "corrupted humors."</note> will occur externally;
But if it tends inward, believe that it will form from within.

->XXXVII.<-

If vomiting of blood should happen to anyone without a fever,
It is a good sign; on the contrary, it is bad if it arises with a fever.
Truly, cold and <term>astringent</term><note>original: "astringentia"; substances that contract body tissues to stop the flow of blood or fluids.</note> remedies will heal it.

->XXXVIII.<-

Whatever <term>catarrh</term><note>original: "destillatio"; the "dripping" of excess fluid from the head into the lower cavities of the body.</note> occurs in the upper belly,
Transitions into pus within the space of twenty days.

->XXXIX.<-

Whenever someone urinates clots and blood,
And passes their urine only drop by drop,
While the <term>perineum</term><note>original: "interfamineo"; the region between the thighs/genitals and the anus.</note> and the lower belly are in pain,
The parts located near the bladder are suffering in these cases.

->XL.<-

For those whose tongue begins to falter, or if a part of the body suddenly becomes
<term>paralyzed</term><note>original: "syderata"; literally "star-struck," referring to a sudden stroke or palsy once attributed to the influence of the stars.</note>,
It signals the presence of <term>black bile</term><note>One of the four humors; an excess was thought to cause neurological issues or "melancholy."</note> in them.

->XLI.<-

In an old man, hiccups are a bad sign when they arise from a <term>purgative</term><note>A medicine used to clear the bowels or induce vomiting.</note>.

->XLII.<-

If anyone has a fever that does not arise from <term>yellow bile</term><note>original: "flava bile"; the humor associated with heat and a choleric temperament.</note>,
Much warm water poured over the head will cure them.

->XLIII.<-

It never happens that any woman is ambidextrous. <note>A common, though incorrect, belief in Hippocratic medicine regarding the biological "heat" and symmetry of the female body.</note>

->XLIV.<-

Whenever the chest is cut or cauterized for <term>empyema</term><note>original: "empyis"; a collection of pus between the lung and the chest wall.</note>,
If the pus flows white and pure, it signifies recovery; on the contrary,
It is bad if the fluid is blood-stained, muddy, and foul-smelling.

->XLV.<-

If the liver is suppurated and a <term>cautery</term><note>original: "ustio"; the practice of burning tissue to treat a wound or infection.</note> is applied,
And the pus flows white and pure, the disease will be cured,
For in those cases, the pus is contained within the membrane of the liver;
However, those from whom the discharge is like <term>oil dregs</term><note>original: "amurca"; the dark, bitter sediment of olive oil, used here to describe thin, dark, unhealthy pus.</note> will perish.

->XLVI.<-

After the drinking of wine and a bath of warm water,
It is proper to treat pain in the eyes by <term>phlebotomy</term><note>The surgical opening of a vein to withdraw blood, a standard treatment to reduce "heat" or "pressure."</note>.

### XLVII.

*For a person suffering from <term>dropsy</term> <note>original: "hydropico"; a condition of excessive fluid under the skin or in a body cavity.</note>, there is no hope of recovery if they also suffer from a cough.*

### XLVIII.

*Difficulty in urinating, as well as the constant dripping of urine, is cured by <term>thorexis</term> <note>original: "thorexis"; an ancient medical term likely referring to drinking unmixed wine to the point of warming the chest or "fortifying" the internal organs.</note> and the cutting of the internal vein.*

### XLIX.

*If a swelling appears in the neck of those seized by <term>angina</term> <note>original: "anginâ"; here referring to a severe, suffocating throat inflammation or quinsy.</note>, it is a favorable sign; for the disease is being turned outward.*

### L.

*Those whose brain has been seized by <term>syderation</term> <note>original: "syderatio"; a term for apoplexy or a sudden stroke, thought by the ancients to be caused by the influence of the stars.</note> are cured if they can survive past the third day.*

### LI.

*Sneezing, whatever the cause in the head, usually arises from the brain being excessively overheated, or it happens when the void within the head is over-moistened. For the air that was trapped inside is poured out and makes a noise, because it is not granted a wide path for exit.*

### LII.

*An oncoming fever dissolves intense pains of the <term>liver</term> <note>original: "hepatis".</note>.*

### LIII.

*For those to whom the cutting of a vein <note>bloodletting</note> is beneficial, it ought to be cut in the spring.*

### LIV.

*For those who have <term>phlegm</term> <note>one of the four bodily humors, associated with cold and moisture.</note> between the stomach and the enclosed <term>diaphragm</term> <note>original: "diaphragmáque".</note> which causes pain, and it can move into neither the upper nor lower belly: if that phlegm is diverted into the bladder through the veins, a perfect resolution of that disease occurs.*

### LV.

*If the liver of any person swells with water and breaks into the <term>omentum</term> <note>original: "omentum"; the fatty lining of the abdominal cavity.</note>, their belly becomes distended with water and they die.*

### LVI.

*Anxiety, <term>yawning</term> <note>original: "oscedo".</note>, and shivering cease from the drinking of wine mixed with an equal portion of water.*

### LVII.

*If a <term>fistula</term> <note>an abnormal tube-like passage in the body.</note> of the urine is closed by a swelling...*

Difficult urination, as well as the constant dripping of urine, is cured by <term>thorexis</term> <note>original: "thorexis"; a Greek medical term referring to drinking wine until one is warmed or slightly tipsy, used here as a diuretic treatment.</note> and the cutting of an internal vein.

### XLIX.

If a swelling arises in the neck of those seized by <term>angina</term> <note>original: "angina"; in ancient medicine, this referred to a severe inflammation of the throat or "quinsy" that threatened suffocation.</note>, it is a good sign, for it means the disease is being turned outward.

### LI.

Whenever sneezing occurs due to a condition of the head, it usually arises because the brain has been excessively overheated, or because the void within the head has become over-moistened. For the air that was trapped inside is poured out and makes a noise, because it is not granted a wide exit.

### LII.

A fever that arises dissolves great pains of the <term>liver</term> <note>original: "hepatis".</note>.

### LIII.

### LIV.

For those who have <term>phlegm</term> <note>original: "phlegma"; one of the four vital humors, considered cold and moist.</note> trapped between the stomach and the diaphragm, which causes torment and can pass into neither of the <term>cavities</term> <note>original: "ventrem"; referring to the upper or lower abdominal regions.</note>, a perfect resolution of the disease occurs when that phlegm is diverted through the veins into the bladder.

### L V I I I.

> Those whose brain has been violently shaken by some accident must necessarily become mute at once.

### L I X.

> For bodies endowed with moist flesh, it is appropriate to induce hunger; for hunger indeed dries out the body.

### L X.

> And when changes occur throughout the whole body—such as if the body becomes cold and then hot again, or if one color arises after another—it signifies a long duration of the disease.

### L X I.

> Constant sweating, whether hot or cold, demonstrates that moisture must be evacuated: from the upper parts in a strong person, but from the lower parts in a weak person.

### L X I I.

> Fevers that afflict every third day <note>original: "tertio quoque die"; these are likely "tertian fevers," often associated with malaria in the ancient world.</note> and do not intermit are more dangerous. However, if they intermit in any way, it signifies the patient is out of danger.

### L X I I I.

> Those who suffer from long fevers will develop <term>swellings</term> <note>original: "tubercula"; small tumors or nodes.</note> or pains in the joints.

### L X I V.

> When swellings or joint pains arise after long fevers, it is because those individuals are consuming too much food.

### L X V.

> If one gives a feverish person the same food offered to a healthy one, it provides strength to the healthy but fuels the disease in the sick.

### L X V I.

> One ought to examine the things passing through the <term>bladder</term> <note>original: "vesicam"; referring here to the urine.</note> to see if they are like those of a person in good health. For those that are least similar are the more unhealthy; whereas those more similar to healthy urine are the least unhealthy.

### L X I I I.

> In those whose <term>stools</term> <note>original: "hypochoremata"; a term for excrement or bowel discharge.</note>, if allowed to settle and remain undisturbed, leave a sediment like <term>scrapings</term> <note>original: "strigmenta"; often interpreted as fatty or mucosal shreds in the stool.</note>: if these are few, the disease is minor; if many, it is great. In such cases, it is beneficial to purge the bowels from below. Otherwise, if the bowels are not purged

->L V I I I .<-

Those who have suffered a violent concussion of the brain must necessarily lose their voice immediately.

->L I X .<-

For those whose bodies are endowed with <term>moist flesh</term> <note>original: "humidiore... carne"; in Galenic medicine, this refers to a constitution with an excess of the watery humor.</note>, fasting is appropriate, since the body is dried out by it.

->L X .<-

When a change occurs throughout the entire body—such as if the body grows cold and then hot again, or if the skin color changes—it is a portent of a long-lasting illness.

->L I X .<-

Constant sweating flowing from the body, whether it be cold or hot, indicates an excess of moisture: in a strong person, this suggests the moisture should be purged from above <note>original: "purganda supernè"; likely referring to induced vomiting or expectoration.</note>; in a weak person, however, it should be purged from below.

->L X I I .<-

A <term>tertian fever</term> <note>original: "terna febris"; a fever that peaks every third day, common in malarial infections.</note>, if it continues without interruption, is not without danger; but if it intermits, it is judged that the danger has passed.

->L X I I I .<-

If fevers afflict anyone for a long period of time, pains or <term>swellings</term> <note>original: "tubercula"; small nodes or growths.</note> will arise around the joints.

->L X I V .<-

When joint pains or swellings arise from a long-lasting fever, you may believe that those individuals are eating too much food.

->L X V .<-

If one gives the same diet to a feverish patient that is given to a healthy person, it provides strength to the healthy man, but fuels the disease in the sick one.

->L X I I I .<-

One must examine the things passed through the bladder <note>original: "vesicam"; referring to urine.</note> to see if they are like those normally excreted by a healthy person; for the further they differ from a healthy state, the more diseased they are; conversely, they are less dangerous if they resemble those of the healthy.

->L X V I I .<-

And for those whose <term>feces</term> <note>original: "alvinæ fæces"</note>, while at rest, settle like <term>scrapings</term> <note>original: "strigmenta"; refers to shreds of mucus or tissue in the stool, often associated with irritation of the bowels.</note>—if there are few, the disease is slight, but if there are many, it is great—it is appropriate for the bowels to be purged from below:

<meta>catchword: LXXIX. When-</meta>

*...the more gruels <note>original: "sorbitiones"; medicinal broths or liquid foods often given to the sick.</note> you provide, and the more of them you give, the more you will cause harm.*

### L X V I I I.

*Undigested matter that passes downwards originates from <term>black bile</term> <note>In ancient medicine, one of the four essential bodily humors; an excess was believed to cause various severe illnesses.</note>: if the amount is large, the disease is greater; if it is small, the disease is less severe.*

### L X I X.

*In continuous fevers, excretions that are leaden-colored, bloody, foul-smelling, and bilious are all bad; however, if they are discharged and lead to a good result, they are favorable, whether they come through the bowels or the urine. But if any of these are excreted without providing relief, it is a bad sign.*

### L X X.

*When one wishes to make the body responsive to treatment, it is necessary to make it fluid. If you wish to make it fluid for purging upwards <note>Referring to the use of emetics to induce vomiting.</note>, the bowels must be bound; but if you wish to purge downwards, they must be moistened.*

### L X I X.

*Sleep and wakefulness, if either exceeds the proper measure, are a bad sign.*

### L X X I I.

*In continuous fevers, if the external parts of the body are cold while the internal parts burn and are tormented by thirst, it is fatal.*

### L X X I I I.

*In a continuous fever, if the lip, or the eyebrow, or the eye, or the nose is distorted, or if the patient cannot see or hear while the body is already in a weak state—whichever of these occurs, death is near.*

### L X X I V.

*<term>Dropsy</term> <note>original: "aqua inter cutem"; literally "water between the skin," referring to the accumulation of fluid in the body tissues.</note> follows from white phlegm.*

### L X X V.

*<term>Dysentery</term> <note>original: "intestinorum difficultas"; literally "difficulty of the intestines."</note> arises from a purging of the bowels.*

### L X X V I.

*<term>Lientery</term> <note>original: "lævitas intestinorum"; literally "smoothness of the intestines," a condition where food passes through the body rapidly and undigested.</note> follows upon dysentery.*

### L X X V I I.

*An abscess of the bone results from <term>sphacelus</term> <note>original: "sphacelismo"; the mortification or gangrene of a tissue, here specifically involving the bone.</note>.*

### L X X V I I I.

*From the vomiting of blood comes <term>consumption</term> <note>original: "tabes"; a general term for the wasting away of the body, often specifically referring to pulmonary tuberculosis.</note> and the expulsion of pus from the upper parts.*

<header>BOOK VII.</header>

But if, when the body has not been purged at all, a medicinal broth <note>original: "sorbitio"; a thin gruel or liquid food given to the sick.</note> is given, the more that is provided, the more it will harm the patient.

### ->L X V I I I.<-

Undigested matters that pass downwards are caused by <term>black bile</term> <note>One of the four humors in ancient medicine, associated with the Earth element; an excess was thought to cause melancholy and serious physical illness.</note>: if they are few, the disease is minor; if they are many, it is greater.

### ->L X I X.<-

If at any time during constant fevers the patient should excrete that which is leaden-colored, foul-smelling, or tinged with bile or blood, it is entirely an evil sign. Conversely, it is a good sign if these are discharged appropriately <note>That is, as a "crisis" where the body successfully expels the disease-causing matter.</note>, whether it occurs through the urine <note>original: "lotia"</note> or through the bowels; however, if they pass in this way without bringing relief to the patient, it is a bad omen.

### ->L X X.<-

In order to purge the body, you must first make it fluid <note>original: "fluxile"; making the humors "flow" so they can be easily expelled.</note>; if you seek to make it fluid for purging from above <note>meaning via the mouth, using emetics to cause vomiting.</note>, you shall bind the bowels; but if you seek to purge from below, you shall loosen them.

### ->L X X I.<-

Both excessive sleep and excessive wakefulness are harmful.

### ->L X X I I.<-

In a continuous fever, if the external parts of the body grow cold while the internal parts burn and suffer from thirst, it is fatal.

### ->L X X I I I.<-

In a continuous fever, if the eyes, the lip, the eyebrow, or the nose of the patient becomes distorted, or if they cannot see or hear properly—whichever of these occurs—their strength is now broken and death is imminent.

### ->L X X I V.<-

<term>Dropsy</term> <note>original: "Hydrops"; a swelling caused by excess fluid in the body's tissues or cavities, now known as edema.</note> usually follows "white phlegm" <note>original: "album phlegma"; likely referring to leucophlegmasia, a pale swelling of the skin.</note>.

### ->L X X V.<-

Griping pains <note>original: "tormina"; severe abdominal cramping or colic.</note> of the belly follow after bouts of diarrhea.

### ->L X X V I.<-

A "smooth" bowel <note>original: "lævior alvus"; likely referring to lientery, a condition where food passes through the body undigested and smooth.</note> follows after griping pains of the belly.

### ->L X X V I I.<-

From <term>sphacelus</term> <note>original: "sphacelismo"; here referring to the necrosis or gangrene of a bone.</note> comes a collection of pus within the bone.

### ->L X X V I I I.<-

From the vomiting of blood comes <term>consumption</term> <note>original: "tabes"; a gradual wasting away of the body, often associated with pulmonary tuberculosis.</note> and the spitting of pus.

<meta>catchword: LXXIX. When-</meta>

<summary>This page details various prognostic signs in acute illness, explaining how the appearance of excretions and the physical distortion of facial features can predict the outcome of a fever. It also describes the progression of certain conditions, such as how diarrhea can lead to colic and eventually wasting diseases.</summary>
<keywords>prognosis, black bile, fevers, purging, dropsy, colic, consumption, Hippocratic medicine</keywords>

--- Page 93 ---
<meta>This page concludes the translation of Book VII of the Aphorisms of Hippocrates. The previous page discussed the dangers of feeding unpurged bodies and the significance of various excretions and sleep patterns. This final page provides a closing rule for judging the severity of a disease based on the body's deviations from its natural state.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>
<header>APHORISMS OF HIPPOCRATES, 79.</header>

One must observe the quality of those things that pass through the urine and the <term>bowels</term> <note>original: "alvum"; referring to the intestinal tract and solid waste.</note>, as well as those that are lost through the <term>flesh</term> <note>original: "carnes"; in this context, it refers to the wasting away of the body's tissues, or possibly excessive sweating.</note>, and any other way in which the body departs from its natural state. For if the deviation is slight, the disease is minor; if it is great, the disease is major; and if it is excessively great, then such a condition is <term>deadly</term> <note>original: "perniciosum"; meaning fatal or destructive to life.</note>.

->END OF THE SEVENTH BOOK,<-
->AND THE LAST.<-

Below the bar is a lush arrangement of fruits and leaves, including what appears to be a bunch of grapes and a pomegranate, tapering down to a single point.</image-desc>

<meta>catchword: IN-</meta>

<summary>This final aphorism of Book VII provides a general rule for prognosis, stating that the severity of an illness is directly proportional to how much the body's waste and physical condition deviate from their natural, healthy state.</summary>
<keywords>Hippocrates, Aphorisms, Book VII, prognosis, urine, bowels, physical wasting, severity of disease, medical observation</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the translation of the Aphorisms of Hippocrates, Book VII. This final aphorism serves as a concluding principle for medical prognosis.</meta>

<page-num>91</page-num>
<header>BOOK VII.</header>

### 79.

One must carefully examine the quality of what passes through the <term>bowels</term> <note>Latin: *ventrem*; literally "the belly," referring to digestive waste.</note>, the <term>urine</term> <note>Latin: *lotia*.</note>, and the <term>flesh</term> <note>Latin: *carnes*; in this context, it refers to the wasting away or shedding of body tissues.</note>, or whether the body <term>fails</term> <note>Latin: *peccet*; used here to describe a "sin" or deviation from the natural, healthy state of the body.</note> in any other way. For a disease is small if the failure is slight, but great if the failure is significant; and if it is very great, the condition is <term>deadly</term> <note>Latin: *perniciosus*.</note>.

---

->END OF THE SEVENTH BOOK,<-
->AND THE LAST.<-

<image-desc>An ornate, symmetrical woodcut tailpiece featuring dense scrolling foliage and floral motifs, arranged in a triangular composition that tapers to a decorative flourish at the bottom.</image-desc>

<meta>catchword: IN-</meta>

<summary>This final aphorism of the collection provides a universal rule for prognosis: the severity of a disease is judged by how far the body's functions and excretions deviate from their natural, healthy state.</summary>
<keywords>Hippocrates, Book VII, prognosis, bowels, urine, physical wasting, severity of illness, medical observation</keywords>

# INDEX
## A MOST ACCURATE [INDEX],

->For finding all and each of Hippocrates’<-
->Aphorisms very easily and quickly, according to<-
->the variety of the matter which they treat.<-

*Since the actions of a <term>Physician</term> <note>Latin: *Medici*.</note>, which otherwise seem infinite, are directed toward two primary goals—namely, the preservation of things <term>according to nature</term> <note>Latin: *secundùm naturam*; the state of health or natural function.</note> in his subject, and the removal of things <term>contrary to nature</term> <note>Latin: *præter naturam*; disease, injury, or imbalance.</note> from the same—for the sake of greater clarity and distinction, we present separately those aphorisms directing both these and those actions of the Physician. However, before the <term>rational Physician</term> <note>Latin: *rationalis Medicus*; a practitioner who uses logic and theory to understand the causes of disease.</note> attempts to apply aids to achieve the desired end and proceed to the treatment itself, he first inquires through <term>signs</term> <note>Latin: *signa*; symptoms or indicators used for diagnosis and prognosis.</note> whether the affliction rejects or receives the treatment. The following Aphorisms, arranged therefore in the first place, provide information on these matters.*

<note>The vocabulary below highlights the key concepts used in the preface to this index.</note>
<gloss>Aphorisms of Hippocrates</gloss>, <gloss>Actions of the Physician</gloss>, <gloss>nature</gloss>, <gloss>rational Physician</gloss>

<summary>This page introduces a thematic index for the Aphorisms, explaining that the collection is organized by the physician's goals: maintaining health and removing disease. It emphasizes that a physician must first look for diagnostic signs before beginning any treatment.</summary>

<keywords>index, Hippocrates, medical theory, nature vs. disease, rational medicine, diagnostic signs, clinical practice</keywords>

## CLASS VI.

->*Containing Aphorisms regarding the diseases of individual parts, from the crown of the head to the soles of the feet.* <note>original: "à vertice ad plantas pedis"; this "head-to-toe" arrangement was the standard organizational method for medical textbooks from antiquity through the early modern period.</note><-

### On lethargy, coma, and catalepsy, etc. <note>original: "De lethargo, cataphorâ & catoche"; these terms describe various states of abnormal sleep or unconsciousness. "Cataphora" refers to a heavy, coma-like sleep, while "catoche" (or catalepsy) describes a state where the patient is conscious but unable to move.</note>

<note>original: "De morbis pudendi." In early modern medicine, this term referred to the external reproductive organs of both men and women.</note>

->On ailments of the uterus.<-
<note>original: "De affectibus uteri." Hippocratic medicine placed significant emphasis on the "womb" as the source of many physical and emotional conditions in women.</note>

->On menstruation.<-
<note>original: "De menstruis." The regular cycle of menstruation was seen as a crucial purging of excess humors, and its suppression was thought to cause various illnesses.</note>

->On conception.<-
<note>original: "De conceptu." These aphorisms typically discuss the signs that a woman has conceived and the factors that influence fertility.</note>

->On miscarriage.<-
<note>original: "De abortu." In this context, the term encompasses both spontaneous miscarriage and the medical dangers associated with a lost pregnancy.</note>

->On the fetus and childbirth.<-
<note>original: "De foetu & partu." This section indexes observations on the development of the child in the womb and the physical process of delivery.</note>

->On diseases of the joints.<-
<note>original: "De morbis articulorum." In the ancient world, "joint disease" primarily referred to conditions like arthritis and gout (podagra), often attributed to an accumulation of cold humors or lifestyle excesses.</note>

### CATEGORY VII.
->**Containing Aphorisms regarding diseases**<-
->**which are called external.**<-
<note>original: "morbos qui externi dicuntur." This section focuses on visible conditions of the skin, hair, and outward physical injuries, as opposed to internal "hidden" diseases.</note>

**On conditions of the**
**hair.**
<note>original: "De capillorum affectibus."</note>
| Book | Aph. |
|:---:|:---:|
| 5 | 11 |
| 6 | 28 |
| 6 | 34 |

**On pustules.**
<note>original: "De puſtulis." These are small, inflammatory skin eruptions or pimples.</note>
| Book | Aph. |
|:---:|:---:|
| 3 | 20 |
| 6 | 9 |

**On tumors contrary**
**to**
**nature.**
<note>original: "De tumoribus præter naturam." In the Hippocratic tradition, a "tumor" referred to any abnormal swelling or growth, not necessarily a malignancy.</note>

**On Erysipelas.**
<note>original: "De Eryſipelate." An acute skin infection, often known historically as "St. Anthony's Fire," characterized by bright red, swollen patches.</note>

**On necrosis and**
**gangrene.**
<note>original: "De ſphacelo, & gangræna." Sphacelus refers specifically to the death or sloughing of deep tissues or bone.</note>
| Book | Aph. |
|:---:|:---:|

| 7 | 77 |

**On cancer.**
<note>original: "De cancro." Ancient physicians used this term for malignant or persistent, crab-like ulcers that were difficult to cure.</note>
| Book | Aph. |
|:---:|:---:|
| 6 | 38 |

**On herpes.**
<note>original: "De herpete." In this era, "herpes" described various spreading skin rashes or "creeping" eruptions, not just the modern viral infection.</note>
| Book | Aph. |
|:---:|:---:|
| 5 | 22 |

**On scrofula.**
<note>original: "De ſtrumis." This refers to chronic swellings of the lymph nodes, usually in the neck, typically associated with tuberculosis.</note>
| Book | Aph. |
|:---:|:---:|
| 3 | 26 |

**On buboes.**
<note>original: "De bubonibus." Swollen lymph nodes in the groin or armpit, often associated with fever or plague.</note>
| Book | Aph. |
|:---:|:---:|
| 4 | 55 |

**On tubercles.**
<note>original: "De tuberculis." Small, hard nodules or swellings on or under the skin.</note>

<summary>This page concludes a thematic index of Hippocratic aphorisms, specifically listing citations for external ailments including skin diseases, tumors, wounds, and fractures. It organizes these medical observations into "Category VII" for easy reference by early modern medical practitioners.</summary>
